PREFACE TO 1951 SUPPLEMENT 

Ten years have passed since the Second and Revised Edition of this work went to press. Since that time the legislative mill has been grinding steadily, revising and greatly changing the law relating to real estate and conveyancing. Some of the more important changes are as follows: The Intestate Law of 1917 has been rewritten and replaced by the new Intestate Act of 1947; so, also, the Wills Act of 1917 has been replaced by the Wills Act of 1947; a new Fiduciaries Act of 1949, effective January 

1, 1950, has worked a radical change in the law by abrogating the lien of decedents' debts not of record, shortening the period of liens of record, and conferring on administrators and executors power to administer and sell real estate; a new revision of the lien of judgments, known as the Judgment Lien Law of 1947 has been enacted; the Municipal Tax Lien Law of 1923 also has been amended and supplemented by the Real Estate Tax Sale Law of 1947; a constitutional Deficiency Judgment Act has been enacted, as has been a new Notary Public Law, a uniform law relating to Acknowledgments; and the 

Uniform Slayers Act has been adopted. Several laws enlarging married women's property rights and governing estates by entireties are among the new statutes. 

All these and more, together with the many important court decisions, make a Supplement to the 1941 Edition necessary.

Because most of the laws mentioned are wholly or in part prospective in operation, lawyers, conveyancers and searchers of title must for a considerable period of time to come, be familiar with the old law as it stood before the changes. Consequently they must continue to have convenient access to the old law as well as the new. For this reason little in the original text of the 1941 Edition iii

PREFACE TO SUPPLEMENT. 

could be safely omitted and much remains the same. 

To reprint the entire work, with the additions that the changes require, would, under the present mounting costs of publication, have placed an unnecessarily burdensome expense on the bar and those having the present edition. 

Therefore, the author deemed it wiser, as well as more economical, to let the Edition of 1941 stand and publish this Supplement, bringing citations down to date and setting forth the changes made by the new Acts. The 

Supplement is arranged in the order of the Sections, with the same headings, as well as the page numbers, of the original text. 

In consulting this Supplement, therefore, the user should first examine the law as expounded in the original work. Then turn to the Supplement which will set forth the changes in the law, decisional as well as statutory. 

This, it is believed, will furnish a rapid and convenient way to find the applicable law to date. Some new matter has been added which is identified by the word "New" in bold face type. Corrections also have been noted of errors in the original text. 

The author acknowledges the assistance of Lawrence 

R. Zerfing, Esq., Vice President and Title Officer of the 

Land Title Bank & Trust Company, in the revisions noted in the chapter on Searches. He wishes also to acknowledge the courtesy of M. Paul Smith, Esq., in permitting him to use the diagram included herein of the New Intestate Act of 1947.

It is the hope of the author that the profession will find this Supplement as useful as the original work. 

GROVER C. LADNER 

January 1951 iv

PREFACE TO 1956 SUPPLEMENT 

During the five years since publication of the 1951 Supplement, the laws relating to conveyancing have been changed and added to here and there by legislation, and have been further developed or clarified by court decisions. While some of the legislation and decisions have a relatively minor effect upon those laws, others are of sufficient importance to merit attention. These relegate the 1951 Supplement to a status somewhat like that of a five year old automobile. It still runs, but not too dependably, and its owner loses increasingly more time in keeping it currently functional.

Specifically, statutory changes of more than cursory interest have resulted from the Incompetents' Estates Act of June 28, 1951, which eliminates the earlier separate

Acts governing undue influence, mental incompetents, habitual drunkards, persons of unsound mind, weakness of mind and senility, and affects the laws relating to conveyance of real estate in such cases. State and local real estate transfer taxes have been imposed since publication of the 1951 Supplement; a new Notary Public Law was enacted in 1953, making numerous changes in the prior law; the Uniform Commercial Code of 1953 radically changed the law relating to lien priorities on fixtures, especially in respect to "plant" mortgages; changes have been made in the procedure for revival of judgments and other liens in some instances; and the 1952 amendments to the Real Estate Brokers License Act have made a number of revisions in and additions to qualifications, penal provisions and sharing of commissions with nonlicensees.

The tremendous spread of housing developments has resulted in thousands of home purchases under forms of agreement of sale containing many provisions virtually unknown to the older conventional forms. These and the settlements upon them are now so common that consider-

PREFACE TO SUPPLEMENT. 

ation of at least their salient features can no longer be ignored; and what is hoped will be deemed adequate attention to them has been added to the supplement. Brief discussions of Federal Savings and Loan Association mortgages and "open-end" mortgages have also been included, in view of their increasing frequency and importance.

All these and other, perhaps less far-reaching, changes and developments indicate the need for a revised and up-to-date cumulative supplement to the book which is so authoritative in its field. Unfortunately, preparation of this supplement by former Justice Ladner, with whom, during his last several years, the editor had the privilege of close association as both partner and friend, was prevented by his untimely passing. Notes made by him, however, in anticipation of such preparation, have been used freely by the editor, and many of them have been incorporated verbatim in this supplement. Every effort has been made to continue throughout, the same pithy and expressive style that was his. Any deviation from it may be laid to human frailty. 

The editor is most thankful to Mrs. J. J. Carter, Justice 

Ladner's daughter, for making his own notes and memoranda available. Without them the preparation of this supplement would have been much more difficult. The editor gratefully acknowledges the courtesy of M. Paul 

Smith, Esq., co-editor of Fiduciary Review, in permitting his excellent chart and explanation of descent under the new laws governing decedents' estates to be reprinted, with appropriate revisions required by the Fiduciaries Act of 1949, in this supplement. The assistance of 

Thomas F. Meehan, Jr., Esq., of the Philadelphia Bar, in presenting the effects of the Internal Revenue Code of 1954 on federal tax liens, is greatly appreciated. 

It is hoped that the new supplement will be found as useful today as was the former supplement five years ago. 

P. NICHOLSON WOOD 

January 31, 1956 vi

II. 

ESTATES IN LAND. 

§ 2. Definition of Kinds of Estates. 

PAGE 4 

An estate in fee tail was created by a conveyance or devise to an individual and "the heirs of his body." It was designed to retain the property in the family line through successive generations. The Act of April 27, 1855, P.L. 368, § 1, 68 P.S. 124, in effect prohibited the creation of estates in fee tail in Pennsylvania, and they are now virtually extinct. 1

§ 3(a)  

§ 3. Freehold Estates. 

New add to § 3 (a): The term "absolute," however, must be qualified. While in general the owner may use and dispose of his property as he pleases, his right to do so is subject to two exceptions: (1) the paramount right of the

Commonwealth and its subdivisions to levy taxes upon the property, to regulate its use under the police power, and to take it for public use under the power of eminent domain; and (2) the owner must use his property with due regard for the rights of others. Thus, his use of the property must not constitute a nuisance (I Tiffany, Modern Law of Real Property, p. 52), as, for example, excessive noise or pollution of air or water. But he may build on his land a structure which obstructs light, air and view of an adjoining owner even though it serves no useful purpose and is erected for the sole purpose of annoying the adjoining owner (Cohen v. Perrino, 355 Pa. 

455-1947; Maioriello v. Arlotta, 364 Pa. 557—1950).

The familiar principle that every land owner in fee simple is entitled to the ownership above the surface of the land to the heavens as well as extending indefinitely below the surface, is now limited by § 401 of the Aeronautical Code of May 25, 1933, P.L. 1001, 2 P.S. 1467, which provides that "ownership of the space over and above the lands and waters of this Commonwealth is declared to be vested in the owner of the surface beneath, but such ownership extends only so far as is necessary to the enjoyment of the use of the surface without interference, and is subject to the right of passage or flight of aircraft. 

Flight, through the space over and above *** at a sufficient height, and without interference to the enjoyment and use of the land or water beneath, is not an actionable wrong, unless said flight results in actual damage to the land or water, or property thereon or therein, or use of the land or water beneath." (See Dlugas v. United Air 

Lines T. Co., 53 D. & C. 402-1944; and Crew v. Gallagher,

§ 3(b)

358 Pa. 541-1948; Reynolds v. Wilson, 67 D. & C. 2861949).

In this connection see also the Airport Zoning Act of 

April 17, 1945, P.L. 237, 2 P.S. 1550 et seq., by which political sub-divisions of the Commonwealth may adopt, administer and enforce under the police power, airport zoning regulations for the airport hazard area. Such zoning regulations must be reasonable and are made by the Act subject to judicial review. The serious question as to the extent to which such regulations may constitutionally be adopted where they amount to imposing a restrictive easement on private property in the area is taken care of by § 14 (2 P.S. 1563) which provides for eminent domain proceedings and payment of compensation. See interesting address of Norman R. Bradley, Esq., of the

Philadelphia Bar, to the Philadelphia Title Group, pub lished in Legal Intelligencer of April 15, 1948, and the case of United States v. Causby, 328 U.S. 256—1946. 

Awards by the Board of View are now indexed by the Prothonotary of Philadelphia County in the Miscellaneous index.

Add to § 3 (b). An estate for the life of another is called an estate per autre vie.

A life tenant may convey or lease his interest (Borie v. 

Crissman, 82 Pa. 125-1876); but he may not disregard the rights of those who take when his life estate ends. So, he is responsible for ordinary repairs and maintenance (Farber's Estate, 70 Pa. Super. Ct. 81, 86-1918); but improvements of a permanent nature, without the acquiescence of the remainderman, are at his own expense even though the property is thereby made more valuable (Appeal of Datesman, 127 Pa. 348-1889; Farber's Estate, 70 Pa. Super. Ct. 81–86—1918). The life tenant must pay the taxes (Spangler v. York County, 13 Pa. 322, 3271850; McDannel v. Weddige, 79 Pa. Super. Ct. 494–1922), municipal assessments for sidewalk paving (Meanor v. 

Goldsmith, 216 Pa. 489-1907; Hitner v. Ege, 23 Pa. 305-

§ 3(a)(1) 

1854), and mortgage interest accruing during his ownership (Shaffer's Estate, 262 Pa. 15—1918). If the timber on the land was treated as a source of profit by the owner of the fee during his own lifetime, the subsequent life tenant may continue to cut and remove it (Ferguson v. 

Rafferty, 128 Pa. 337-1889; Willard v. Willard, 56 Pa. 

119-1867). He may also cut and remove timber to the extent reasonably proper to clear the land (Lynn's Appeal, 31 Pa. 44—1857; Morris v. Knight, 14 Pa. Super. Ct. 

324-1900), or for other purposes required for reasonable care of the property (Sayers v. Hoskinson, 110 Pa. 4731885; Lynn's Appeal, 31 Pa. 44-1857; Neel v. Neel, 19 

Pa. 323, 329-1852; McCullough v. Irvine's Extrs., 13 

Pa. 438, 442-1850; Morris v. Knight, 14 Pa. Super. Ct. 

324-1900). So, also, a life tenant may operate oil and gas wells, mines and quarries, if they were opened before his life estate began (Crozer's Estate, 336 Pa. 266—1939; 

Knox's Estate (No. 1), 328 Pa. 177–1937; Woodburn's 

Estate, 138 Pa. 606-1891; Sayers v. Hoskinson, 110 Pa. 

473-1885; Irwin v. Covode, 24 Pa. 162-1854; Neel v. 

Neel, 19 Pa. 323—1852); but he has no right to open new mines or wells (Marshall v. Mellon, 179 Pa. 371–1897; 

Appeal of Westmoreland Coal Co., 85 Pa. 344—1877).

§ 4. Estates Less Than Freehold. 

New add to $4 (b).

The Landlord and Tenant Act of 1951 

(Act of April 6, 1951, P.L. 69, art. V, § 501; 68 P.S. 250.- 

501) carries over the provisions of the Act of March 31, 1905 to the effect that under a lease "for an indeterminate time," the lessor may bring his action for possession after thirty days' written notice to quit has been served; so that a tenancy at will is still, for all practical purposes, a month to month tenancy.

PAGE 7 

New add to § 4 (c). An estate at sufferance is possibly the lowest grade of an estate in real property, and an occupant of such an estate is distinguished from a mere trespasser or squatter only by the fact that he entered originally by permission of the owner. It has been held that a tenant at sufferance acquires, as against the owner, at least, no rights and no term, but only a naked possession due to the owner's neglect to disturb him, and that the owner or his successor in title is entitled to possession at any time and without notice (Dougherty's Estate, 58 D. & C. 202, 209-1947). See that case also for the distinction between a license and a lease. 

New. The Landlord and Tenant Act of 1951 (Act of April 

6, 1951, P.L. 69, art. V, § 501; 68 P.S. 250.501), requiring at least thirty day's written notice to quit, would seem to have no application to a tenancy at sufferance, since the Act, by its terms, applies only to a landlord and tenant relationship under a lease, either oral or written, whereas a tenant at sufferance holds possession, not by virtue of any lease, but solely by reason of the owner's indulgence in failing to oust him. 

§ 5. Joint Estates. 

PAGE 7 

Correction in Text. The last sentence of the first paragraph should read: The second is virtually obsolete

§ 5(a)  

as to real estate and no longer requires much consideration; the other three survive to the present day, the last one especially being frequently met with in modern conveyancing. 

§ 5 (a). Joint Tenancy. 

PAGE 8 

Add to § 5 (a), page 8. For more recent cases on the right to create the incident of survivorship despite the Act of 

March 31, 1812 P.L. 259, 20 P.S. 121, see Lafayette v. 

Brinham, 363 Pa. 360-1949 and Teacher, Excx. etc. v. 

Kijurina 365 Pa. 480-1950.

The survivorship characteristic which attaches to a joint estate precludes a joint tenant from disposing of his interest by will. During his lifetime, however, he may convey his interest to a third party, or he may have the joint property partitioned (Duffy v. Duffy, 81 D. & C. 366 -1951, citing Dana v. Jackson, 6 Pa. 234-1847), or his interest may be seized and sold by his creditors in execution. In any such case, the joint tenancy comes to an end, so far as his interest is concerned, and the new owner becomes a tenant in common with the other former joint tenant or tenants (Browning v. Cover, 108 Pa. 595-1885; 

American Oil Co. v. Falconer, 136 Pa. Super. Ct. 5981939), who, if more than one, are, together, tenants in common with the new owner, but remain joint tenants as to each other. This right of a joint tenant to sever the joint tenancy during his lifetime has been held to constitute a power of revocation within the meaning of Section 

11 of the Estates Act of 1947 (Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 

100, 20 P.S. 301.11), so that where a married person creates a joint estate with another who is not his or her spouse, in real or personal property, the surviving spouse may treat it as a "conveyance" within the definition of that term by Section 1(2) of the Act, and testamentary in character and therefore subject to the surviving spouse's election to take against the will of the deceased

§ 5(a) 

7 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

spouse (Longacre v. Hornblower & Weeks, 83 D. & C. 

259-1952). In all such cases of severance of the joint tenancy, the interests of the two tenants is presumed equal, regardless of the amount actually contributed by each (Longacre v. Hornblower & Weeks, supra, citing 

American Oil Co. v. Falconer, supra).

A conveyance to a man and woman as husband and wife as tenants by entireties (but in fact unmarried) is a sufficiently clear expression of intention to overcome the presumption of the act and create a joint tenancy (Maxwell v. Saylor, 359 Pa. 94-1948). The same intent was found where a mother and daughter took title as ten ants by entireties (Hoffert v. Bastian, 54 D. & C. 1461945). Where however the granting clause of a deed read merely "to Nick Kijurina and Sarah his wife" it was held not to be sufficiently indicative of an intent to create the incident of survivorship if the grantees were in fact not husband and wife (Teacher, Excx. etc. v. Kijurina 365 Pa. 

480-1950).

New add to $5 (a), page 8. Where joint tenants die simultaneously or perish in a common disaster and there is no evidence that they died otherwise than simultaneously, the property is distributable one half as if one had survived and one half as if the other had survived. See 

Uniform Simultaneous Death Act of June 19, 1941, P.L. 

138, 68 P.S. 523.

New add to § 5 (a), page 8. The Act of August 5, 1941, P.L. 816, §§ 1, 2, 20 P.S. 3441, 3442, defines any person who participates, either as principal or as an accessory before the fact, in the wilful and unlawful killing of any other person as a "slayer." Such slayer is forbidden to acquire in any way any property, or benefit as a result of the death of the person slain. § 6 of said Act (20 P.S. 

3446) provides that one half of any property held by the slayer and slain as joint tenants shall pass at the death of the party slain (termed decedent in the Act) to the decedent's estate and the other half shall pass upon the death of the slayer to the decedent's estate unless the

§ 5(a) 

CONVEYANCING IN PENNSYLVANIA, slayer obtains a severance of the property or a decree of partition. This Act also makes provision for cases where the joint tenancy involves three or more persons and as well as where the property consists of trusts, reversions or remainders. It has been held by Judge 

Van Roden that the result of the criminal trial of homicide is not the controlling factor in determining the right of a person to inherit or benefit in the slain person's estate. The Orphans Court must hear the case "de novo." The result of the criminal trial, while not conclusive or res adjudicata, is a relevant fact, however, to be considered with other facts in determining whether the slaying was wilful and unlawful (D'Amore Est. 

37 Del. 360; 64 York 185-1950). The Slayer's Act of 

1941, supra, has been held not to operate retroactively (Smith Estate, 3 D. & C. 2d 757-1955; Wyckoff v. Clark, 

New add to § 5 (a), page 8. Municipalities as Joint Tenants. The Act of July 28, 1941, P.L. 536, 72 P.S. 6151.1, authorizes political subdivisions (other than cities of the first class) or the Commonwealth to agree through their properly constituted authorities to take title at tax sales jointly in proportion to their respective claims and to so manage or sell the same after redemption period has expired. 

PAGE 8 New add to § 5 (b), page 8. Estates in Coparcenary. § 4 

(6) of the new Intestate Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 80, 20 P.S. 1.4 (6), continues the rule that when real or personal property descends to two or more persons they shall take as tenants in common unless they be husband and wife when they shall take by entireties. 

PAGES 8-9 Add to § 5 (c), page 9. Tenancy in Common. By express terms of the Orphans Court Partition Act of June 7, 1917, 

P.L. 337, 20 P.S. 1284, in partition proceedings of real

§ 5(c) 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

estate in that court, a tenant in common who has been in possession shall have deducted from his distributive share the proportionate part of the rental value of the real estate partitioned, to which his co-tenants are entitled, and this without proof that he excluded the other co tenants from occupation (Huffman Estate No. 1, 349 Pa. 

18-1944; Search Est., 160 Pa. Super. Ct. 488-1947).

Note the curious anomaly of the decisions by which the 

Act of June 24, 1895, P.L. 237, 68 P.S. 101, though reading substantially the same as the Orphans Court Partition Act, supra, is interpreted to forbid the recovery of the proportionate share of the rent from a co-tenant in possession either in assumpsit or in equity partition proceedings unless there be a showing that he agreed to pay or excluded the other co-tenants from possession (Beck v. Beiter, 146 Pa. Super. Ct. 114-1941).

New. Co-tenants stand in a relation of confidence to each other. If one of them purchases an outstanding title, and undertakes to claim under it the common property against the others, if they contest it, his claim will not be allowed; because it is presumed that each, as regards the common interest, acts for all; the same principle is invoked as is enforced between all persons who occupy toward each other a fiduciary or confidential relation (Tanney v. Tanney, 159 Pa. 277-1893; Raker v. G. C. 

Murphy & Co., 358 Pa. 339-1948). Nor can a co-tenant purchase at a sheriff sale and hold adversely against his other co-tenants (Gibson v. Winslow, 46 Pa. 380-1863; 

Enyard v. Enyard, 190 Pa. 114-1899; Faust v. Heckler, 359 Pa. 19-1948); nor acquire an outstanding encumbrance against the whole title, foreclose it and secure a clear title as purchaser (Raker v. G. C. Murphy & Co., supra). Nor, within the time allowed for redemption, may he or his spouse acquire title from the County Commissioners of real estate sold at tax sale (Collins v. 

Wilkinson, 366 Pa. 108-1950; Hunt v. Mestrezat, 361 Pa. 

415-1949; Doud's Petition, 59 D. & C. 674-1947). In all these instances the co-tenant can be compelled to re-

§ 5(c) 

convey to the other co-tenants their proportionate interests upon payment of their proportionate shares of the cost of acquisition (Tanney v. Tanney, supra). The title acquired by a co-tenant so offending the rule of confidence is not absolutely void, but may be ratified expressly or by conduct of those whose interests are affected and so become valid, says Judge McCreary in Doud's Petition, 59 D. & C. 674, 680—1947, citing Richards v. Richards, 31 Pa. Super. Ct. 509-1906.

In a single proceeding to partition several tracts, each party to the proceeding must have an interest in every tract; otherwise a separate proceeding for each tract is necessary except where the rights of all parties are derived from a co-tenancy as a common source of title in what was then a single tract. Thus, where one tenant in common conveys his interest in a specific part of a single tract to a third party, the other co-tenant can have partition of the whole tract in a single proceeding, even though it now consists of two separate tracts and one of the parties has an interest in only one of the tracts. But the exception applies only where the co-tenancy which was the common source of title was of a single tract (Keystone 

State Theatre Co. v. Widmyer, 366 Pa. 533-1951). The principal rule above stated has been applied to result in voiding, ab initio, a reservation of haulage royalty rights in a conveyance by one co-tenant of his one-half interest in a tract to a third party who also acquired the other, unencumbered half interest. It was there held that the cotenant who conveyed subject to the reservation could not, by his conveyance, thus prejudice his co-tenant's rights by, in effect, imposing an encumbrance on the half interest acquired from the original non-reserving co-tenant, and that the owner of the whole tract was entitled to utilize the haulage rights as an incident of the half interest not so encumbered, free and clear of any obligation to pay royalty (Shober Farms, Inc. v. Merrill, 179 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 446-1955).

$ 5 (d). Estates by Entireties.

PAGE 10 

Where a deed conveys to A and B, his wife, as tenants by entireties, but in fact they are not husband and wife, a joint tenancy is created (Maxwell v. Saylor, 359 Pa. 

94-1948). So, also, a joint tenancy results from a deed to two sisters as "tenants by entireties" (Nolan's Estate, 82 D. & C. 197-1952). But where the conveyance is merely to A and B, his wife, with no other indication of intent to include the right of survivorship, and A and B actually are not husband and wife, the conveyance effects a tenancy in common (Teacher, Excx., v. Kijurina, 365 

Pa. 480-1950). In such a case, either may have partition under the Act of April 7, 1807, P.L. 155, 4 Sm. L. 398 § 1; 

12 P.S. 1694 (Briggs v. Hunter, 61 D. & C. 465-1947).

A conveyance to A and B and C, where A and B are husband and wife, will result in a tenancy in common as between A and B together (who take a one-half interest as tenants by entireties as between themselves) and C (who takes a one-half interest), if A and B are designated in the deed as husband and wife; but if they are not so designated, then A, B and C each take a one-third interest as either tenants in common or joint tenants, as may be proper (Heatter v. Lucas, 367 Pa. 296-1951; Nixdorf v. 

Busser et al., 62 D. & C. 567-1948. And see Kleinschmidt

Estate, 362 Pa. 353-1949, where the rule is applied to inheritance tax liability).

PAGE 13 Add to $5 (d) 2, page 13. Tenants by entireties may, by a conveyance in trust for their children, effectually bar a creditor of one of the grantors even though the debtor survives (Murphey v. C. I. T. Corporation, 347 Pa. 5911943; C. I. T. Corp. v. Flint, 333 Pa. 350-1939). While partition between husband and wife of entireties property cannot be compelled while the marital status exists, nevertheless there may be amicable or voluntary partitions

CONVEYANCING IN PENNSYLVANIA, effected (Runco v. Ostroski, 361 Pa. 593-1949). Also where one spouse has been declared a mental incompetent whether under the Lunacy or Habitual Drunkard Act of 

June 13, 1836, P.L. 589, or under Weak Minded Act of 

May 28, 1907, P.L. 292, repealed and supplied by the

Incompetents' Estates Act of June 28, 1951, P.L. 612, § 

102 (as amended by Act of July 28, 1953, P.L. 689, § 1) and § 443, 50 P.S. 1632, 1823, the Court may direct the committee or guardian to agree with the spouse of the incompetent to sell the real estate held by them by entirety and to divide the net proceeds equally between guardian and the competent spouse (Interboro B. & T. Co. 

Appeal, 359 Pa. 315-1948). In absence of agreement it was held the Court has no power to divide the surplus remaining after sheriff's sale of the entireties property between husband and wife (Bender v. Killey, 55 D. & C. 

700-1945). The Act of May 24, 1923, P.L. 446, 48 P.S. 

137, cited in text on page 13, was construed though not unconstitutional, to be prospective only and may not be applied to a tenancy already in existence at the time of its passage (Comm. v. Greenawalt, 347 Pa. 510-1943).

New add to § 5 (d) 2, page 13. Effect on Entireties Property where spouses die simultaneously or one slays the other. By the Uniform Simultaneous Death Act of June 

19, 1941, P.L. 138, § 3, 68 P.S. 523, where there is no sufficient evidence that the tenants by entireties have died otherwise than simultaneously, the property so held shall be distributed, one half as if one survived and the other half as if the other survived. By $$ 5 and 6 of the Act it is provided the Act does not apply to property of decedent dying before its effective date nor where the instrument provides otherwise.

The Property Rights of Slayer's Act of August 5, 1941, 

P.L. 816, § 5, 20 P.S. 3445, as to tenancies by entirety, provides one half of any property held by the slayer and decedent as tenants by the entirety shall pass on death of decedent to his estate and the other half shall be held by the slayer during his life, subject to pass upon his 1

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

death to the estate of the slain spouse. For cases on general application of the Act see Pinders Est., 61 D. & C. 

193-1948; Johnson v. Travelers Inc. Co., 95 P.J.L. 

296; D'Amore Est., 37 Del. 360; 64 York 185-1950. The

Slayer's Act of 1941 has been held not to apply retroactively to property acquired prior to the effective date of the Act by the slayer and his wife as tenants by en tireties (Wyckoff v. Clark, 77 D. & C. 249-1951; Smith 

Estate, 3 D. & C. 2d 757-1955).

New. Effect of Presumed Death of Spouse on Property 

Held by Entireties. A spouse cannot, by a decree of the 

Orphans' Court, on her petition under § 6 (a) of the 

Fiduciaries Act of June 17, 1917, P.L. 447, 20 P.S. 371, that her husband is legally presumed to be dead because of his absence of seven years, thereby perfect her title as survivor to real estate held by entireties, in order to pass good title on a sale by her alone. Her proper remedy is under 2 (a) of the Revised Price Act (Act of June 7, 1917, P.L. 388, as amended; 20 P.S. 1563), under which the

Orphans' Court may authorize the sale by the petitioning spouse (Muschlitz v. Kalman, 85 D. & C. 341-1952). The 

Fiduciaries Act of 1949 makes no change which would affect such a situation; both Acts treating of property of the presumed decedent which would pass under his will or under the intestate laws, and not of property which passes or continues by survivorship only. (See Fiduciaries Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 512, art. XII, § 1201 et seq.; 20 P.S. 

320.1201 et seq.)

PAGE 14 Add to § 5 (d) 3, page 14. Effect of Divorce on Entireties. 

Rent. 

The Act of May 10, 1927, P.L. 884, 68 P.S. 501, cited in original text, authorizing partition after divorce, was held not to operate to automatically change the entireties holding to one of tenants in common upon the granting of a divorce, nor could a creditor of one spouse institute proceedings under the Act and compel sale so as to recover

§ 5(d)3 

against proceeds due debtor spouse (Eastern Acceptance 

Corp. v. Gold, 60 D. & C. 95-1947. For recent cases interpreting this Act, see Lazare v. Lazare, 365 Pa. 5911950; and Collins v. Wilkinson, 366 Pa. 108—1950). This

Act, which repealed the earlier Act of May 13, 1925, P.L. 

649, but substantially reenacted its provisions, continues the provisions of the earlier Act in operation, so that all rights and liabilities acquired under the Act of 1925 are preserved (Christner v. Christner, 366 Pa. 41—1950).

The Act of 1927, supra, was held in Blumner v. Metropolitan Life Ins. Co., 362 Pa. 7—1949, to apply to equitable estates created by agreement of sale, as well as to legal estates and whether one spouse paid the whole consideration or not. 

New. The Act of May 17, 1949, P.L. 1394, 68 P.S. 501, effective September 1, 1949, amends the Act of 1927, supra, so that property thereafter acquired as tenants by the entirety upon divorce, will automatically be owned as tenants in common, each having one half share in value. 

This amendment therefore changes the rule of the Eastern Acceptance Corporation case, supra, as to property acquired after September 1, 1949, but not as to property acquired before that date. Another New Act of May 13, 1949 P.L. 1319, 21 P. S. 52.1, empowers either divorced spouse to convey his or her interest in real estate held by entireties directly to the other and declares such conveyance effective to establish a fee simple title in the grantee free from any interest grantor may have had. § 2 validates conveyances so made before enactment. 

PAGE 14 

Rents. Mere separation is not equivalent to divorce and a wife therefore cannot maintain a bill in equity against her undivorced husband for an accounting for the rents in absence of an allegation of non-support (Wakefield v. Wakefield, 149 Pa. Super. Ct. 9—1942; 

Lalich v. Lalich, 60 D. & C. 337—1947).

§ 5(d)15 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

PAGES 14, 15 § 5 (d) 4. Entireties in Personal Property. 

In Berkowitz Estate (No. 1), 344 Pa. 481-1942, it was held that an agreement between spouses or other independent evidence of transfers was not necessary to create an entirety, and that where a bank savings account appears in the name of husband and wife or husband or wife, an intention to create a tenancy by entirety is assumed from the deposit in both names and from the fact of marital relationship. In McEwen Estate, 348 Pa. 23 -1943, it was held that a beneficial interest in a trust could be held by entireties. As to whether household furniture jointly possessed by husband and wife living together is presumably held by entireties, the law is in a state of flux. Schwartz Estate, 68 D. & C. 154, 161-1949, which followed McCarter's Estate, 36 D. & C. 625-1939, cited in original text, was reversed by the Superior Court, 166 Pa. Super. Ct. 459-1950, but allocator was allowed by the Supreme Court. Unfortunately, the case was settled before a decision was made. However, see as contra to the Superior Court decision, Alcorn v. Alcorn, 364 Pa. 375-1950, and see Fine v. Fine, 366 Pa. 2271951.

PAGE 15 

§ 5 (d) 4. Neither Spouse May Exclude the Other From Enjoyment of Entireties Property. 

While there is no general agency nor presumption flowing from an entireties estate that either spouse has authority to convey away entireties property without the other's joinder (Schweitzer v. Evans, 360 Pa. 552—1949; 

Peterson v. Chandler, 362 Pa. 102-1949; Polka v. May, 383 Pa. 80-1955), yet where both signed an agreement of sale, an extension of the time of settlement granted by one spouse alone bound the other also, since either spouse presumptively has the power to act for both so long as the marriage subsists in matters of entireties without

§ 5(d)6 

specific authorization provided the fruits or the proceeds of such action inures to the benefit of both and the estate is not terminated (Williams v. Barbaretta, 359 Pa. 4881948). Upon the same theory a contractor who improved the entireties property was held entitled to recover against both (Wickerham v. Vitori, 345 Pa. 111-1942 and see Sidle v. Kaufman, 345 Pa. 549—1942).

PAGE 16 § 5 (d) 6. Taxation. 

The law as laid down in Walkers Est., 340 Pa. 13—1940, cited in the original text to the effect that a tenancy by entirety could not be legally created by a deed of a husband granting to his wife an undivided one half interest in his real estate, has had the attention of the legislature. 

In 1941, the Uniform Interparty Agreement Act of May 13, 1927, P.L. 984, was amended by the Act of July 3, 1941, P.L. 273 to direct it to be construed to authorize "a conveyance of an interest in real property by either husband or wife to husband and wife by entireties." Of course, this did not meet the situation in Walker's Est., supra. So in 1945 the legislature tried again (Act of 

March 21, 1945, P.L. 53) by another amendment, adding to the above quoted words of the Act of 1941, "and by husband and wife as tenants by entireties to either husband or wife alone." This still did not affect the ruling in Walker's Estate. In 1947, a third amendment (Act of 

May 31, 1947, P.L. 353) provided: "This section shall be construed as authorizing a conveyance of an interest in real property by either husband or wife without the joinder of his or her spouse to husband and wife as tenants by the entireties, and by husband and wife as tenants by the entireties to either husband or wife alone.” This was merely a rewording of the two earlier amendments combined, and left the Walker's Estate situation unchanged. By a fourth amendment (Act of August 17, 1951, P.L. 1275; 21 P.S. 551; 69 P.S. 541), the legislature

§ 5(d)17 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

added to conveyances authorized by the previous amendments, a conveyance "by either tenant by the entireties alone to the other without the other joining in the deed." 

This still does not alter the result of a conveyance such as in Walker's Estate, where the husband alone sought to convey an undivided one-half interest in his own real estate to the wife alone to hold by entireties. See Walker v. Colvin, 85 D. & C. 225-1953, where a deed by a husband to his wife in 1939 conveying an undivided one-half interest in real estate held to be his own, was held to create a tenancy in common and not by entireties. Dictum in that case to the effect that an estate by entireties could now be created by a deed similar to that in Walker's Estate if the intention to create such an estate were clearly expressed, may be open to some question, and should not be relied upon until conclusive determination by the Supreme Court. 

By the several amendments, however, (1) a husband alone or a wife alone may now convey directly to the husband and wife by entireties, or (2) the husband and wife, as tenants by entireties, may convey directly to either spouse alone, or (3) either spouse alone may convey his or her interest in property held by both by entireties (which is not the Walker case) directly to the other alone. 

Thus, under the various amendments, it is now possible for a husband or wife alone to vest in themselves a tenancy by entireties by direct conveyance, without following the old practice of conveying first to a third party and having the third party convey to the husband and wife. The last two amendments attempt to validate, retroactively, the types of conveyances thereby authorized; and the amendment of 1951 purports to validate all such conveyances made since the Act of June 3, 1911, P.L. 631 (authorizing direct conveyance from wife to husband, and amended by the Act of May 31, 1947, No. 1, P.L. 352, 48 P.S. 71). However, these attempts are of doubtful constitutionality so far as deeds made before the amending Acts are concerned, for the same reason that the Act

§ 6 

of 1911 was itself held unconstitutional as to deeds made before its enactment: see Elder v. Elder, 256 Pa. 1391917; Buchanan v. Corson, 51 Pa. Super. Ct. 558-1912; and § 34-A of original text and of this supplement.

TITLE, KINDS OF. 

§ 6. Title, What It Is. 

PAGE 17 

Add to citation at end of § 6, page 17: Henry v. Grove, 356 Pa. 541-1947.

Add to § 6, page 17. Color of Title is merely the appearance of title without its reality and an occupier of land under "color of title" differs from the naked possession of an avowed squatter only in his state of mind (Dice v. 

Reese, 342 Pa. 379—1941).

Presumptive Grant is very similar to title by adverse possession. The doctrine of presumptive grant provides that after a great lapse of time and a series of circumstances disclosing the enjoyment of unchallenged title during such period, the courts will presume whatever grant may be necessary to quiet the title (Townsend v. 

Boyd, 217 Pa. 386-1907; Donegal Twp. School Dist. v. 

Crosby, 178 Pa. Super. Ct. 30-1955).

New add to end of § 25-C, page 36. A riparian or littoral owner has in general the right of access to navigable water in front of his land, which right is distinct from and in addition to the general right of the public to use

§ 25-D 

such waters or to use the beach. Such right is compensable in damages (Philadelphia v. Penna. Sugar Co., 348 Pa. 599, 603-1944; Philadelphia v. Commonwealth, 284 Pa. 225-1925). Sand, gravel, coal and stones may, in absence of a legislative act, be freely dredged from the beds of navigable waters of the Commonwealth without let or hindrance from State officials and the right to do so under existing laws was upheld by the Dauphin County Court in McGrady-Rodgers Co. et al. v. Commonwealth et al., 43 Dauphin Co. R. 275-1937; Central

Construction Supply Co. v. Commonwealth et al., 43 

Dauphin Co. R. 293-1937. But a legislative grant of the use of water confers no title to the corpus of the water (Lehigh Coal and Nav. Co. v. Scranton Gas & 

Water Co., 6 Dist. R. 291, 307—1897). 

§ 25-D. Riparian Owner's Title in Non-Navigable 

Waters.

PAGE 37 In a case of a non-navigable pond or lake, when the land covered by the water is owned by others and no riparian rights attach to the property bordering on the lake, the ownership of land bordering on the lake does not confer on such person the right to fish in the lake or to license others to do so. One attempting to exercise such right is guilty of trespass (Loughran v. Matylewicz, 367 Pa. 593-1951; Miller v. Lutheran Conference & 

Camp Assn., 331 Pa. 241, 247—1938; Camp Chicopee v. 

Eden, 303 Pa. 150-1931; Matthews v. Bagnik, 157 Pa. 

Super. Ct. 115—1945).

or hold any real estate in Pennsylvania directly or indirectly or through a trustee or other device unless specially authorized by the laws of the Commonwealth. 

PAGES 42-45 § 29. Persons of Unsound Mind. 

§ 30. Habitual Drunkards. 

§ 30-A. Procedure to Alienate Real Estate of Mental In competents and Habitual Drunkards. § 31. Weakness of Mind, Senility and Undue Influence.

New. The Acts of May 28, 1913, P.L. 358 (relating to real estate of a lunatic), June 13, 1836, P.L. 589 (relating to procedure for sale or mortgage of real estate of a lunatic or habitual drunkard), April 27, 1903, P.L. 325 (authorizing private sale of such real estate), and May 28, 1907, P.L. 292, as amended (for protecting the property of the aged and weak-minded), all cited in the original text, were repealed as of January 1, 1952 by art. VIII, § 801 of the Incompetents' Estates Act of June 28, 1951, P.L. 

612, 50 P.S. 2041, which Act, in other sections, supplies the Acts so repealed (50 P.S. 1631 et seq.). § 1 of the 1951 

Act as amended by the Act of July 28, 1953, P.L. 689, 50

P.S. 1632, brings under the provisions of the Act virtually all types of mental incompetents, including lunatics, habitual drunkards, insane, weak-minded, senile persons and drug addicts, under the single designation "incompetent."

Under the new Act, on petition of the spouse, a relative, a creditor, a debtor, or any person interested in the alleged incompetent's welfare, the court, after hearing and inquisition, may make a finding and decree of incompetency and appoint a guardian or guardians of the in competent's estate (art. III, § 301, 50 P.S. 1681). Under art. IV, §§ 401-448, 50 P.S. §§ 1781-1828, the guardian is given the right to possess, maintain, administer and dispose of the real and personal property of the incompetent; and by art. V, § 511, 50 P.S. 1861, the decree of incompetency terminates the legal capacity of the incompe-

§ 25 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

tent to make any contract or gift or any instrument in writing. Such incapacity continues until he is adjudged to have regained his competency. Meantime, a mortgage executed by the incompetent, for example, is void (Penna. 

Co., etc. v. Phila. Title Insurance Co., 372 Pa. 2591953). Under this section of the Act, however, an interest in the incompetent's real estate situate in another county from that in which the incompetency was adjudicated, if acquired by a bona fide grantee or lienholder, will not be impaired unless the original or a duplicate original or a certified copy of the decree of incompetency is recorded in the office of the recorder of deeds of the county where the real estate is situate before the recording of the instrument under which the grantee or lienholder claims. 

It is to be noted that the 1951 Act contains no provision for any finding as to how long the incompetency has existed prior to the decree, as was required under the Act of June 13, 1836, P.L. 589, § 3 in cases of lunacy. The decree under the 1951 Act operates prospectively only; and whether a deed or other document executed by a person, later adjudged incompetent, is void because of actual incompetency at the time of execution, is still dependent upon an issue of fact, the burden of proof being upon him who asserts the incompetency.

While the Act of 1951 gives the guardian of an incompetent's estate ample powers necessary for management and administration, the real estate cannot be sold or mortgaged (nor, under art. IV, § 442, 50 P.S. 1822, leased for a term of more than five years) without court authority (art. IV, § 443, 50 P.S. 1823). Such authority may be granted whenever the court finds it to be for the best interests of the incompetent, on petition of the guardian setting forth the necessary facts and supported by the pertinent documents such as two appraisal affidavits, copy of agreement of sale (if any), etc. The Act of 1951 does not specify the form of the procedure nor what the petition shall contain; but it is usual to follow the procedure provided by $ 4 of the Revised Price Act of June 7, 1917,

§ 31 

P.L. 388 as amended (20 P.S. 1601), and the petition is frequently stated as filed under both Acts. It is essential that the proper procedure be followed; and failure to notify the next of kin of the intended application for authority to sell the incompetent's real estate makes the sale void as to such next of kin (Frederick Estate, 156

Pa. Super. Ct. 547-1944). But it was held otherwise as to one of the kin whose interest was merely contingent, inchoate and expectant and was discharged by consumption of the proceeds of sale during the lifetime of the incompetent (Foley v. Smay, 352 Pa. 292-1945). While these last two cases were decided under the earlier Acts, the 1951 Act requires such notice as the court may direct; and the court will ordinarily require notice to the next of kin of the incompetent (i. e., to those having any present or expectant interest in the real estate, as required by $ 4 of the Revised Price Act, 20 P.S. 1601), unless, of course, written consents of the next of kin are attached to the petition. Hence the rulings in Frederick 

Estate, supra, and Foley v. Smay, supra, are probably applicable to sales and mortgages under the 1951 Act. See also McFarland Estate, 377 Pa. 290-1954, holding that where an incompetent had a husband who had deserted her, a father and three sisters, the father was the only next of kin and therefore was the only person entitled to notice of sale of the incompetent's real estate under the 

Act of 1907. 

Whether a committee be appointed after inquiry into lunacy or habitual drunkenness under the Act of June 13, 1836, P.L. 589 and its supplements, or a guardian under the Act of May 28, 1907, P.L. 292 and its supplements, the duties are the same (Riebel's Estate, 321 Pa. 1451936). They are mere bailiffs of the court whose ward the incompetent is, and his estate is in "custodia legis.” 

They must act by direction of the court and have no authority to dispose of the incompetent's real estate without authorization of the court (Interboro Bank and Trust 

Co. Appeal, 359 Pa. 315-1948). This is true also under

§ 27 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

the 1951 Act (McFarland Estate, 377 Pa. 290, 296–1954).

Both the Revised Price Act of 1917 as amended (20 

P.S. 1563) and the Incompetents' Estates Act of 1951 (50 

P.S. 1823) give the court jurisdiction to decree sale where either spouse has been declared mentally incompetent and the spouse of sound mind joins in the petition. But apparently this power exists independently of these statutes (Interboro Bank and Trust Co. Appeal, 359 Pa. 315-1948).

On the death (or adjudication of competency) of the incompetent the functions and powers of his committee or guardian cease, and only the duty and liability to account remains (Davidson's Estate, 323 Pa. 113-1936; 

Harvey Appeal, 170 Pa. Super. Ct. 289-1952; Gerlach's 

Estate, 127 Pa. Super. Ct. 293-1937).

PAGE 45 Undue influence which will avoid a deed is such influence as is obtained by excessive importunity, superiority of will or mind, or any other means constraining the grantor to do what he is unable to refuse (Withers v. 

Withers, 363 Pa. 431-1950). Whether undue influence was exercised must usually be inferred from facts and circumstances such as the situation of the grantor and his relationship to others, his condition of health and its effect on his body and mind, his dependence upon and subjection to the persons claimed to have influenced him and their opportunity to wield such influence and the inadequacy of consideration if any (Teats v. Anderson, 358 Pa. 523-1948). Generally the burden of proof rests on the one who asserts the facts but this rule is not applicable where the relationship of the parties to each other or some vicious element in connection with the transaction is such that the law compels the recipient of the bequest or gift to show it was the free voluntary and intelligent act of the person giving it (Kees, Exr. v. 

Green, 365 Pa. 368-1950; Williams v. McCarroll, 374 

Pa. 281, 295-1953. See also Lasky v. Paprocki, 363 Pa.

§ 32 

50-1949 and Williams v. Lucas, 376 Pa. 413-1954, where the burden of proof was not met). 

§ 32. Infants. 

PAGE 45 An infant may disaffirm an agreement to buy real estate and may recover the deposit money (Ferita v. Farrise, 360 Pa. 382-1948).

PAGE 46 The right to disaffirm is given to infants for their own protection and will not be extended beyond the required limits. So where an adult husband and minor wife executed an agreement to buy real estate it was held the disaffirmance by the minor wife did not affect the liability of the husband even though the purchase was by entireties (Wharen v. Funk, 152 Pa. Super. Ct. 133—1943).

PAGE 46 As to mortgage loans to minor servicemen or minor wives under U. S. Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 

1944; see Acts of April 6, 1945, P.L. 164 as amended by 

Act of June 28, 1947, P.L. 1069 and the Act of May 24, 1951, 51 P.S. 701.

PAGE 46 

Under the old Act of March 22, 1865, P.L. 30, 21 P.S. 51, declaring valid the execution and acknowledgment of a minor wife in conjunction with an adult husband, a deed conveying his real estate was held still to be in force and not repealed by the Price Act of 1917 (20 P.S. 1561 et seq.). See In re Sarama, 33 D. & C. 82—1938. This Act has now been limited in its application to minors of 17 years and older, but is extended to cover also cases where a minor husband joins with an adult wife in conveyance of her real estate (Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 508, 21 P.S. 

51). This Act contains a validating clause as to such deeds previously executed.

PAGE 48 Failure of consideration alone is not ground for setting a deed aside because grantor has a remedy at law to recover agreed consideration (Sellers v. Sellers, 316 Pa.

(b) Fraud on Creditors. Fraudulent Conveyances Acts. 

PAGE 48 The English statute of 13 Eliz. Ch. 5 is now set forth in 39 P.S. Appendix, page 309 et seq.

A conveyance by a person in trust with a spendthrift trust provision securing him the income for life with general power of appointment will not operate to defeat existing or subsequent creditors (Bowers' Trust 

Estate, 346 Pa. 85, 89-1943; Mogridge's Estate, 342 Pa. 

308-1941; Jackson's Estate, 351 Pa. 89-1945). But where a husband and wife as tenants by entireties execute a trust deed conveying a beneficial interest to themselves for life as tenants by entireties with spendthrift trust clause and remainder to their children, such a deed is not void under the fraudulent conveyances Act at the instance of the creditor of one of them. However, if the debtor survives then his life interest but not the remainder estate would be subject to his creditors' execution because the spendthrift trust clause is ineffectual as to his creditors (Murphey v. C. I. T. Corporation, 347 Pa. 591-1943; 

Bowers' Trust Estate, 346 Pa. 85, 89–1943; and see Use of United States National Bk. v. Penrod, 354 Pa. 1701946).

§ 31 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

PAGE 49 For more recent cases interpreting Sec.

4 of the Fraud ulent Conveyances Act, see Berger v. Kraisman, 376 Pa. 

127-1954; Commonwealth Trust Co. of Pittsburgh v. 

Cirigliano, 352 Pa. 108-1945; Lunnen v. Hunter, 348 Pa. 

402-1944; Angier v. Worrell, 346 Pa. 450-1943; Commonwealth v. Smith, 344 Pa. 381-1942.

PAGE 50 

A fraudulent grantor cannot have his deed set aside. 

Only his creditors may do that (Eaves v. Snyder, 368 Pa. 

459-1951; Smart v. Baroni, 360 Pa. 296-1948).

Where a husband renders himself insolvent by using his own property as a portion of the purchase price of real estate acquired by him and his wife as tenants by entireties, such real estate is subject to the claim of a creditor of the husband to the extent of his investment in the property (Taylor v. Kaufhold, 379 Pa. 191-1954; 

Amadon v. Amadon, 359 Pa. 434-1948).

New. A transfer by an insolvent debtor that does not reduce the value of his estate, in that he receives in exchange full value for the transfer, is not a preference that can be set aside either in bankruptcy or under $ 2 of the State Insolvency Act of June 4, 1901, P.L. 404, 39 P.S. 152 (Schooley v. Schooley & Co., Inc., 355 Pa. 

507-1947). But the consideration must be fair and adequate (Waldman v. Redding, 70 D. & C. 585–1950). 

§ 34. Married Women. 

PAGE 51 New. The legislature attempted to remove the restrictions of the Act June 8, 1893, P.L. 344, 48 P.S. 31, 32, which prevented a married woman from mortgaging or conveying her property without joinder of her husband, by the amendment of May 17, 1945, P.L. 625, 48 P.S. 31.

The amendment was defective, however, because in its title it purported to authorize a married woman "to

§ 34 

mortgage or convey her real property" to the same extent as a married man, whereas § 2 of the amendment expressly prohibits her from conveying her real property unless her husband joins in the deed. This defect raises a serious question of constitutionality of the amending 

Act. Article III, § 3 of the Pennsylvania constitution requires that the subject matter of an Act be “clearly” expressed in the title; and a misleading title has been regarded as a more serious defect than one that is merely inadequate (see Union Passenger Ry. Co.'s Appeal, 81 * 

Pa. 91-1872 and Provident L. & T. Co. v. Hammond, 230

Pa. 407-1911). While the constitutionality of the amending Act of 1945 has not been judicially passed upon, it was held by Judge Gordon in a well considered opinion (Briggs v. Hunter, 61 D. & C. 465-1947) that § 1 of the

Act, which provided that a married woman might "sell or otherwise dispose of" her real property "in the same manner and to the same extent as a married man, was a general provision; that under settled principles of construction of statutes, the general provision must yield to the special provision of § 2 which prohibited a married woman's conveyance without the joinder of her husband; and that consequently a deed of a married woman without such joinder was still as void under the Act of 1945 as it was under the old Act of 1893. See also Bosses v. 

Mahalsky, 365 Pa. 184, 186-1950, in which an option to the lessee to purchase, contained in a lease signed by a married woman as lessor, without joinder of her husband, was held void because it was a written instrument conveying an interest in real estate within the meaning of § 2 of the Act. 

Other decisions construing the Act of 1945 are Palatucci v. Woodland, 166 Pa. Super. Ct. 315-1950 and Banos v. Banos, 60 D. & C.'658 which give effect to that part of § 2 which abrogates the provision of the prior law of 1893, § 2 (48 P.S. 32), prohibiting a married woman from becoming "an accommodation indorser, maker, guarantor or surety for another." The Superior Court in the Palatucci case did not pass on the constitutionality of the Act.

§ 33 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

And in the Banos case Judge Knight ruled only that the title was sufficient notice of the abrogation of the surety or accommodation prohibition and he further said the defect so far as conveyances and mortgages are concerned did not render the rest of the Act invalid so far as the surety provision was concerned. 

In 1947, § 2 of the Act of June 8, 1893, supra, as amended by the Act of 1945, supra, was again amended by the Act of April 11, 1947, P.L. 60, 48 P.S. 32, by adding a further provision that the required joinder in the conveyance may be (1) by the husband personally, or (2) by the wife as attorney in fact under a power of attorney executed by the husband or (3) by a third person as attorney in fact for the husband under a power of attorney executed by the husband. This amendment of course did not correct the defect of the 1945 Act. At the same session of the legislature by a separate Act, being the Act of 

May 31, 1947, P.L. 352, #159, 48 P.S. 32, made a further amendment to § 2 of the Act of 1893 as amended by the Act of 1945, by adding a clause reading "Provided however that this section shall not be construed to apply to conveyances of real estate made by a married woman to her husband, or to her husband and herself jointly." 

This also does not cure the defect of the Act of 1945 above referred to.

In 1951 the legislature, apparently recognizing the difficulty with the 1945 amendment, endeavored to correct it by the Act of August 24, 1951, P.L. 1416, 48 P.S. 31. The title of the Act states its purpose to be to further amend 1 of the Act of 1893 by "clarifying the prohibition against a married woman's right to sell real estate without the joinder of her husband." The amending Act then provides that a married woman may lease or mortgage her real estate (omitting the right to sell or dispose of it) "in the same manner and to the same extent as a married man, but she may not execute or acknowledge a written instrument conveying her real property unless her husband join in such conveyance. 99 If the 1945 amendment should be held unconstitutional,

§ 34 

as it may be if and when the question is raised, the 1893 Act and the 1951 amendment will remain as the only statutes to be considered. Under the 1893 Act, a married woman could neither mortgage nor convey her real property without joinder of her husband. The 1951 amendment, while in terms giving her the right to mortgage without such joinder, fails to indicate such a purpose in its title. Hence it, too, may well be held unconstitutional for that reason, although the question has not yet been passed upon judicially. In the recent case of Pedrick v. 

Gordon, 382 Pa. 26-1955, the Supreme Court held that a married woman's lease without joinder of her husband is not a conveyance, and therefore is not within the prohibition against such conveyances imposed by the Act of 1893 and the two amendments discussed above. In its opinion, the Supreme Court, referring to the Act and the two amendments says (page 28): "She is not only specifically empowered to lease her real estate, she may even mortgage it." While this might seem conclusive, it should be borne in mind that the question of constitutionality of the amendments does not appear to have been raised, was not considered (so far as the opinion indicates), and certainly was not decided.

Because of the constitutional doubts indicated, no title company in Philadelphia will at present insure a mortgage (or deed) by a married woman unless her husband joins; and no conveyancer or title searcher can afford to approve either a deed or a mortgage by a married woman without her husband's joinder unless and until the Supreme Court holds one or both to be valid in a decision which considers and decides the constitutional questions referred to above.

The apparent conflict in the cases on whether the deed of a married woman without her husband's joinder is absolutely void (Richards v. McClelland, 29 Pa. 385–1851; 

Glidden v. Strupler, 52 Pa. 400—1866), or merely voidable and hence susceptible of ratification (Jourdan v. 

Dean, 175 Pa. 599-1896; Haines Trust, 356 Pa. 10-1947), has now been resolved by the clear and convincing opin-

34-A 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

ion of Mr. Justice Stearne in Bosses v. Mahalsky, 365 

Pa. 184-1950. There it is held that a married woman's execution of a written instrument which operates to convey an interest in real estate without joinder of her husband is absolutely void and during coverture cannot be validated by ratification or estoppel, but should she become discovert, then there may be a ratification express or implied (Simons Est., 20 Pa. Super. Ct. 450, 468, 4691902; Haines Trust, 356 Pa. 10, 13-1947). Mr. Justice

Stearne well says, "Whether we use the word void or voidable in describing a deed executed solely by the wife during coverture is immaterial. The settled rule is that such a deed is defective; it cannot be used as a basis of title legal or equitable, unless and until either the husband actually joins in executing such deed in the form. prescribed by statute or until the incapacity imposed by coverture is removed and the defect cured by some act of the wife indicating her election to exercise her new power to solely execute the deed." See also Smigell v. 

Brod, 366 Pa. 612, 614-1951, stating without discussion that a married woman may neither mortgage nor convey her real property without her husband's joinder.

This Act will probably for the same constitutional reasons mentioned in Elder v. Elder, 256 Pa. 139-1917, be construed to be prospective only. § 36. Fiduciaries. 

PAGE 55 All executors must join in the sale of decedent's real estate even where the will works a conversion (Simmons 

Est., 254 Pa. 231-1916), unless of course the will otherwise directs. But as to estates of decedents dying after 

January 1, 1950 the rule is different because § 519 of the 

Fiduciaries Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 512 (20 P.S. 320.- 

519) authorizes a majority of the personal representatives to determine questions if a dispute arises, unless the will provides otherwise.

PAGE 56 New. A radical change concerning the power of fiduciaries to sell real estate was made by the new Fiduciaries 

Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 512 (20 P.S. 320.101 et seq.) effective January 1, 1950, as to decedents dying after that date, and as to testamentary trusts and irrevocable trusts only if becoming effective on or after that date. 

Under this Act, personal representatives, under §§ 541, 542 (20 P.S. 320.541, 320.542) may, unless the will otherwise provides, sell at public or private sale a decedent's real estate not specifically devised, and may lease the same for a term expiring not more than a year after de-

§ 37 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

cedent's death, unless the lease is terminable on 30 days' notice or unless a longer term is approved by court. By $ 

543 (20 P.S. 320.543), where forbidden by the will, or not authorized by the preceding sections of the Act, or a judicial sale is desirable, the personal representatives may sell, mortgage, lease, exchange or grant option to do so under order of the Orphans Court of the County where the letters testamentary or administration were granted. 

Similar power is given to trustees under §§ 961, 962, 963 (20 P.S. 320.961, 320.962, 320.963). The broad powers of sale given to personal representatives and trustees is not given to guardians. Guardians may lease minors' real estate under § 1062, but not for more than five years nor beyond date when the minor shall attain his majority (20 

P.S. 320.1062). A guardian may not sell either at public or private sale or mortgage or grant an option, etc., without order of the Court.

For further discussion of these new powers of fiduciaries see infra this Supplement § 170-D et seq. The implied power to mortgage inferred from a power of sale given to a fiduciary, discussed on page 57 of the original text, does not include authority of a fiduciary, by an extension agreement, to charge the trust assets (other than the mortgaged property) with liability for a mortgage which, though a lien on decedent's real estate, was not a debt of decedent (Borden's Est. O.C. (Phila. Co.) #777 of January 1927, not reported). Nor does authority to execute a renewal mortgage include authority to a fiduciary to execute the bond (Hartje's Estate, 345 Pa. 570-1942).

Pittsburgh, 365 Pa. 386-1950). ་ ་ས་ 

§ 37. The Commonwealth or the State. Eminent Domain. 

PAGE 58 The United States also has the right of eminent domain and it has been held when the Government so acquires property it acquires a fee simple title regardless of outstanding interests therein or encumbrances or liens. The fund paid is distributable to persons determined entitled by appropriate proceedings, in the State 

Courts (U. S. v. 25,936 Acres of Land, 153 Fed. 2nd, 277-1946). (a) Eminent Domain. Where no property is actually taken by the Commonwealth but the existing highway is resurfaced within the existing lines or location though the grade be changed, the abutting property owner, in absence of legislation so providing, cannot recover damages (Wahaly v. Allegheny County, 345 Pa. 509—1942).

Nor is Commonwealth liable for interest after a verdict unless bound to pay by statute or contract of executive officers (Culver v. Commonwealth, 348 Pa. 472—1944), although condemnors other than the Commonwealth are

liable for interest from the date of filing of the report by the viewers (Act of March 26, 1931, P.L. 10, No. 7, 26 P.S. 

43). Unless there has been a reservation in the deed the grantor cannot recover damages where the condemnation took place after the land was conveyed (Smith v. Commonwealth, 351 Pa. 68-1945). The damages awarded are payable to the legal owner of the land at the time of the taking (Hunter v. McKlveen, 353 Pa. 357-1946); but he holds the money as trustee for others having an interest in the property, including an equitable owner

The use for which a State can condemn property must be a public use for its own citizens and not for the citizens of another state or sovereignty (Darlington v.
