§ 40. Unincorporated Societies and Churches.

PAGE 66 The rule that a trustee has no power to sell trust property unless such power is conferred by the instrument creating the trust either by express words or necessary implication (see § 33 of original text), applies with all its force to land held by charitable institutions. Land dedi-

§ 41 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

cated to charity and religion was held to be practically inalienable (Nauman v. Weidman, 182 Pa. 263-1897; 

Woodring v. Lesher, 147 Pa. Super. Ct. 340-1942. See also Lehigh University v. Hoover, 159 Pa. Super. Ct. 

84-1946). It therefore cannot be sold without authorization of the Court (Seif v. Krebs, 239 Pa. 423-1913) in proceedings under § 2 (a) (as amended by Acts of July 

1, 1937, P.L. 2659, § 1 and August 5, 1941, P.L. 824, § 1) 

3, 4, 5 of the Revised Price Act of June 7, 1917, P.L. 388 (20 P.S. 1562 et seq.). However, distinction may be made between real estate dedicated, conveyed or devised to the institution in trust and that acquired for its charities or corporate purposes. The former can be conveyed only upon authorization of the Court under the Revised Price Act (supra). The latter can be sold without authorization under § 308 of the Non Profit Corporation Law of 1933, 

15 P.S. 2851-308 (see § 38 (c), page 64 of original text) 

(Florence Crittenton Circle v. Kotuna, 32 D. & C. 4131938). But Judge Trimble, in Keefe's Est., 42 D. & C. 

426-1941, held that the strict rule of inalienability without court authorization no longer applies to churches where the rules, regulations and usages of the church provide for power of disposition under the Act of June 20, 1935, P.L. 353, 10 P.S. 81. This was followed in Lamb, Bishop v. Yencsik, 89 D. & C. 35-1954.

For a more recent case holding that title to a church property is retained by that part of a split congregation, whether majority or minority, which continues in harmony with the laws, usages and customs of the church body before the split, see First Church of the Brethren v. 

Snider, 367 Pa. 78-1951. PAGE 67 

Canovaro v. Brothers, etc., 326 Pa. 76–1937, cited in original text, was cited and followed in Chester Housing 

Authority v. Ritter, 344 Pa. 653-1942.

§ 41 

§ 41. Partnerships. 

PAGE 68 (a) General Partnership. As between partners and their devisees, real estate originally acquired with partnership funds or by crediting a partner's capital account with moneys expended by him for it, is partnership property under § 8 clause 2 of the Partnership Act and where title is taken in the name of a partner, he holds the property under a resulting trust regardless of the fact that part of the purchase money was not advanced by the partnership in the first instance (Gerlach Est., 364 Pa. 207-1950; see also Miller v. Miller, 370 Pa. 520-1952). But after liquidation of the partnership, all creditors having been paid and settlement having been made among the partners, firm real estate reverts from its personal property status and is thereafter held by the former partners as tenants in common of real property (Faust v. Heckler, 359 Pa. 19-1948).

CHAPTER IV. 

METHODS OF ACQUIRING TITLE BY PURCHASE. 

INSTRUMENTS OF CONVEYANCING. 

AGREEMENTS OF SALE. 

AGREEMENTS OF SALE. 

§ 42-A. Options. 

PAGE 75 

Definition of Option. See Phillips et ux v. Tetzner, 357 

Pa. 43—1947, citing Ladner, and Taylor v. Hartman, 370 

Pa. 146-1952.

PAGE 76 

As to termination of option, see also Phillips et ux v.

Tetzner supra. And as to title of option relating back to

§ 42-A 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

date when option was granted, see Detwiler v. Capone, 357 Pa. 495, 499-1947, citing Ladner. The same case holds also that when no definite time for exercise of option is fixed in a lease, the law implies the term of the lease is the time within which it must be exercised. PAGE 76 New. When the option is contained in a lease, a purchaser of the premises takes subject to the option, for he is charged with knowledge of the lease provisions, being bound to inquire from the tenant on what terms he holds possession (Atlantic Refining Co. v. Wyoming Nat. Bank, 356 Pa. 226-1947; Taylor v. Hartman, 370 Pa. 1461952).

PAGE 76 New. An option is exercised by any clear communication by the optionee to the optionor to that effect, within the time provided in the option, and payment or tender of the purchase price at that time is not necessary in order to exercise the option unless the option agreement provides for it (Taylor v. Hartman, 370 Pa. 146-1952). While the option itself must be in writing, the notice of its exercise may be oral in the absence of a provision requiring such notice to be in writing (Detwiler v. Capone, 357 Pa. 495, 500-1947).

PAGE 76 New. It has been held that a provision in a lease that "lessor shall have the first option on buying the property" at a stated price does not give lessee an absolute option to purchase, but the right so acquired is conditional upon desire or purpose of lessor to sell (Reichman v. 

Fisher, 60 D. & C. 673—1947); but where the provision read that the tenant should have the "first option and privilege" of purchasing demised premises at any time before a stated date, the option was held unconditional and absolute to that date (Lancaster Malleable Castings

§ 42-A 

Co. v. Dunie, 365 Pa. 95—1950, distinguishing the Reichman case).

Where a lease gives the lessee the first right to meet an offer to purchase, and he declines to meet a third party's offer to purchase the leased property and other property of the lessor, the lessor is not free to sell to the third party, and if he does so the lessee can compel the third party to reconvey the leased premises to the lessor to be held by him subject to the lessee's option. Of course, if the lessor does not accept the third party's offer to purchase, the property remains subject to the lessee's option (L. E. Wallach, Inc. v. Toll, 381 Pa. 423-1955). Note, however, that a provision in a deed conveying a fee, that either the grantee or the grantor (who owns adjoining property) shall have first right to purchase the other's property at its fair market value, if such other party desires to sell, has been held void and unenforceable, first, because it is an unreasonable restraint on alienation (since it is not merely a matter of meeting a third party's offer but requires determination of the fair market value), and second, because it violates the rule against perpetuities, since no time limit is provided (Concannon v. Haile, 81 D. & C. 480—1952).

PAGE 77 An option, even before exercise, is so far real property as to enable the optionee to restrain the optionor from removing the top soil of the optioned property (Walsh v. 

Powell, 76 D. & C. 108—1951).

Since an option is an interest in real estate it must conform to the Statute of Frauds (see § 43 of original text) i.e., it must not only be in writing but signed by the party to be charged. Therefore a lease containing an option signed only by one spouse cannot be enforced against the other spouse who did not sign (Bosses v. 

Mahalsky, 365 Pa. 184–1950; Gilman v. Dektor, 56 D. & 

C. 522—1946). But where the option lease was executed by a man before he married it was held valid and enforce-

§ 45 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

able though not exercised until after his marriage (Detwiler v. Capone, 357 Pa. 495-1947).

New 

§ 42-B. Options by Fiduciaries. 

As a general rule, fiduciaries, unless given the express right to do so by the instrument under which they are acting, have no power to grant an option. A discretionary power of sale does not by implication include the power to grant an option, for the reason that an option is not the exercise or use of the power but a suspension of it (Hickok v. Still, 168 Pa. 155-1895). So also, while a trustee possessing the power to sell may lease until a sale is actually made, subject, of course, to governing statutes (see pp. 202-3 of this Supplement), he cannot include in that lease an option to sell (Moore v. Trainer, 252 Pa. 367-1916. See, also, article by Israel Packel, Esq., of the Philadelphia Bar, in Fiduciary Review of May, 1947).

No right to give an option is included in the statutory power to sell given to personal representatives by § 541 of the Fiduciaries Act of 1949 (20 P.S. 320-541), nor to trustees by § 961 (20 P.S. 320-961), and is expressly excluded from the implied powers of the power to sell granted to them (§§ 544, 964); nor is any such power given by the Act to guardians, but power is given to the Court to authorize the granting of options by all such fiduciaries by $$ 543, 963, 1063 of the Act (20 P.S. 

320-543, 320-963, 320-1063). For fuller discussion of fiduciaries' power to deal with real estate under the new Fiduciaries Act of 1949, see pages 205 et seq. of this Supplement. 

§ 43. Must be in Writing. Pa. Statute of Frauds. 

PAGE 77 New. "The object of the statute is to prevent the assertion of verbal understandings in the creation of in-

§ 43(a)  

terests or estates in land and to obviate the opportunity, for fraud and perjury. It is not a mere rule of evidence but a declaration of public policy. In the absence of equities sufficient of themselves to take the case out of the statute it operates as a limitation upon judicial authority to afford a remedy unless renounced or waived by the party entitled to claim its protection." (Haskell v. Heathcote, 363 Pa. 184, 188—1949). (a) Pennsylvania Statute of Frauds PAGE 78 The provisions of the Statute of Frauds relating to leases, as construed in Ferri v. Liberatoscioli, 338 Pa. 

454-1940 (cited in the original text) have been repeated in substance and codified by Art. II, § 202 of the Landlord and Tenant Act of April 6, 1951, P.L. 69, 68 P.S. 250202. That section provides that an unwritten lease for more than three years shall have the effect of a lease at will only, except that where the tenancy has continued for more than one year and the landlord and tenant have recognized its existence by claiming and admitting liability for the rent, the tenancy shall become one from year to year.

Oral express authority from one spouse to another to execute a 25 year lease of real estate held by entireties, is not binding because of the Statute of Frauds (Schweitzer v. Evans, 360 Pa. 552-1949. See also Polka v. May, 383 

Pa. 80-1955).

The Statute of Frauds does not require an agreement of sale of real estate to be signed by the purchaser, but only by the parties making or creating "the interest in land." Hence, the purchaser is bound by his oral contract (Di Benedetto v. Di Rocco, 372 Pa. 302-1953); and since the Statute is for the benefit of vendor, he may waive it (Haskell v. Heathcote, 363 Pa. 184-1949). Such waiver may be by admission in pleadings or testimony that the seller did in fact enter into the contract (Soffee v. Hall,

§ 43(a) 

47 

1956 SUPPLEMENT, 

377 Pa. 306, 311-1954; Suchan v. Swope, 357 Pa. 16, 211947; Zlotziver v. Zlotziver, 355 Pa. 299-1946).

PAGE 78 

New. A sale of trees or standing timber sold for immediate severance is not within the statute; but where the contract is indefinite as to time of cutting and removal it is a sale of an interest in land and is then within the Statute 

(Edwards v. Glaske, 165 Pa. Super. Ct. 108-1949). Other things held to be interests in land and hence within the Statute are: Lease of coal in place (Shenandoah Borough v. Philadelphia, 367 Pa. 180-1951; Smith v. Glen 

Alden Coal Co., 347 Pa. 290-1943); Oil and gas "in solido," (Dierken v. Shultz, 159 Pa. Super. Ct. 1731946); Parol antenuptial agreement concerning real estate (Kearney's Est., 60 D. & C. 217-1947). An oral promise to give a mortgage on real property as security for a loan is barred by the Statute of Frauds because a mortgage creates an interest in land within the meaning of the statute (Flood, J., in Zack v. Bak, 83 D. & C. 1191952).

PAGE 79 

Oral evidence of a material misrepresentation which induces the execution of a written agreement of sale is admissible for it does not alter or vary the instrument but aims to strike it down as though it had never existed (that is, in the absence of an integration clause providing that there were no oral representations) (La Course v. 

Kiesel, 366 Pa. 385, 390, 391-1951). As to what constitutes such fraud or bad faith as to entitle a vendee to recover more than hand money and title charges, see 

Frey v. Nakles, 380 Pa. 616–1955 and Smith v. Krause, 64 D. & C. 451-1948. 

For more recent cases setting forth requisites for specific performance of an oral contract of sale where there has been part performance or other equities, see 

Yarnall Estate, 376 Pa. 582-1954; Rarry v. Shimek, 360

§ 43(a) 

CONVEYANCING IN PENNSYLVANIA, 

Pa. 315-1948 (parol gift); and Brotman v. Brotman, 353

Pa. 570-1946. The enforcement of such oral contracts is a matter of grace and will not be enforced if unconscionable (Ewing's Est., 41 D. & C. 44–1941), and the proof must be full, complete, satisfactory and indubitable (Klingensmith v. Klingensmith, 375 Pa. 178—1953).

New. Neither the Statute of Frauds, nor the Act relating to oral trusts (Act of April 22, 1856, P.L. 532, § 4, 33

P.S. 2) apply to constructive trusts or trusts ex male ficio (Hamburg v. Barsky, 355 Pa. 462–1947). But an action to enforce such implied or resulting trust must be brought within five years from time the trust arose or the fraud was or should have been discovered, because of the limitations in § 6 of the Act of 1856 (12 P.S. 83) (Keefer v. Mc- 

Ginnis, 365 Pa. 601-1950; Ross v. Suburban Counties 

Realty Corp., 356 Pa. 126-1947). In Sneiderman v.

Kahn, 350 Pa. 496-1944 it was held that a resulting trust can be raised only by actual fraud against the putative cestue que trust in and at the time title is obtained, or by payment of purchase money by the c. q. t. at that time. 

See also to same effect Moyer v. Moyer, 356 Pa. 1841947; and Gray v. Leibert, 357 Pa. 130-1947.

Where a parent furnishes the purchase money and title is taken in the name of a child, there is a presumption of a gift; but where title is taken by the parent and the purchase money is furnished by the child, the presumption is that it is a resulting trust, not a gift (Ehnes v. Yowell, 374 Pa. 17-1953).

Where a resulting trust arises, it is nevertheless void as to bona fide judgment creditors unless a written declaration of trust has been executed and acknowledged by the holder of the legal title and recorded under the Act of June 4, 1901, P.L. 425, § 1, 21 P.S. 601. But where a judgment or other lien is not acquired until after the holder of legal title has executed the trust by conveying the property to the beneficiary, the Act of 1901 has no application (Ehnes v. Yowell, supra, citing, inter alia, Malamed v. Sedelsky, 367 Pa. 353, 355, 356-1951).

§ 49 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

(b) Written Agreement Cannot be Reformed by Oral Testimony 

PAGE 80 In the absence of fraud, accident or mistake, parol evidence is inadmissible to show a different consideration from that expressed in the written agreement (Grubb v. Rockey, 366 Pa. 592-1951). 66 While extrinsic evidence is admissible to explain or clarify an ambiguity in a written instrument, if the instrument is clear and unambiguous, its terms may not be explained" by parol evidence (Boardman-Smith Corp. v. Sherman, 176 Pa. Super. Ct. 302-1954; Barlett v. 

Beverly School Land Co., 170 Pa. Super. Ct. 307-1952). 

§ 44. What the Writing Must Contain: Form. 

PAGE 80 The agreement may consist of several writings only one of which is signed if (1) the signed writing is physically annexed to the other writing by the party to be charged, or (2) the signed writing refers to the other, or (3) it appears from examination of all the writings that the signed writing was signed with reference to the unsigned writings (Fleming v. Strayer, 163 Pa. Super. Ct. 607-1949).

PAGE 82 Note that since many municipalities now impose sewer rents as well as water rents, the apportionment clause of form of agreement on page 82 of original text may read: 

Taxes, water and sewer rent, if any, etc. 

Since the imposition of realty transfer taxes on conveyances, by the State (Act of December 27, 1951, P.L. 1742 as amended by Act of May 29, 1953, P.L. 257 and Act of 

June 1, 1955 (No. 38), 72 P.S. 3283 et seq.), and by many local municipalities, a clause should be inserted in the agreement of sale to provide who is to pay such taxes. 

The State tax was initially imposed as a temporary tax

§ 44 

for a two year period; but each subsequent legislature has reenacted the tax measure for an additional two years. 

The Act of June 1, 1955, in effect, extends the tax through May 31, 1957. Under the provisions of § 3 of the amended 

Act (72 P.S. 3285), which impose the liability on any party making or accepting a deed, either party can be held liable to the Commonwealth; and liability under the several municipal ordinances depends upon the provisions of the particular ordinance which should be examined if the question arises. Each of the State and municipal realty transfer taxes is one percent of the sale price; and in most cases, the parties agree that the seller shall pay the State tax and the buyer shall pay the local tax on the conveyance.

New. Zoning Statements. The Act of July 27, 1955 (No. 

104), 21 P.S. 611-615, effective September 1, 1955 in cities of the first class (i.e., Philadelphia) makes it mandatory, under penalty of $1,000. fine or one year imprisonment, or both, for the seller of any building "used, designed or intended for use by three or more families" or any commercial establishment containing one or more dwelling units, to include in the agreement of sale (including a lease with an option to purchase and a lease which, in effect, is an instalment sale) a "statement as to the zoning classification and legal use of the property to be conveyed and whether the present use of the property is in compliance with, or in violation of, zoning laws and ordinances." In addition, the seller must deliver to the buyer, at or before settlement, a "use registration permit," issued by the appropriate city officer, indicating the zoning classification and legal use of the property. (See page 267 of this supplement for further discussion of the permit requirements.) Whether such zoning statement and permit are required in the case of a lease containing a right of first refusal, as distinguished from an outright option to purchase (see pages 43-4 of this supplement) is not clear; but in view of the expressed intent of the 

Act to protect purchasers of such properties against

§ 45(b) 51 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

owners who know that the apparent use or intended use is a zoning violation and who sell without disclosing that fact, and in further view of interpretations of "first refusal" rights as options, either conditional or absolute (see pages 43-4 of this supplement), it is probable that leases containing rights of first refusal will be held to be subject to the provisions of the Act. 

The following form of statement in the agreement of sale is suggested:

Seller states that the zoning classification of said property is C-1 Residential (or whatever classification it may be) under which the legal use is (here insert the uses permitted under the stated classification), and that the present use of the property is in compliance with (or in violation of, if such be the case) the zoning laws and ordinances. 

§ 45. Meaning of the Various Parts. 

(b) Description:- 

PAGE 83 

A receipt reading "March 30, 1945, Received of Anna 

Suchan Fifty ($50.00) dollars first payment on my farm the balance $3950 (sgd.) Ray Swope" was held sufficient to satisfy the Statute of Frauds in Suchan v. Swope, 357 

Pa. 16-1947. See opinion of Mr. Justice Stern in that case for review of other cases in which meager descriptions were held sufficient. See also Sawert v. Lunt, 360 Pa. 

521-1948 and Cheney v. Carver, 370 Pa. 543-1952 for discussions of descriptions in agreements. PAGE 84 A description incomplete in itself but referring to a recorded deed by reference to the record may thus incorporate into the agreement an incumbrance recited in the deed (Heppenstall v. O'Donnell, 165 Pa. 434-1895), unless the description in the agreement is sufficiently identifying without examining the deed reference (Geyer v. 

Walton, 151 Pa. Super. Ct. 549–1943).

§ 45(c) 

(c) Terms and Conditions. 

PAGE 84 Original text in stating remedies available to vendor for breach of agreement does not mention specific performance. The vendor has such a remedy also, but such remedy must be brought within five years after the contract was made because of the Act of April 22, 1856, P.L. 

532, § 6, 12 P.S. 83 (Lamb v. Allegheny County Institution 

District, 363 Pa. 66, 70-1949).

For less ancient cases holding that on default of the vendee, the vendor may resell and sue the vendee for any loss on the resale provided the terms are as favorable to the purchaser on the resale as they were to the original vendee, see Ellis v. Farber, 382 Pa. 499—1955, Pepper v. 

Deakyne (No. 1), 212 Pa. 181-1905 and Bowser v. Cessna, 62 Pa. 148-1869.

PAGE 85 

Where a vendee notifies the vendor of his refusal to perform, the vendor is not required to make tender of deed and such vendee cannot recover the down money even in absence of a liquidated damage clause (Messinger v. Lee, 163 Pa. Super. Ct. 297-1948 (citing Ladner); 

Sunseri v. Mancuso, 362 Pa. 161-1949). For a case construing liquidated damage clause quoted in original text see Tudesco v. Wilson, 163 Pa. Super. Ct. 352-1948. As to what down payments might be considered disproportionate or too unconscionable to be upheld as damages under a liquidated damage clause see Ellis v. Roberts, 98 Pa. Super. Ct. 49-1929, in which 15% of the gross purchase price was held unconscionable, and Kraft v. 

Michael, 166 Pa. Super. Ct. 57-1950, in which 10% was held not unconscionable.

PAGE 85 New. Liquidated damage clause held not to restrict remedy thereto or bar specific performance unless the

§ 45(c)53 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

language of the part of agreement in question, or of the entire agreement, together with the relevant evidence, if any, shows a contrary intent (Roth et al. v. Hartl, 365 

Pa. 428-1950).

PAGE 86 New § 45 (c) 2. Vendee's or Purchaser's Remedy. 

When the vendor or seller defaults the general rule is a vendee is entitled to recover as damages for the loss of his bargain if his contract is in writing and the vendor in bad faith refuses to convey (Frey v. Nakles, 380 Pa. 

616-1955). Otherwise he is limited to recovery of the down money and necessary expenses incurred and required in such transactions (Polka v. May, 383 Pa. 801955; Kargiatly v. Provident Trust Co., 338 Pa. 3581940). And inability of the vendor to make title is not bad faith (Bitner v. Brough, 11 Pa. 127-1849; Craven 

Estate, 169 Pa. Super. Ct. 94-1951). But a refusal to convey because the vendor could not make a profit on the transaction was held to be sufficient evidence of bad faith (Seidlek v. Bradley, 293 Pa. 379-1928); as also is demand by the vendor for a greater consideration than that agreed upon (Delvitto v. Schiavo, 370 Pa. 299—1952).

Of course, there is also the right of the vendee to take such title as the vendor has and compel such conveyance by specific performance with possibly a reduction in purchase price (See Roth v. Hartl, 365 Pa. 428-1950; Merritz v. Circelli, 361 Pa. 239-1949; Bloshinski v. Falaz, 168 

Pa. Super. Ct. 565-1951 and p. 65 of this Supplement).

If the vendor is to take a purchase money mortgage from the vendee as a part of the consideration, it is advisable that the terms of the mortgage be set forth in reasonable detail as to amount, term, rate of interest, times of payment of interest (and principal, if payment is to be made in instalments), prepayment provisions, if any, and any special provisions agreed upon. Otherwise the agreement of sale may be wholly unenforceable for

§ 45(d) 

uncertainty (See Frick v. Ozias, 1 Phila. 177-1851, where an agreement of sale calling for a purchase money mortgage in a specified amount was held unenforceable for uncertainty because the term of the mortgage was not stated. See also Zimmerman v. Rhoads, 226 Pa. 174, 177-1910 stating the general rule that if any of the essential details are wanting, the court will not supply them in a suit for specific performance. But see also 

Detwiler v. Capone, 357 Pa. 495, 502-1947 holding that statement of the amount of the purchase money mortgage, without more, is sufficiently certain and definite.)

PAGE 90 (f). Fixture Clause. Window screens made especially for the property were held, as between vendor and vendee, to be fixtures (Batcheler v. Lally, 66 D. &. C. 25-1948).

A clause in the agreement of sale specifying what fixtures are included in the sale may be enforced even after delivery of the deed (Remmett v. Smith, No. 2, 61 D. & C. 635-1947).

New. Store Fixtures. When the subject matter of the agreement of sale is a store or other property occupied by a tenant engaged in trade it must be remembered that a tenant, unless otherwise specified in his lease, has the right to remove trade fixtures placed by him on the premises for carrying on his business, because these are chattels irrespective of their size, substantial construction or manner of annexation (First Nat. Bank of McAdoo v. 

Reese, 356 Pa. 175-1947; Berry v. Heinel Motors, Inc., 162 Pa. Super. Ct. 52-1948). (h) Apportionment of Taxes, etc. Interest and Rents.

PAGE 91 New. Note that sewer rents should be added to charges to be apportioned in Philadelphia and wherever such rents prevail. A clause reading "Taxes, water rent and sewer rent if any, are to be apportioned to date of settlement" was held to relate only to taxes, etc. for the current year, and not to unpaid penalties and interest which become part of delinquent taxes (Smelo v. Girard Trust Co., 158 

Pa. Super. Ct. 473-1946). And coal royalties accruing between the date of the agreement of sale and delivery of the deed, not being in the nature of interest or ordinary rent, belong to the purchaser and are not apportionable (Brier v. Ahlborn, 57 D. & C. 399-1946).

§ 45(i) 

(i) Kind of Title Agreed on, Time of Performance, etc. 

PAGE 93 

Correction in text:-The word "vendor" in the 22nd and 23rd lines of the original text, should read "vendee. 

The test of nonmarketability of title, viz., a title which exposes the holder to the hazard of a law suit, is the test before delivery of deed. After acceptance of the deed, the vendee has the burden of showing the title to be positively bad by proving a superior title in a third person who is asserting a right thereto by virtue of such title (Medusa 

Portland Cement Co. v. Lamantina, 353 Pa. 53, 591945). However, the grantee might recover upon a general warranty, if any, in the deed (Kamel's Estate, 42 D. & C. 

130, 134, 135-1941).

Where an agreement provided that if the vendor fail to deliver a good and sufficient deed by a stated date the down payment should be restored, it was held that this did not give the vendor an option to rescind the agreement, but that it remained enforceable by the vendee subsequent to the specified date (Bradish v. Hahn, 59 D. & C. 4371947).

Performance. Time of the Essence Clause. 

PAGE 94 Where no time is specified for settlement in an agreement of sale a reasonable time is implied (L.C.S. Colliery, Inc. v. Globe Coal Co., 369 Pa. 1-1951; Glover v. Grubbs, 367 Pa. 257-1951; Marshall Construction Co. v. Forsyth, 359 Pa. 8-1948). And even a specified time is not of the essence unless expressly so made (Svenson v. Rohrer, 206 Pa. 407-1903).

Extension of Time for Performance. 

PAGE 95 Whether an agreement of sale declaring the time within which settlement must be made, "to be the essence of

§ 45(i) 57 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

this agreement, unless extended by mutual consent in writing endorsed hereon" can be extended orally or by waiver is still not clearly settled. Four recent cases must be mentioned: Cohn v. Weiss, 356 Pa. 78-1947 rules that though the agreement makes time of settlement "of the essence, "' nevertheless the time of settlement could be waived either by agreement or conduct of parties. 

However, the agreement of sale there construed did not include the clause that time of settlement could only be extended "by agreement in writing endorsed thereon." 

Moreover, the Court mentioned a further reason for decreeing specific performance, viz., that the vendor never complied with request of the vendee to execute affidavits to remove certain objections appearing on the settlement certificate, thereby being himself in default. In Williams v. Barbaretta, 359 Pa. 488-1948 the clause requiring extensions of time to be made by "agreement in writing endorsed on" the contract of sale was included with the provision making time of the essence, but the vendor himself asked a postponement of settlement because he could not find a house to move into. Vendee agreed to postponement but no definite time was fixed. Later, when the vendee fixed a time for settlement, the vendor refused to appear. No point seems to have been made of the fact that the extension was not made in writing, and the case turned on whether the wife, with whom title was held by entirety, was bound by her husband's request for postponement. The Court held that she was as much the beneficiary of the postponement as her husband, and having accepted the benefits in the shape of continued occupancy of the property, she was estopped. In DiGuiseppe v. DiGuiseppe, 373 Pa. 556-1953, and in Warner Company v. MacMullen, 381 Pa. 22-1955, it was held that the provision that a specified time for settlement was of the essence could be extended by oral agreement or waived by conduct of the parties. Again, however, it does not appear in the reports whether the agreement expressly required an extension of time to be in writing; and if it did, the

§ 45-A 

question of its effect upon an alleged oral extension or waiver by conduct was neither mentioned nor decided by the Court. Since the question of validity of oral agreements of extensions was not specifically passed upon by the Court, Wasserman v. Steinman, 304 Pa. 150—1931 (cited in the original text) may not be disregarded, especially as that case was not overruled or apparently brought to the attention of the Court in the cases just discussed. Consequently, in absence of estoppel, attorneys and agents cannot rely with confidence on oral agreements of extension but should see that they are in writing if the sales agreement so requires (see Schar v. 

Maier, 355 Pa. 153—1946). 

§ 45-A. Fiduciary Agreements of Sale. 

PAGE 95 The rule that an agreement of sale of a fiduciary subject to supervision of the Orphans' Court must be regarded as conditioned on the fiduciary receiving no higher offer before settlement and delivery of deed, has been modified by the Act of May 24, 1945, P.L. 944, 20 P.S. 

818, which provides that when a fiduciary enters into a contract not requiring approval of court, or when the court approves one requiring its approval, neither inadequacy of consideration, nor receipt of an offer to deal on other terms shall, except as otherwise agreed by all parties, relieve the fiduciary from performing or constitute ground for any court to set aside or refuse to enforce it except for fraud, accident or mistake. However, the liability of the fiduciary for surcharge on the ground of negligence or bad faith remains unaffected.

This Act was held constitutional in Brereton Est., 355 

Pa. 45-1946. And in Van Voorhis Est., 355 Pa. 821946, was held applicable to estates of decedents dying before its enactment, where the sale is subsequent to the Act.

Until the court's approval is obtained, however, the

§ 45-A 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

1 Act of 1945 (supra) does not apply and the fiduciary is bound to report to the court, at the time he presents his petition or moves for approval, any higher offers received (cf. McKees Rocks Borough etc., Kozell Appeal, 360 Pa. 285, 288-1948). The practice prevailing in the

Orphans' Court of Philadelphia County is for the attorney for the fiduciary to notify the buyer, whose agreement is subject to the approval of the court, and the bidders of the increased offers, to be present in Court, whereupon he states the facts to the court. If the increased offers are substantial and not merely a nominal increase, the court directs one of its clerks to take the parties into another room where the clerk conducts, so to speak, an auction. When the respective parties are through bidding the original purchaser, because he is first in right and therefore first in equity, is given the opportunity to take the property at the highest bid made. If he refuses 

1 the sale is then made to the highest bidder and the decree is accordingly signed by the court. An official stenographer is in attendance at the bidding and reports the same. This practice has worked admirably in Philadelphia in that it fully protects the fiduciary, assures the highest price to the beneficiaries, and gives the original purchaser the first right to buy at the proper price.

The Act of 1945 (supra) operates to protect a purchaser in two instances: (1) where the sale is under a power, and (2) where the Court has approved the sale. § 545 of the new Fiduciaries Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 

512, 20 P.S. 320-545, seems to leave the Act of 1945 unaffected.

More or less pertinent decisions since the original text was published are now cited here because the Act of 1945 does not apply where the agreement specifies otherwise (Levin v. Fidelity-Phila. Trust Co., 358 Pa. 1241948; Stone Est., 358 Pa. 335, affg. 59 D. & C. 37-1948), or where there has been fraud (Reichert's Est., 356 Pa. 

269-1947, affg. 56 D. & C. 1; Heilig Bros. Co., Inc. v. 

Kohler, 366 Pa. 72-1950). Herbert Est., 356 Pa. 107-

§ 45-A 

1947 held power of court to restrain the sale or require acceptance of a better offer applies to sales at public auction as well as private sales. Kane v. Girard Trust 

Co., 351 Pa. 191-1945, holds that a higher offer must be accepted up until the deed is executed by all of a number of trustees and delivered, and that depositing the purchase money and stating settlement figures is not sufficient unless the fiduciary is arbitrarily tardy. Levin v. Fidelity-Phila. Trust Co., 358 Pa. 124-1948 follows the

Kane case just cited, where the right to accept a higher offer was reserved in the agreement of sale in language similar to that appearing on page 96 of original text, but the court, while saying there was no evidence of the "fiduciaries' arbitrary tardiness," did not define or further explain what the phrase meant. It would seem that if the fiduciary deliberately delays settlement or delivery of the deed so as to angle for further offers, or wilfully delays for an unreasonable period of time, that would be arbitrary tardiness. After the court approved a sale to the highest bidder, a reservation in the agreement of sale which followed, is unenforceable because it is without consideration and is nudum pactum (Commonwealth Trust Co. Mtg. Investment Fund case, 357 Pa. 349-1947). 

Fiduciaries to whom the rule does not apply. It has been held the rule that a fiduciary must, even after a contract sale is executed, accept a higher offer, does not apply to sales by the Secretary of Banking acting as Receiver (In Re Central Trust & Savings Co., 41 D. & C. 

304-1941; In Re Shenandoah Trust Co., 43 D. & C. 662 —1941; Freeman's Petition, 51 D. & C. 323—1944); Liquidating Trustees of a Building and Loan Assn. (Jinks v. 

Geo. S. Hensel B. & L. Assn., 47 D. & C. 145—1943);

County Commissioners (Washington County Commis sioners Petition, 58 D. & C. 338-1946 (construing the 

Act of 1945, supra, as applying to all fiduciaries), affirmed on appeal, Nom. Superior Mining Co. Property Tax Sale, 

Hecla Coal & Coke Co. Appeal, 359 Pa. 357-1948).

Whether the rule applies to a corporate trustee for bondholders selling under a foreclosure was not decided in 

Spingarn v. Fidelity-Phila. Trust Co., 49 D. & C. 3831943, because the right to accept a higher offer was specifically reserved in agreement of sale. See footnote of 

Judge Sloane in that case at p. 387 for an excellent summary of cases.

§ 46. Execution of Agreement of Sale; Signatures by 

Agent.

PAGE 97 New. Sales and conveyances of real estate by agents acting under a power of attorney are regulated by the Act of 1705, 1 Sm. L. 69, 21 P.S. 301, which provides in § 4 (21 P.S. 304), inter alia, that such powers shall be "deemed in force until the attorney or agent shall have notice of a countermand, revocation, or death of the constituent." This has been held to protect a bona fide pur- 

7 chaser without notice of revocation of the agent's power (Morgan v. Stell, 5 Binn. 305-1812). For form of Power of Attorney for Service Men drafted by John R. Umsted, Esq., see article entitled "Powers of Attorney for Men in

Service" in Legal Intelligencer of August 22, 1950 (vol. cxxiii, pp. 169-172).

PAGE 97 Where a written contract of employment of the agent contained a provision reading, "with power for me, and in my name to sell and execute contracts of sale," of employer's described property at a stated price, it was held sufficient written authority to authorize execution of an agreement of sale by the agent so as to bind the owner,

§ 46-A  

even though no other express authority was contained in the employment contract as to other terms such as date of settlement, etc. (Nuzum v. Spriggs et al., 357 Pa. 5311947).

As to liability of an agent who signs an agreement of sale for an undisclosed principal, see § 199, page 530, of original text, and page 264 of this supplement. 

§ 46-A. Execution Where Vendors are Married. 

PAGE 97 For discussion of recent cases and acts of assembly relating to a married woman's right to convey without joinder of her husband see ante § 34, 34-A, pages 31 to 35 of this supplement.

PAGE 98 

An agreement signed by the husband alone to sell real property owned by entireties cannot be specifically enforced (Peterson v. Chandler, 362 Pa. 102-1949; Polka v. May, 383 Pa. 80—1955).

Where a spouse executes an agreement of sale before marriage and thereafter marries, the new marital partner can be compelled by action of specific performance to join in the deed (Detwiler v. Capone, 357 Pa. 495—1947, where a husband contracted while single, and Lapida v. 

Goodman, 61 D. & C. 323—1947, where the wife contracted while single).

New. Execution of Agreement of Sale on Sunday. 

PAGE 98 

An agreement of sale, though executed on Sunday, was held enforceable despite the prohibition in the Sunday 

Laws of the State Penal Code (Act of June 24, 1939, 

P.L. 872, § 699, 18 P.S. 4699.4) against worldly employment or business (works of necessity and charity excepted). It was held valid as a work of necessity in view of the housing shortage (Chadwick v. Stokes, 162 Fed. 

2nd 132-1947).

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

§ 48. Legal Effect of Agreement of Sale. 

PAGE 100 

A contract to buy real estate signed by husband and wife creates in them an equitable estate by entireties, and where the husband died before settlement, his estate was bound to pay his share of the purchase price for the property, to which his surviving spouse had the right to take title in her own name (Long Est., 65 D. & C. 95-1948).

Although the purchaser must pay interest on the unpaid balance of the purchase price when the settlement is unavoidably delayed and he has taken possession (Leafgreen v. Drake's Executors, 300 Pa. 369-1930, cited in original text; Witmer v. Delone, 225 Pa. 452-1909; 

Hershey's Estate, 213 Pa. 601-1906), note that where a vendor had refused to perform and vendee had deposited the purchase money with the title company, the vendor was held not entitled to interest when specific performance was decreed (Candy v. Kean, 159 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 596-1946; Kreider v. Brubaker, 371 Pa. 279—1952).

Where the premises sold are destroyed by fire after the date of the agreement of sale but before delivery of the deed, as between the insured and the insurer, the insured remains the owner of the property until the sale is completed, but as between the vendor and vendee, the vendor holds only legal title and if he receives the proceeds of the insurance policy on his property he holds these as trustee for the buyer (Dubin Paper Co. v. Ins. 

Co. of North America, 361 Pa. 68-1949).

PAGE 100 

Neither husband nor wife acquire any interest in real estate of the respective spouse as to which an agreement of sale was executed by the spouse before marriage (Detwiler v. Capone, 357 Pa. 495-1947; Lapida v. Goodman, 61 D. & C. 323-1947; Ladley v. Ladley, 85 D. & C. 200-1953).

§ 49(a) 

PAGE 101 New. Where a vendor makes an agreement of sale or grants an option exercised by the optionee and the vendor has no legal title at the time but acquires such title thereafter, the vendee becomes entitled to enforce the agreement. This may be called acquisition of title by legal estoppel (Phillips v. Tetzner, 357 Pa. 43—1947, citing 

Ladner). For further discussion of this principle see § 

90A of original text.

New. Realty Transfer Taxes. As pointed out on page 

49 of this supplement, the existence of the State Realty Transfer Tax under the Act of December 27, 1951, P.L. 

1742 as amended, 72 P.S. 3283 et seq., as well as somewhat similar taxes imposed by numerous local municipalities, makes it important to include in the agreement of sale a provision to define the liability for such taxes as between the vendor and the vendee. Without such a provision, the vendor will be liable (as between vendor and vendee) for the State tax stamps on the deed where he contracts to give a "good and sufficient deed," for otherwise his deed will not be "good and sufficient" since it cannot be recorded nor received in evidence (and thus is unusable) without the proper State tax stamps. Furthermore, § 4 of the Act requires the stamps to be affixed by the grantor (Stefanko v. Reed, 86 D. & C. 145—1953).

The agreement of sale itself does not require State realty transfer tax stamps. They are to be affixed to the deed, and not to the agreement of sale (Smith v. Messner, 372 Pa. 60-1953). 

§ 49. Extinction of Agreement of Sale.

(c) By Vendee for False Representations. Correction:

§ 49 (c) 65 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

Citation of Ohlbaum v. Mayer in original text should read 

285 Pa. 260. To justify rescission of an agreement of sale, a misrepresentation must be material (Brown v. Buckner, 69 D. & C. 629-1949; but if it is material and is relied upon, rescission is justified (LaCourse v. Kiesel, 366 Pa. 385-1951, discussing also the materiality of misrepresentations and the parol evidence rule as applied to oral evidence of misrepresentation as affecting a written agreement of sale). See also Abrams, Inc. v. Wolkov, 371 Pa. 44-1952, holding that a vendee had no right to rescind where the vendor represented that each apartment had two baths whereas in fact each had only one, but the agreement of sale stated that the vendee had made and relied upon his own inspection and not upon any representation of the vendor. That mere physical appearance of the property might, in some instances, operate as a misrepresentation by the vendor, justifying rescission by the vendee, is indicated in Woldow v. Dever, 374 Pa. 

370-1953. Note that while a principal is liable in assumpsit for return of the hand money because of his agent's misrepresentation even though unauthorized, he is not liable in trespass for deceit unless he authorized or participated in the representation (Littler v. Dunbar, 166 Pa. Super. Ct. 271-1950). However, in that case, on appeal, the Supreme Court sustained the verdict because it merely awarded the plaintiff the down money with interest and the cause of action is amendable at any stage of the proceedings (id. 365 Pa. 277-1950). 

New. Specific Performance with Allowance of Compen sation.

Where a vendor is unable to completely perform his agreement, the vendee, instead of rescinding, may elect to have specific performance with proper abatement of the purchase price (Roth v. Hartl, 365 Pa. 428, 4331950). However, this right to have the purchase price reduced does not exist in all cases, but is limited to where there is a defect in vendor's title, or a deficiency in quantity of land conveyed. Where there is merely a claim

§ 50 

based upon a representation collateral to the contract (e.g. that there was a sewer in an abutting street) the only remedy available to the vendee is rescission or recovery of damages at law (See excellent opinion of Mr. 

Justice Stern, reviewing applicable principles, in Merritz v. Circelli, 361 Pa. 239-1949; see also page 53 of this supplement).

Suggestion #1. Revise to read: 

Specify when and how possession is to be given. For example: (a) by delivery of deed at settlement (where property is vacant and without buildings). (b) by delivery of deed and keys at settlement (where property includes a vacant building). (c) by delivery of deed and assignment of existing lease at settlement (where property leased and occupied). 

Suggestion #3. Add "sewer rents (if any)" to payments to be apportioned. Many municipalities now impose such rents for sewer service. See also page 55 of this supplement (§ 45 (h)).

Suggestion #7. Add to citations: Dubin Paper Co. v. Ins. 

Co. of North America, 361 Pa. 68-1949 holding that the vendee may require the vendor to account for the proceeds collected from insurance where a fire occurred after the agreement of sale was executed but before settlement and delivery of the deed. See § 45 (d), pages 86-87 of original text as to perpetual policies. PAGE 107 Suggestion #8. It should be noted that most commercial and many typewritten forms of agreement of sale expressly provide that the agreement shall not be recorded. 

This is for the benefit of the vendor who, if the vendee should default, would find himself in difficulty with the cloud of the recorded agreement on his title. If he elects to retain the down payment as liquidated damages and terminates the agreement, he may be faced with the necessity of bringing an action against the vendee to remove the cloud in order to clear his title and be able to make good title to another purchaser or to create a good mortgage. (See also § 50-A (c), pages 110-111 of original text).

$ 50 

Suggestion #9. Note that an agreement to sell entireties property, signed by the husband alone, cannot be specifically enforced, even as against the husband alone. In such a case, the vendee is restricted to rescission or damages at law (Peterson v. Chandler, 362 Pa. 102-1949).

Hence, it is more important than ever to see that the agreement of sale in such a case is signed by both spouses (or under written authority of either non-signing spouse). 

Suggestion #11. In addition to Federal internal revenue tax stamps, State realty transfer tax stamps must now be affixed to the deed, and a realty transfer tax must also be paid, in many cases, to the local municipality in which the real estate conveyed is located. While the State 

Realty Transfer Tax Act of December 21, 1951, P.L. 

1742, as amended, 72 P.S. 3283 et seq., permits the Commonwealth to hold either the grantor or the grantee liable for the State tax, as between the parties themselves the grantor has been held responsible for the tax in the absence of agreement to the contrary (Stefanko v. Reed, 86 D. & C. 145-1953). By Section 8 of the amended Act 

(72 P.S. 3289), an unstamped deed cannot be recorded nor can it be received in evidence or made the basis for any legal action; and Section 9 (72 P.S. 3289) subjects both parties to criminal penalties for failure to have the proper State tax stamps affixed. Liability for the local municipality transfer tax depends upon the precise provisions of the particular ordinance. Thus, regardless of liability as between grantor and grantee, it is of great concern to the grantee to see that the proper State stamps are affixed to the deed and the proper local tax paid. 

Otherwise his deed will be virtually useless; and if the grantor refuses to pay these taxes, the grantee may be compelled to make a quick decision at the settlement, whether to refuse the deed and decline to settle under the ruling in Stefanko v. Reed, supra (in which case he runs the risk of being obliged to sue for the return of the down payment, etc., or defending a suit for specific perform-

§ 69 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

tance), or paying the taxes himself and suing the grantor to recover that amount for which the grantor, in the absence of express agreement, is liable. Hence, it is advisable, from the point of view of either party, to insert in the agreement of sale a provision expressly stating which t party shall be responsible for the payment of which of 

SE these taxes. See also pages 49-50, 64 of this supplement.

Suggestion #12. When representing the vendor it is advisable to insert in the clause regarding insurability of title a provision as follows: "Title to be such as will be insured by the Title Insurance Co. at regular rates" 

specifying the name of the title insurance company that had insured the vendor's title. If merely the phrase "any reputable title insurance company in Philadelphia" is used, it is tantamount to covenanting that all such title companies will insure, so that if the vendee can show that only one title company refuses to insure, even though all the rest would insure, the covenant of the kind of title agreed on would be breached (La Torre v. Edelsman, 22 D.R. 845-1913; Srolovitz v. Margulis, 35 Pa. Super.

Ct. 252-1908). Moreover, specifying a particular title company expedites the searches, speeds up the issuance of a settlement certificate and avoids duplication of affidavits or proofs already in the title company's possession by which objections that may have arisen when the vendor took title, were removed.

Suggestion #16. Where there is more than one executor of a decedent dying prior to January 1, 1950, or more than one trustee under an irrevocable deed of trust created prior to that date, all must execute an agreement of sale of real estate, else the agreement will be unenforceable (Simmons Est., 254 Pa. 231—1916; Rider v. Crisman, 61 York 76–1947 (Executors); Dorrance Est., 333 Pa. 

162-1939 (trustees)); unless, of course, the will or deed of trust provides otherwise (Foulkes Estate, 334 Pa. 186 -1939). As to decedents dying on or after January 1, 1950 and trusts created to become effective thereon or thereafter, or as to revocable trusts, the new Fiduciaries 

Act of 1949, P.L. 512, § 519, 20 P.S. 320-519, and § 949 of the same Act (20 P.S. 320–949) applies. By this Act a majority of the executors or trustees are empowered to act, and if, on disagreement, there is no majority, the Orphans' Court, on petition, may direct a sale or refuse to direct. 

Suggestion #17. Where an agreement of sale provides for the creation of a purchase money mortgage or that the property is to be conveyed subject to an existing mortgage, remember that such an agreement of sale cannot be assigned by the vendee without consent of the vendor, unless the agreement so provides. (See $ 48-A, page 101 of original text). A provision permitting assignment should be included if that is part of the bargain. 

Suggestion #18. If the agreement provides for a purchase money mortgage to be created by the vendee, the agreement should state as many of the essential terms of the mortgage as possible, viz., amount, term, rate of

§ 71 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

interest, times of payment of interest, amortization provisions (if any), prepayment rights (if any), and any special provisions agreed upon (see page 53 of this supplement). 

Suggestion #19. If the property being sold is situated in Philadelphia, and includes a building designed or intended for use by three or more families or a commercial establishment containing one or more dwelling units, be sure to insert a zoning statement by the vendor under the Act of July 27, 1955 (No. 104), 21 P.S. 611-615 (see page 50 of this supplement). 

Suggestion #20. Where the property to be sold is a vacant lot in a housing development or one of a group of lots laid out on a plan, the purchaser and his representative should be certain that the description in the agreement of sale accurately designates the correct lot. While a description merely by lot number on a specified and recorded plan is a sufficient description legally (see § 

45 (b), page 83 and § 54 (e), page 124 of original text), if the purchaser picks out one particular lot on physical inspection of the ground, but signs an agreement to purchase a lot designated only by number, which turns out to be a different lot from the one he had selected, he may be faced with expensive and time consuming litigation or at least delay, inconvenience, arguments with the seller and his agent, and probably additional expense, in order to correct the mistake. It is far better in the end, if there is any doubt, to require a metes and bounds description in the agreement, checked against an accurate survey, even if the purchaser is obliged to pay for the survey; and the lot selected by the purchaser should be physically pointed out to the surveyor, on the ground. The surveyor should stake the boundaries of the lot, and, if possible, the lines for the foundation of the building to be erected, and should indicate all such lines on his plan. The purchaser should inspect the property after the staking to see that the lot staked is the one he is buying, and the

§ 50-A  

building lines are where he expected them. Only then can it be certain that he is getting what he intended to buy, and that the building will not be so placed as to violate any zoning or building regulations or restrictions as to side yard and set-back requirements.

$50-B 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

New. § 50-B. Housing Development Form of Agreement of Sale.

In recent years a tremendous upsurge in small home building has occurred, especially in what may be called housing or building operation, developments. In such a 

C project, a large tract is purchased by a builder (or by someone who hires a builder). He then has it laid out in streets and lots by a surveyor or engineer, has plans and specifications for one or more types of houses prepared, and builds one or two sample houses. Through a real estate agent or realtor, he then sells the lots under a form of agreement which is not only an agreement of sale but also contains provisions for the construction of a house on the lot in accordance with one of the sample houses or one of the builder's sets of plans and specifications.

This type of transaction has become so common and widespread, and many of the provisions of the agreement ordinarily required of the buyer (on a more or less take it or leave it basis) are at such variance with and beyond those of the conventional agreement of sale that, although the subject of building contracts is outside the scope of this book, some of the more important features of these agreements require attention. 

At the outset it is to be observed that virtually all of these agreements, although varying in minor detail, are heavily weighted in favor of the seller and his agent. 

While there are many reasons for this, consideration of which is beyond the scope of this book, it behooves the purchaser and his attorney to read and understand the fine print, so that if (as is usually the case in a seller's market), the purchaser must accept the provisions without substantial change if he wants the house, at least he will be aware of the risks involved.

Title Provisions. Frequently these agreements provide that the title shall be such as will be insured at regular rates, either by a title insurance company named (by the seller) in the agreement or by one to be designated by the

§ 50-B 

seller. Such a provision requires only insurable title, which may or may not be good and marketable title (see § 45 (i), pages 92-93 of original text). It also permits the seller to select the title insurance company. If the company so named is satisfactory to the purchaser, further discussion is academic. Under the conventional agreement of sale, however, it is the buyer's prerogative to select the title insurance company. Since it will be his title, it is normally his right to decide whether he wishes it insured at all, and if so, by which company; and this is especially so in view of the fact that he is the one who pays for it. If it does make any important difference to the buyer, therefore, the seller should be persuaded, if possible, to amend the provision so as to permit the buyer to select the title insurance company, or to name in the agreement the one desired by the buyer. These comments are of corresponding application where the agreement provides for a title examination and opinion by an attorney in areas where that method, rather than title insurance, is employed.

The agreement usually provides that the title is to be free of all liens and encumbrances, but subject to some or all of existing restrictions, easements, agreements with public utilities, nuisances, signs, public or private sewers and restrictions imposed (or to be imposed) by the seller. 

Obviously, the purchaser should know (but usually does not), in advance of committing himself, exactly what these things are and how they affect the property he proposes to buy. They should be readily ascertainable from or through the seller or his agent, with a reference to the record of each and a verbatim copy of any restrictions not recorded but which the seller intends to impose in the deed. A copy of such new restrictions should be attached to the agreement with an appropriate provision in the agreement itself to the effect that they are the restrictions to be imposed by the seller. 

The agreement almost invariably provides that if the property may be subject to the filing of mechanics' liens

§ 50-B 

75 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

or municipal claims (which will probably be the case since there will be new construction), the buyer will accept and pay for special insurance against actual loss by reason of such liens or claims (see § 202, page 543 of original text). Some agreements provide that such special insurance shall be equal in amount to the first mortgage (which most buyers create). This is obviously intended as a minimum requirement rather than a fixed amount. As a practical matter, that amount will sometimes suffice; but technically, it may not. If a mechanic's lien should be filed, it would have priority over the mortgage (see § 184, page 460 of original text). Under many forms of mortgage, the mortgage is in default if the mortgagor does not promptly free the property from a prior lien, such as a mechanic's lien. Thus, the mortgagor (here, the purchaser) must get rid of the mechanic's lien in order to prevent action against him on his bond or mortgage. At that point, it is usually hopeless to try to compel or induce the seller to pay the holder of the mechanic's lien, for it was the seller's failure and probable inability to pay that brought about the lien. So the purchasermortgagor is forced to pay it for his own protection. He thereby suffers a loss by reason of a mechanic's lien. 

But it is his loss, and is no loss to the mortgagee. If, therefore, the special insurance against actual loss by reason of mechanic's liens was issued only in the amount of the mortgage (i.e., only for loss sustained by the mortgagee), the mortgagor, as the loss-sufferer, has no recourse to the insurance. It is, therefore, advisable for the purchaser, especially if he creates no mortgage but pays the full purchase price in cash, or if the mortgage is for a relatively small part of the purchase price, to take out a sufficient amount of such insurance to protect himself.

Financing. Under the conventional agreement of sale, the seller is interested only in getting the balance of the purchase price at settlement. It is immaterial to him where or how the purchaser obtains the money to be paid.

§ 50-B  

Consequently, the buyer makes his own arrangements for financing the purchase. If he needs a mortgage for that purpose, he obtains it himself, on terms and with a lender satisfactory to him, frequently, but not necessarily, through the real estate broker who made the sale to him. 

But neither the broker nor the seller have any right to place the purchaser's mortgage or his fire or other insurance, except by express request or agreement of the purchaser.

Substantially all of these housing operation agreements provide that the seller or his agent shall have broad and exclusive powers to place or obtain the mortgage, and frequently also, fire and other insurance, arrange the terms and make all the arrangements. The amount of the mortgage is usually specified in the agreement, but the rate of interest, term of the mortgage and other provisions of more or less importance are left to the seller or his agent to negotiate, all subject to the approval of the buyer. Many of the agreements provide that if the seller or his agent is unable to obtain the mortgage applied for or one satisfactory to the buyer, the latter will then be responsible for obtaining his own financing. Thus, although the purchaser authorizes the seller or his agent to arrange for and obtain a mortgage loan to the buyer, neither the seller nor his agent is obligated to do so. Consequently, the buyer, relying upon the seller to arrange the financing, might, on last minute notice from the seller of inability to obtain the mortgage, find himself without the necessary mortgage money on the eve of the settlement date, and unable to obtain it in time for himself or through other avenues. He would thus be obliged to default under his agreement, with the ensuing consequences (see $ 45 (c), pages 84-86 of original text and pages 52-3 of this supplement). While this is an unlikely occurrence in normal times (for the seller and his agent are ordinarily anxious to complete the transaction), it is not too remote a possibility in times of scarcity of mortgage money or a rapidly declining real estate market.

§ 50-B 

77 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

It is one of the risks the purchaser under such an agreement must take unless he can induce the seller to change these provisions in such a way as to either (a) permit cancellation of the agreement, with return of all sums deposited by the buyer (and reimbursement to him for all expenses for title insurance, drawing papers, etc.) in the event that the seller or his agent fails to obtain a mortgage satisfactory to the buyer, or (b) eliminate from the agreement all reference to financing, leaving it to the buyer to arrange for his own. In this connection, if the provisions discussed are eliminated, whether because the buyer is paying cash without a mortgage or because he is to obtain a mortgage elsewhere, another clause found in some agreements should also be eliminated; that is a clause requiring the buyer, in such case, to pay such sum (if any) as the seller may be obligated to pay his financing agent under a contract between those two parties (which contemplates that the financing agent will have the mortgages of the buyers of the houses and requires the seller to pay a fixed sum to the agent for each home sold on which the mortgage is not placed with the financing agent), and also to pay the amount of premium or commission which the seller or his agent would have received if he had placed the mortgage.

Construction. The agreement usually provides that the house shall be completed "substantially" similar to the sample house or "substantially" in accordance with the plans and specifications, which are declared to have been examined and approved by the buyer but are not attached to or made part of the agreement. The word "substantially" is subject to nebulous interpretation and application, and at best, opens the door to great liberties by the seller. Some agreements, making certain that the door is open wide, expressly provide that the seller may substitute other materials, fixtures, etc. of "substantially' equivalent quality. These provisions are so loosely drawn that the seller has great leeway, and there is little that the buyer can do about it if the seller takes full advantage 99

§ 50-B  

of them. This, again, is one of the risks a purchaser under this type of agreement must take. Probably the best that he can do is to insist upon plans and specifications regardless of any sample house (eliminating all reference to the latter in the agreement), have them checked and approved (or changed, if necessary) by his own architect, and have the construction itself spot checked by him as it progresses and when it is completed. 

Sometimes the agreement contains provisions for grading, driveway, seeding, landscaping, etc. If any such work is to be done as a part of the transaction, it should be clearly set forth in the agreement and coupled with a provision that the construction shall not be deemed completed until all of such work has been done. Too frequently, especially where the agreement is silent on the subject, the purchaser is called upon to make settlement when the house itself is "substantially" completed but the driveway is unpaved, or the ground is mere rubble and ungraded. In such a case the purchaser is compelled to rely upon the naked promise of the builder to complete the outside work later-which often turns out to be very much later and only after innumerable requests and urgings.

Many agreements provide that the seller is relieved of all responsibility for delay in completion, no matter how caused, and that the time for settlement shall be extended until completion. The buyer is thus bound indefinitely, even though the seller's delay is wholly arbitrary. Of course, the buyer has his remedies after a reasonable time (see § 45 (i), pages 93-4, of original text, § 48, pages 99-100 of original text, and pages 56, 63 of this supplement), but that is too indefinite as to time to be satisfactory. If possible, the buyer should require an "on or before" date for completion to be expressed in the agreement, with specific reasons, beyond the control of the seller, as the only permissible grounds for delay, and even then, for not more than a specified maximum period, after which the buyer should be given the right to rescind.

§ 50-B 

79 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

A so-called escalator clause is found in most of these agreements. By that clause, the buyer agrees to pay any additional cost to the seller resulting from any increase in labor or material prices between the date of the agreement and the date of settlement. If this is coupled with an option to the buyer to rescind the agreement and obtain a refund of his deposit and reimbursement for title and conveyancing expenses (if any) if he does not wish to pay the additional cost, the clause is not objectionable. 

Otherwise, it should be eliminated entirely or the option to the buyer above mentioned should be required. It is to be observed that there is no corresponding provision that the price to the buyer should be reduced if labor or material prices should decrease before settlement. In recent years, when labor and material costs have been on the rise, the omission has been of no importance; but no one can foretell when the trend will reverse, and the reduction clause might well be inserted when possible. 

Settlement and Possession. These agreements are usually so worded that (a) no fixed time for completion, settlement or possession is provided, but (b) when the seller decides that the work is completed (i.e., "substantially" in accordance with the sample house or the plans and specifications), the buyer is obliged to settle at the time and place designated by the seller, within five or ten days (depending on the terms of the agreement) after notice from the seller. Despite the fact that a varying number of items are still unfinished or defective (usually each one, by itself, is relatively minor, but cumulatively they are often impressive), the house can probably be construed as 'substantially" completed, and if so, the buyer is bound to settle. 

Now the agreement also provides that if the buyer makes settlement, accepts the deed or key, or takes possession, he thereby accepts entire completion and releases the seller from all further claim. He also agrees to give the seller at settlement an affidavit certifying that the property has been inspected and accepted by the buyer

§ 50-B  

and that they are entirely completed in every detail. The buyer is thus on the horns of a dilemma. If he makes settlement, gives the required affidavit, etc., he relieves the seller of all further responsibility, even though both of them know that there are a number of items, some small and some not so small (as, for instance, grading, driveway paving, etc. to which reference has been made above) which, in fact, have not been completed or are defective. Unfortunately, this is a far from unusual pattern. On the other hand, if the buyer refuses to settle until all of the work is completely and properly done, he must be reasonably certain that he can show that the uncompleted and defective work is substantial; otherwise he risks loss of his deposit as well as of the property. 

In most cases this risk is not justified, nor does the purchaser wish to litigate, with the attendant delay, expense and uncertainty. As a practical matter, in most cases, his best course is to: (a) As soon as he receives notice to make settlement -indeed, as soon as he has even unofficial information that the settlement date is near-inspect the property carefully, with his architect if he has one, and make a duplicate list of all incomplete and defective items. (b) Notify the seller or his agent at once, giving him one of the duplicate lists, and insist that every item be completed and defects remedied before settlement. (c) Inspect the property again as close to the time of settlement as possible, checking off on the list those items which have been completed and corrected. If any items are still unfinished or uncorrected, bring the list to the settlement. (d) Obtain from the seller, if possible, a written agreement, prepared by counsel, admitting non-completion or defectiveness of the items specified and agreeing to complete them (or remedy defective items) by a fixed date. 

The agreement should include provisions to have a stated amount of the proceeds of sale held by the title insurance company, to be paid over to the seller only upon comple-

§ 81 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

tion of the specified work within the time fixed; otherwise, to be paid to the buyer who may then have the work done at his own expense, if he wishes. Care should be exercised in drawing this agreement to provide expressly that the affidavit of the buyer as to completion (referred to above) shall not be required until the items specified have been completed, and that acceptance of a key or deed, making settlement or entry into possession shall not be acceptance of completion nor release the seller from further claim, etc.

CHAPTER V. 

INSTRUMENTS OF CONVEYANCING. DEEDS. 

I. 

DRAWING OF DEEDS. 

§ 52. Kinds of Deeds. Deed Poll. Indenture. 

PAGE 116 

Of the various modern forms of deed, the nearest to a true indenture is a voluntary deed of partition, which is always executed and delivered by more than one party. 

By such a deed each of two or more joint tenants or tenants in common conveys to the others his interest in all but one separate and specific piece of the entire property. The result is that each tenant becomes the sole owner of one separate and distinct piece of the property, instead of holding merely an undivided fractional or joint interest in the whole, so that the whole property is thus divided, or partitioned, among the former co-owners (see Mitchell, The Law of Real Estate and Conveyancing in Penna., pp. 410-11, and Fallon, The Law of Conveyancing in Penna., pp. 128-9). For form of voluntary deed in partition see § 243, page 618, of original text. A ground rent deed (see § 105, pages 293-298 of original text) is

§ 54(b) 

also close to a true indenture, being executed, in duplicate, by both parties. 

§ 54. Meaning of Parts. Premises: 

PAGE 120 

(b) Names of Parties. It is good conveyancing practice, and is customary, in drawing a deed to indicate after the name of an individual grantor his or her marital status, as, e.g., Andrew Black, grocer, unmarried (customarily used if the grantor is divorced), or singleman, or singlewoman, or widower, or widow, as the case may be. The name of a corporation grantor (or grantee) should be followed by "a corporation," and preferably also by indication of the State in which it is incorporated, in order to distinguish it from other concerns (e.g., a partnership, an individual doing business under a trade name, or another corporation of a different State) having a similar or identical name. (c) Consideration.

PAGE 121 Correction in text: In line 27 the citation of the Act of

PAGE 122 The words of grant under the Act of May 28, 1715, § 

6, 1 Sm. L. 94, 21 P.S. 8, were held as far back as the year 1809 not to amount to a general warranty but to import only a special warranty (Lessee of Gratz v. Ewalt, 2 Binn. 95-1809. See $ 55c of original text for meaning of terms general and special warranty). The same construction is placed on the words "grant and convey" used by the Act of April 1, 1909, P.L. 91, as amended by the Act of April 30, 1925, P.L. 404, 21 P.S. 2, 3 (Wood v. 

Evanitzsky, 168 Pa. Super. Ct. 484-1951, reversed on other grounds, 369 Pa. 123-1951).

§ 54(d) 83: 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

3 PAGE 123 As provided by the Acts under discussion, the words "grant bargain and sell" or "grant and convey" imply also the further covenant for quiet enjoyment, but as held in Lessees of Gratz v. Ewalt, 2 Binn. 95-1809, a breach of that covenant can be enforced only where it arises from acts done or suffered by the grantor, not by other persons having no legal right; and actual or constructive eviction is necessary before a breach can be claimed (Rawle on Covenants, $$ 97 and 137; Litmans v. O'Donnell, 173 Pa. Super. Ct. 571-1953). 

When used in a fiduciaries' deed, the words "grant, etc." imply no such personal undertaking, because a grantee in such a deed is entitled to no covenant, express or implied, except that the grantor fiduciary has done no act to encumber the title (Little v. Thropp, 245 Pa. 539 

1-1914; see also § 55d of original text).

Except when the grantee is a single individual or corporation, or two or more tenants in common in equal shares, the nature of the estate conveyed should be indicated immediately following the granting clause (changing the period after the word "assigns" to a comma).

Thus, if two or more grantees are to take as joint tenants, the words indicating this should be "as joint tenants with right of survivorship and not as tenants in common. "If they are to hold as tenants in common in unequal shares, the phrase should be "in the undivided portions or shares of one-half to A, his heirs and assigns, one-fourth to B, his heirs and assigns, . ." etc. Where the grantees are husband and wife as tenants by entirety, the words "as tenants by entirety." should be used.

Occasionally a conveyance is made to husband and wife and a third or other grantees. In such case, usually the husband and wife are to take as tenants by entirety (as between themselves), and they, together, are to hold as joint tenants or as tenants in common with the other grantee or grantees. Care must be exercised to use

§ 54(e) 

phraseology to correctly state the relationship intended. among the grantees (see page 11 of this supplement). 

Thus, the phraseology might be "as tenants by entirety as between A and B, his wife, and as joint tenants with right of survivorship and not as tenants in common as between them as such tenants by entirety, and C." If the conveyance is to a partnership, as partnership property, apt words are "as tenants in copartnership for the uses and purposes of the copartnership.' 

If only a life estate is intended, the words "his heirs and assigns" must be omitted, and the words "for the term of his natural life only" should be added. A life estate per autre vie should be indicated by adding, after "his heirs and assigns," the words "for the term of the natural life of X only." (e) Description.

PAGE 134 For a more recent decision on the applicability of restrictions imposed on some of the lots in a general development plan, to the other lots in the plan, see Hilsee v.

§ 54(g)3 (g) 3 Rules Governing Restrictions. 

PAGE 135 

A three family apartment house or use of one private residence by more than one family was held to violate a restriction that nothing other than private residences shall be erected or used in Pehlert v. Neff, 152 Pa. Super. Ct. 

84-1943. So also, a tourist home was held to violate a restriction against a use "other than as a private dwelling" (Thackray v. Crager, 43 D. & C. 301-1941).

In Bennett v. Lane Homes, Inc., 369 Pa. 509-1952, a restriction that "not more than 1 house, same to be detached or semi-detached, and private garage to be used in connection therewith, shall be erected on each lot" was held to be violated by construction of a three story, 34 unit apartment building on several adjoining lots all subject to the restriction. But in Kaufman v. Dishler, 380 

Pa. 63-1955, a similar restriction in identical words was held not to be violated by a two story, three unit apartment building, similar in size and outward appearance to other houses in the same block. This was differentiated from the Bennett case (supra) on the ground that the apartment building in the Bennett case was so large, both in size and in number of units, that it could not be considered a "house", whereas a two story, three unit building such as in the Kaufman case is a "house". The difference in the buildings in these two cases was so great that if size and appearance is the correct criterion, as the Court seems to hold in the Kaufman case, there is no difficulty in reconciling the two decisions; but it remains to be judicially determined at what point in size, appearance and number of units an apartment building ceases to be a "house" within the meaning of such a restriction. 

Construction of a street through a lot which is subject to a restriction that except for a detached single-family dwelling house to be used for private residential purposes, "no dwelling house or any other structure shall be erected" has been held to be the erection of an "other

§ 54(h) 89 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

structure" and violates the restriction (Crayder v. Seidman, 87 D. & C. 118-1953).

PAGE 136 The trend toward holding that restrictions not limited as to time may nevertheless become obsolete, was maintained in Peoples-Pittsburgh Trust Co. v. McKinley 

Gregg Automobile Co., 353 Pa. 110-1945, and in LaRue - v. Weiser, 378 Pa. 438-1954.
