New. Restrictions Against Race. Enforceability of re strictions against ownership or occupancy by a specified race is doubtful in Pennsylvania (Ellsworth v. Stewart, 9 Erie 305-1928). Yoshida v. Gelbert Imp. Co., 58 D. &

C. 321-1946, held such a restriction unenforceable as against public policy. Elsewhere authorities seem divided. See discussion of the question and collection of cases in 114 A.L.R. 1227, 66 A.L.R. 531 and 3 A.L.R. 2d series, 441. In the last citation will be found the U.S. 

Supreme Court case, Shelley v. Kraemer, 334 U.S. 1, 92 

L.Ed. 1161-1948, reversing the Supreme Court of State of Missouri (355 Mo. 814) and ruling that the 14th amendment of the U. S. Constitution prohibits the enforcement of a restriction against occupancy of the property by any person not of the Caucasian race. The question pointed out in the A.L.R. annotation at page 473, whether the ruling in the Kraemer case would prevent an action for damages against vendor based on violation of such a restriction in a deed has since been answered in the affirmative in Barrows v. Jackson, 346 U.S. 249, 97 1 L.Ed. 1586-1953. (h) Appurtenance Clause. Easements. Exceptions and

(h) 2. Easements, How Created. Exceptions and Reser vations.

PAGE 140 

A reservation or exception to the grantor of all minerals operates as severance of mineral rights or as a horizontal division of the land. Where there is such a severance from the title to the surface, the owner of the mineral rights is obligated to support the superincumbent estates. 

This right to support is not merely an easement but an absolute proprietary right necessarily arising out of ownership of the surface unless the right has been released by express words or necessary implication (Commonwealth v. Fisher, 364 Pa. 422-1950; Commonwealth v. Fitzmartin, 376 Pa. 390—1954).

PAGE 141 (b) 

Where a right of way is expressly granted and its precise location and limits are not fixed by the deed, it is competent for the parties to define the location and determine the limits by subsequent agreement, use and acquiescence (Taylor v. Heffner, 359 Pa. 157-1948).

PAGE 142 (b) 

New. A grantee of a right of way has no right to erect fences along the sides of the right of the way unless the instrument granting it expressly gives such right (Engel v. 

Cuddeback, 59 D. & C. 18-1947). A right of way appurtenant to a particular lot of land cannot be lawfully used

Pa. Super. Ct. 212-1949).

A grant of an easement which read "to open a road or lane twelve feet (12) wide to give entrance [for the grantee] to get from the public Road to land heretofore described forever" was construed to include the right of the grantee (a church) to have the right of way used by automobiles in funeral corteges to the cemetery established on part of the land conveyed to grantee (Garan et al., Trustees, v. Bender, 357 Pa. 487-1947). 

Where a deed reserved a right of way for a driveway "as now used", it was held that words "as now used" referred to the location of the easement and not the manner of its use (Taylor v. Heffner, 359 Pa. 157-1948).

PAGE 142 

New. The case of Allen v. Scheib, 257 Pa. 6-1917, cited in the original text, has been modified by Dowgiel v. Reid, 359 Pa. 448-1948, which holds that proper application of the rule (that an easement is strictly limited to the purposes intended by the parties between whom it was created) puts the limitation upon the purpose to be served by a right of way, and not upon the character of the traffic. Hence it was held that where a private road or cartway was established by partition for the benefit of a defendant's real estate to which it was the only means of access, and the right of way was without limitation or restriction, the defendant had the right to erect poles along the road on which might be strung wires for transmission of electric current to defendant's premises. But where the grant of an easement is expressed to be for a specific or limited use, it is restricted to the use specified (Nallin-Jennings Park Co. v. Sterling, 364 Pa. 6111950). (h) 3 Easements by Separate Deed of Grant. 

PAGE 142 A swinging gate across a private alley, equipped with padlock, key to which was offered to the owner of the dominant tenement, was held not to be an unreasonable interference with the easement (Haig Corporation v. Thomas 

Gassner Co. Inc., 163 Pa. Super. Ct. 611—1949. See also 

Davis v. Winsor, 165 Pa. Super. Ct. 212-1949). (h) 4 Easements by Prescription or Adverse User.

PAGE 143 New. An easement to light and air cannot be acquired by prescription (Maioriello v. Arlotta, 364 Pa. 557-1950; 

LaRue v. Weiser, 378 Pa. 438, 440-1954). In DiGirolamo v. Philadelphia Gun Club, 371 Pa. 40-1952, it was held that an easement to shoot over another's land could not be acquired by prescription or adverse user, because the trespasser could not show that the use was visible and of such nature and frequency as to give reasonable notice to the land owner that the right was claimed against him. 

Under the Act of April 25, 1850, P.L. 569, § 21, 68 P.S. 

411, a right of way through an uninclosed woodland cannot be acquired by prescription; and where a right of way claimed by prescription passes through both open fields and uninclosed woodland, it stands or falls as a whole; and since it cannot stand as to the woodland, it falls entirely, and the owner of the fields cannot be enjoined from preventing entry through his fields (Trexler v. Lutz, 180 Pa. Super. Ct. 24—1955).

The doctrine that permissive use, no matter how long continued, does not give rise to a presumption of grant, was applied to right claimed by prescription in a private lake (Shinn v. Rosenberger, 347 Pa. 504-1943), and to a claim of right to use a drainage system (Pittsburgh &

§ 54(h)93 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

Lake Erie RR Co. v. Stowe Township, 374 Pa. 54-1953).

But use of an easement whenever the party claiming it saw fit to use it without asking leave, and without objection was held to be sufficiently adverse (Mather-Klock 

Inc. v. Plymire, 349 Pa. 194—1944).

On the question of notorious use, see Cohen v. Williams et ux, 60 D. & C. 221-1947, holding that an underground sewer, of which there was no notice though used for over 21 years, will not be deemed adverse, the burden 1 being on one seeking to establish a non apparent easement to show that its original use was adverse. But a rainspout which is open to the air and the operation of which is continuous and notorious in every rain can become an easement if used adversely for 21 years (De- 

Stefano v. Meglio, 64 D. & C. 599—1948). (h) 5 Easements by Implication and Ways of Necessity.

PAGE 144 New. An easement by implication cannot arise if the owner of both tenements sells them to different vendees at the same time, for where both properties pass out of the vendor at the same time it is impossible to imply that he is making one servient to the other (Maioriello v. 

Arlotta, 364 Pa. 557-1950).

Where an easement consisting of the right to drain kitchen wastes into a cesspool was raised by implication, the use was limited to that at the time of severance of the tenements, and such use could not be extended by the addition of other types of household drainage (Snyder v. Bryson, 71 D. & C. 463-1950).

An implied easement on severance of the unity of ownership of an estate arises when (1) there is a separation of the title to two or more parts of the real estate, (2) there is such obvious and continuous user before the separation as to show an intent to make the alleged easement permanent, (3) the easement must be reasonably necessary to the beneficial enjoyment of the dominant tenement, and (4) the servitude must be continuous and self-

§ 54(h)5 

acting (Spaeder v. Tabak, 170 Pa. Super. Ct. 392-1952).

The same case holds that once the creation of an easement has been established, the burden is on him who so avers to show its extinguishment, and that the payment of rental for use of a driveway which had been effectively shown to be an implied easement does not establish its relinquishment.

For a more recent case on the general rule that when an owner subjects a part of his land to an open, visible, permanent and continuous servitude in favor of another part, a purchaser of one part takes subject to or with the benefit of the easement, see Tosh v. Witts, 381 Pa. 2551955.

A subterranean drain, not being apparent, does not raise the implication of an easement upon severance of the servient from dominant tenement which would bind a subsequent purchaser without notice (Cavany v. Sherer, 60 D. & C. 462-1947); nor does an underground sewer 

(Fidelity-Phila. Trust Co. v. Wengert, 67 D. & C. 3511948). But a valve box plainly apparent was held to be sufficient notice of an underground water main in an unopened street (Reading Co. v. Maguire, 82 D. & C., 599-1952). So, also, a telephone cable, partly underground and partly above ground was held sufficient notice of an implied easement to bind the grantee of the servient tenement (Bajor v. Bell Telephone Co. of Penna., 86 D. & C. 159-1953). On the point of permanence of location of an easement to be implied, see Phila. Steel Abrasive Co. v. Gedicke Sons, 343 Pa. 524-1942, which holds that a railroad siding was sufficiently permanent to raise the implication of an easement, and overrules Nauman v. 

Treen Box Co., 280 Pa. 97-1924 on this point (cited on pages 87 and 88 of original text). 

On the point of implied dedication, where the grantor designates an unopened street as a boundary of land conveyed, see Fidelity-Philadelphia Trust Co. v. Forster, 346 Pa. 59-1943, and Dulany v. Bishoff, 165 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 207-1949.

(h) 6 Extinguishment of Easements.

PAGE 145 (I) By Release. On the point that an easement is not lost by mere nonuser, see also Haig Corporation v. 

Thomas S. Gassner Co. Inc., 163 Pa. Super. Ct. 611-1949; 

Dulaney v. Bishoff, 165 Pa. Super. Ct. 207-1949; Grucinski v. Tetlak, 173 Pa. Super. Ct. 158-1953. 

(II) By Operation of Law. Denial of Right for Twenty one Years. While it has been said that an easement may be lost by "abandonment", actually such an extinguishment arises by reason of adverse possession, for it requires not only nonuser, but also an absolute denial of the right, followed by an enjoyment inconsistent with its existence for twenty-one years (Grucinski v. Tetlak, 173 

Pa. Super. Ct. 158-1953).

Merger. An owner in fee cannot have an easement over his own property. So, where a right of way was reconveyed to the owner of the servient tenement, it merges into the fee and is extinguished (Obringer v. Minnotte 

Bros. Co., 352 Pa. 188-1945). But a merger will not effect an extinguishment where there is an intervening or outstanding interest, or title, held by some third person in or to the easement (Wedge v. Schrock, 146 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 425-1941).

PAGE 146 Reasonable, rather than absolute, necessity is required for an easement by implication (Spaeder v. Tabak, 170 

Pa. Super. Ct. 392, 396-1952).

New. (IV) Extinguishment by Foreclosure of Mortgage 

Antedating Easement. Where an easement is created after a mortgage was executed on the servient tenement, foreclosure of the mortgage will extinguish the easement on the principle that the security of a mortgage cannot be impaired by an easement subsequently imposed on the mortgaged land without consent of the mortgagee (Vanderwerff v. Consumers Gas Co., 166 Pa. Super. Ct. 358-

§ 55 

1950). And a purchaser at the foreclosure sale takes free of the easement because his title relates back to the date of the mortgage (Dexter v. Pennsylvania Power Co., 127

Pa. Super. Ct. 419-1937, and Vanderwerff v. Consumers 

Gas Co., supra).

§ 63. Delivery of Deed. 

PAGE 161 

Although title passes only upon delivery of the deed (Kane v. Girard Trust Co., 351 Pa. 191, 197-1945), physical delivery is not always necessary to the passage of title (City Stores Co. v. Philadelphia, 376 Pa. 4821954; Kanawell v. Miller, 262 Pa. 9-1918); nor is recording of the deed required for such purpose (Mower v. Mower, 367 Pa. 325—1951; Malamed v. Sedelsky, 367 

Pa. 353, 358—1951). But recording a deed in the lifetime of both parties, of itself, raises a presumption of delivery (Herr v. Bard, 355 Pa. 578-1947; Stiegelmann v. Ackman, 351 Pa. 592–1945). Whether a deed has or has not been delivered depends upon the intention of the grantor as shown by his words and actions and by the circumstances surrounding the transaction (City Stores Co. v. 

Philadelphia, supra; Mower v. Mower, supra).

For a later case on presumption of delivery from acknowledgment, etc. see Cray Estate, 353 Pa. 25-1945. 

§ 63-A. Acceptance. 

PAGE 163 The presumption of acceptance, which arises from the fact that a deed has been recorded, may be rebutted by showing that the recording was without the knowledge or consent of the grantee (Piper v. Queeney, 282 Pa. 1351925).

§ 64. Delivery in Escrow.

Correction in text: In the third line of this section, "same" should read "some".

PAGE 164 

Delivery direct to the grantee will not operate as an escrow (Medusa Portland Cement Co. v. Lamantina, 353 

Pa. 53-1945; Weisenberger v. Huebner, 264 Pa. 316, 320-1919). For definition of an escrow and distinction between an escrow and an escrow agreement, see Angelcyk v. Angelcyk, 367 Pa. 381-1951, holding further that the rule that a written agreement is deemed to contain all of the terms agreed to (i.e., a written agreement cannot be varied by parol evidence-see § 43 (b), pages 79-80 of original text and page 49 of this supplement), does not apply to escrow agreements which may be partly written and partly oral.

New. § 65-A. Material Alterations in Deed. 

Material unauthorized alterations made in a deed after its delivery voids the deed entirely, so that it cannot be received in evidence to establish the grantee's title. It destroys only the instrument itself, however, not the title or estate of the grantee, since the title or estate is susceptible of proof by other means (Godding v. Swanson, 173 Pa. Super. Ct. 575-1953, citing Newman v. Cover, 300 

Pa. 267-1930). Ordinarily, a party producing an instrument which shows apparent alterations has the burden of explaining them, especially when they are to his benefit (Tosh v. Witts, 381 Pa. 255-1955).

By Whom Acknowledgments May Be Taken. § 2 provides that the acknowledgment of any instrument within the state may be made before: (1) A judge of a court of record; (2) A clerk, prothonotary or deputy prothonotary or deputy clerk of a court having a seal; (3) A recorder or deputy recorder of deeds; (4) a notary public; (5) A justice of the peace, magistrate or alderman. § 3. Outside of the state but within the United States or a territory, insular possession, the District of Columbia, or the Philippine Islands, an acknowledgment may be made before: (1) A clerk or deputy clerk of any federal court; (2) A clerk, prothonotary or deputy prothonotary or deputy clerk of any court of record of any state or other jurisdiction; (3) A notary public; (4) A recorder of deeds. § 4. Outside of the United States it may be taken by: (1) An ambassador, minister, charge d'affaires, counselor to or secretary of a legation, consul general, consul, viceconsul, commercial attache or consular agent of the United States accredited to the country where the acknowledgment is made; (2) A notary public of the country where the acknowledgment is made; (3) A judge or clerk of a court of record of the country where the acknowledgment is made. Requisites. § 5-6 cover the requisites of acknowledgment and provide that the officer taking the acknowledgment shall know or have satisfactory evidence that the person making it is the person described in and who executed the instrument, and that an acknowledgment of a married woman.

Title of Officer 

§ 8 requires the certificate of acknowledgment to be completed by the acknowledging officer's signature, his official seal, if he has one, the title of his office, and if a notary public, the date his commission expires.

Necessity of Authentication. 

By authentication is mean a certification of the fact that the acknowledging officer is the official that he claims to be. Under § 9 of the Act as amended: (1) An acknowledgment taken within the state, or if taken without the state by an officer of this state, or if made without the United States by an officer of the United States, no authentication is necessary; (2) If taken without the state but in United States territory, District of Columbia, insular possession, or the Philippine Islands, authentication is required as follows: (a) If taken by a clerk or deputy clerk of a court, by the presiding judge of the court, or (b) if taken by some other authorized officer, by the official having custody of the official record of the election, appointment, or commission of the officer taking such acknowledgment; (3) If made without the 

United States by a notary public or judge or clerk of a court of record of the country where made, the certificate must be authenticated by a certificate under the great seal of such country, affixed by the custodian of such seal, or by a certificate of a diplomatic, consular or commercial officer of the United States, accredited to that country. 

Acknowledgments Under Laws of Other States. 

§ 10 provides that any instrument acknowledged outside of this state in compliance with the manner and form prescribed by the law of the place of execution, if in a state, a territory or insular possession of the United

§ 105 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

States, or in the District of Columbia, or in the Philippine Islands, verified by the acknowledging officer's official seal and authenticated as indicated in § 9 (2) (supra), shall have the same effect as an acknowledgment in the manner and form prescribed by the laws of this state for instruments executed within this state (except a deed by a resident of this state to convey his homestead in this state and the deed is not additionally acknowledged in the form prescribed by the law of this state for validity of a conveyance of a homestead).

The exception in brackets has no application in Pennsylvania, which has no homestead provisions.

§ 71. Notary Public. How to Become One. 

PAGE 175 In 1949 there was enacted (effective September 1, 1949) a "Notary Public Law" being the Act of May 

18, 1949, P.L. 1440, 57 P.S. 121. It was virtually a codification of the existing law of Notaries Public as summarized in the original text, pages 175 to 179.

§ 109 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

By the new Notary Public Law of August 21, 1953, 

P.L. 1323, 57 P.S. 147-168, effective on that date, a number of changes were made in the prior law. 

Appointment. Under § 2 of the 1949 Act (57 P.S. 122), notaries public were appointed by the Governor, with the advice and consent of two-thirds of the Senators. 

Under § 2 of the new Act (57 P.S. 148), the appointments are made by the Secretary of the Commonwealth, and the advice and consent of two-thirds of the Senators are no longer required. However, the application for appointment must now bear the endorsement of the Senator of the district in which the applicant resides (or, if there is a vacancy in that district, then of the Senator of an adjacent district) (§ 5, 57 P.S. 151).

Eligibility. Under the § 3 of the 1949 Act (57 P.S. 123), the applicant must have resided within Pennsylvania for at least two years, and within the county for which he sought appointment (or an adjoining county) for at least one year. Under § 3 of the 1953 Act (57 P.S. 149), he must have resided within the State for at least two years and must be a registered voter in the Commonwealth. 

The county of his residence is now immaterial to appointment (see Jurisdiction, below). 

Application for Appointment. Under the earlier Act, § 5 (57 P.S. 125), application for appointment was made to the Governor. Now, under § 5 of the 1953 Act (57 P.S. 

151) it is made to the Secretary of the Commonwealth, who "shall satisfy himself that the applicant is of good moral character, and is familiar with the duties and responsibilities of a notary public." As noted above, under 

Appointment, the application must now be endorsed by the Senator of the district in which the applicant resides (or, if there is a vacancy in that district, by the Senator of an adjacent district).

Jurisdiction, Residence and Office. Under the 1949 Act, 

§§ 14, 15 (57 P.S. 134, 135), a notary public was required to reside within the county (or an adjoining county) in which he was commissioned to maintain his office. Now,

§ 71  

under the 1953 Act, § 2 (57 P.S. 148), his jurisdiction extends throughout the State (that is, he may perform notarial acts anywhere within the State), regardless of his place of residence (within the State), and there is no longer any requirement as to the county in which he resides, so long as he remains domiciled and maintains an office within the State. If he removes from the State, however, he thereby, ipso facto, vacates and resigns from his office as notary public (1953 Act, § 7, 57 P.S. 153). 

The same section requires that within five days after a change in his office address, a notary public must notify in writing the Secretary of the Commonwealth and the Recorder of Deeds (in Philadelphia, the Commissioner of 

Records) of such change. 

Seal. Formerly (1949 Act, § 16, 57 P.S. 136), the seal of the notary was required to include the location of the office of the notary. Under the 1953 Act, § 12, 57 P.S. 

158, the seal must contain the words "Notary Public, Commonwealth of Pennsylvania," and the name and surname of the notary.

Statement of Date of Expiration Commission. Under the 1949 Act, § 17, 57 P.S. 137, the notary was required to state in writing immediately below his signature, only the date of expiration of his commission. The location of his office appeared in his seal (see Seal, above). Now, however, since his seal no longer states the location of his office, the notary is required to state such location, as well as the date of expiration of his commission, immediately below his signature (1953 Act, §§ 13, 14, 57 P.S. 

159, 160).

Change of Name. Under the former law, when a female notary changed her name as a result of marriage or divorce, she could no longer act as a notary public under the commission she held, but was required to apply for a new commission in her new name. While no additional fee for the new commission was required, the applicant was obliged to take a new oath of office and to file a new bond in her new name (1949 Act, § 12, 57 P.S. 132). The

§ 

111 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

results accomplished by such requirements were obviously not worth the formalities and expense; and § 10 of the 1953 Act (57 P.S. 156) yields to the practicalities by providing that in such cases the notary may continue to act under her existing commission, and under the name in which it was issued, until the expiration of her term, provided only that within thirty days she notify the 

I Secretary of the Commonwealth and the appropriate

Recorder of Deeds, in writing, of the change of name, and that she make her application for reappointment (if and when she makes one) in the new name. The procedure thus indicated is also broadened to include any notary public, male or female, whose name is changed by decree of court. 

Fees. 26 of the 1949 Act (57 P.S. 146) specified the fees to be charged by notaries public for designated notarial acts. The 1953 Act, § 21, 57 P.S. 167, provides that notarial fees shall be fixed by the Secretary of the Commonwealth with the approval of the Attorney General. Accordingly, a notary public should obtain from the office of the Secretary of the Commonwealth before or promptly upon receipt of his commission a list of the notarial fees as currently fixed, and should request of that office advice when any changes are made or should make inquiry from time to time as to any such changes. 

There is nothing in the Act which requires the Secretary of the Commonwealth to notify notaries public of the notarial fees fixed or changed by him, or even to publish any list of such fees or changes. 

Limitation on Powers. Note that under both § 24 (a) of the 1949 Act (57 P.S. 144) and § 19 (a) of the 1953 Act (57 

P.S. 165), a notary public who is a stockholder in a bank, banking institution or trust company may now perform a notarial act for that bank, etc., provided he is not one of its officers or directors. 

In Notaries Public, 68 D. & C. 151-1949, the Department of Justice ruled that a person in the inactive reserve of the armed forces of the United States or of the fed-

§ 71-A  

erally recognized National Guard is not ineligible as being a holder of a federal office of trust or profit within the meaning of Article XII, § 2, of the Constitution. More recently, in Federal Employees as Notaries Public, 71 

D. & C. 365-1950 the Department ruled that whether a federal employee is disqualified from holding the office of Notary Public by Article XII, § 2 of the Pennsylvania 

Constitution depends on whether his duties as a federal officer interfere with his duties as a notary public. 

§ 71-A. Responsibility and Liability of Notaries Public and Their Sureties. 

PAGE 178 

A mortgagee is entitled to recover the amount paid by him on a forged mortgage where he relied on a notary's certificate of acknowledgment in which the notary falsely certified that the alleged mortgagor acknowledged the mortgage before him (Com. to use of 

Miller v. Doak et al., 352 Pa. 380-1945; Com. to use v. 

U. S. Fidelity & Guaranty Co., 364 Pa. 543-1950. See also Com. to use v. Maryland Casualty Co., 369 Pa. 300 -1952, and same v. same, 373 Pa. 602-1953). 

(a) Notary's Record or Register. 

PAGE 178 While the Notary Public Law of 1953 contains no direct provisions requiring the filing, in the office of the recorder of deeds, of his register and other public papers within any fixed time after termination of his office as notary public, § 8 of the Act (57 P.S. 154) provides that the notary's bond shall be conditioned (inter alia) "for the delivery of his register and all other public papers into the office of the recorder of deeds of the proper county in case of his death, resignation, disqualification, or removal." Under this provision it would seem that the register and other papers need be deposited with the recorder of deeds only within a reasonable time after

§ 72-A 

113 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

the death, disqualification, etc. of the notary. The penalty for failure to do so (within a specified time) imposed under the 1949 Act (§ 20 (c), 57 P.S. 140) has been eliminated in the 1953 Act which, by $ 23 (57 P.S. 147 note) repealed the 1949 Act in toto, and did not re-enact or supply such provisions. Consequently the only penalty which now appears to attach for failure to file the register and other papers within a reasonable time is through action on the notary's bond; and even that is of no avail unless a loss can be shown (Com. to use v. Maryland 

Casualty Co., 369 Pa. 300, 305-6-1952).

§ 72. Probate of Deeds Where Grantor Is Dead or Un able to Appear.

That the magistrate's certificate, and not the witness' signature to the affidavit, is the essential to probate of a deed where the grantor is dead and the deed was not acknowledged by him, was held in Dana v. Bank of the

New. 872-A. Realty Transfer Taxes. 

As indicated heretofore (see pages 49-50, 64 of this supplement), realty transfer taxes on conveyances are imposed by the State under the Act of December 27, 1951, P.L. 1742 as amended, 72 P.S. 3283 et seq., in the amount of one per cent of the value of the property. "Value" is defined as the amount of the actual consider-

§ 72-A  

ation, including liens, encumbrances and ground rents; and when the deed recites a nominal consideration, then "value" means the actual consideration or, in the case of a gift, "the actual monetary worth of the property, but in no event less than the amount of the highest assessment for local real estate tax purposes (for the current year). When a nominal consideration is recited in the deed, an affidavit by the grantor or grantee, stating the actual value of the property and the assessed value for real estate tax purposes for the current year, must be attached to the deed when recorded, and the proper 

State realty transfer tax stamps must be affixed to the deed and cancelled. Affidavit forms are obtainable at the office of the Recorder of Deeds. The Commonwealth customarily designates one or more of the banks in the larger cities as its agent for the sale of the tax stamps. 

It should be noted that under § 2 of the Act as amended (72 P.S. 3284) the tax does not apply to wills, mortgages, transfers between husband and wife, transfers between parent and child or the spouse of such child, and leases. 

Under the Sterling Act of August 5, 1932, P.L. 45, 53

P.S. 4613, municipalities were forbidden to tax anything taxed by the State. But $ 4 of the Act excluded Pittsburgh from this prohibition after June 1, 1935. The Act of June 25, 1947, P.L. 1145, as amended, 53 P.S. 2015.1, expressly permitted taxation by municipalities other than first class cities and first class school districts on the transfer of real estate situate within their respective boundaries. The State Realty Transfer Tax Act, by $ 

10 as amended (72 P.S. 3292), in effect removes the prohibition of the Sterling Act as to similar taxes by municipalities. As a result of these various statutory provisions (and § 8-407 of the Charter in the case of Philadelphia), many of the municipalities have imposed a 1% tax on the transfer of real estate situate within their respective boundaries.

The Philadelphia Documentary Tax Stamp Ordinance of September 7, 1937 imposed a tax on deeds delivered in

§ 72-A 115 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

a real estate transaction. In City Stores Co. v. Philadelphia, 376 Pa. 482-1954, this tax was held to be imposed upon the transaction. Under the Sterling Act, supra, such taxes could be imposed only on transactions, subjects, etc. within the limits of the city. Accordingly it was held in the City Stores Co. case that where the deed was executed and delivered and settlement made outside the city limits, it was not subject to the city documentary stamp tax, even though the real estate conveyed was within the city. The next year the legislature promptly amended the Act of 1947 by the Acts of June 28, 1955 (No. 60) and December 22, 1955 (No. 276), 53 P.S. 

2015.1, to provide that taxes on transfers of real property within its border might be imposed by any of the designated municipalities "regardless of where the instruments making the transfers are made, executed or delivered or where the actual settlements on such transfers take place."

The Philadelphia Documentary Tax Stamp ordinance was repealed December 31, 1952 and was replaced by the 

Philadelphia Realty Transfer Tax ordinance of Decem ber 9, 1952, effective December 31, 1952 and accomplishing substantially the same purpose. But the Supreme 

Court has held that the city ordinance of 1952 was the same in substance as the earlier ordinance, in that both ordinances imposed a tax on the transaction, and therefore neither ordinance had any application where the transaction (i.e., settlement and delivery of the deed) took place outside the city limits, even though the real estate itself lies within the city (Philadelphia Appeal, 383 Pa. 428-1956). In that decision it was noted that shortly after the decision in the City Stores case (supra), a city ordinance of December 7, 1954 (not applicable to the case before the Court because the transactions occurred prior to the ordinance), was passed expressly including in the term "deliver" the presentation of the deed for recording regardless of where it was executed, delivered or accepted. This, the Supreme Court

$ 73 

said (without deciding the point, however), brought the transaction of recording within the scope of the taxing provision, and apparently remedied the situation that the 1952 ordinance failed to reach. See also Dziak v. Novinger, 180 Pa. Super. Ct. 167-1956, reaching the same result with respect to a similar tax by a township school board.

CHAPTER VI. 

MORTGAGES. 

I. 

NATURE OF MORTGAGE. ANALYSIS. 

§ 73. Definition of Mortgage, Bond and Warrant. 

PAGE 184 Supporting the proposition that a bond and warrant, though usually accompanying a mortgage, is not necessary to its validity, see Hartje's Estate, 345 Pa. 5701942. And Steinhardt v. Russian Orthodox, etc. Society, 366 Pa. 222-1951 reiterates the rule that the lien of a judgment on a mortgage bond relates back to the date of recording of the mortgage, as to the mortgaged property. See also Keene Home v. Startzell, 235 Pa. 1101912, stating the rule. 

§ 75. Origin of Equity of Redemption of a Mortgage. 

PAGE 187 

Remedies in Pennsylvania. Foreclosure of a mortgage by sci. fa. under the Act of 1705 (1 Sm. L. 57, § 6, 21 P.S. 

791) is no longer permissible. Effective April 1, 1950, the Act of 1705 was suspended by the Pennsylvania Supreme Court Rule 1460 of Civil Procedure (adopted pursuant to authority conferred by the Act of June 21,

§ 77(1)(a)(1) 117 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

1937, P.L. 1982 (No. 392), § 1, 17 P.S. 61). The sci. fa. sur mortgage procedure under the Act of 1705 has been replaced since April 1, 1950 by "Action of Mortgage 

Foreclosure" under Rules of Civil Procedure 1141-1148, adopted by the Supreme Court. The procedure now is to file a complaint setting forth the necessary factual matters, including a full description of the mortgaged property, and to proceed in general as in assumpsit. 

§ 77. Form of Mortgage. Analysis of Principal Parts. 

PAGES 188-192 FORM OF SCI. FA. MORTGAGE. 

Because of the change in procedure and in the name of the action as noted above, present day mortgage forms eliminate all reference to "a writ of Scire Facias" (as in the third line from the bottom of page 189 and in the fifth line of page 192 of original text), and substitute the words "a Complaint or any other legal proceeding" or other words of like import. 

(1) Conveyance Part: (a) Premises :- 

PAGE 193 

Correction in text: The word "reduce" in line 6 of third paragraph on this page should read "produce." 

(1) Term of the Mortgage:- 

A mortgage of $2,500 payable "within five years from the date hereof in monthly installments of $25 each" was held to mean the property owner might nevertheless pay it off in full at any time within that period. It is obvious that the monthly instalments of $25 would not amount to $2500 in five years. Hence it was held that the $25 monthly instalment was a minimum which the mortgagor must pay, but not a maximum which the mortgagee could be required to accept (Blumenfeld v. Weisberg, 56 

D. & C. 519-1946). But a mortgage payable "within 

15 years in monthly instalments of $15.82" (including

§ 77(1)(a)(2) 

interest), where the total of the stipulated monthly payments equals the principal of the mortgage debt with interest to the expiration of the 15-year term, the monthly payments specified are both the minimum required to be paid and the maximum required to be accepted; and the mortgagor is not entitled to prepay the balance of principal (Hensel v. Cahill, 179 Pa. Super. Ct. 114-1955).

On the principle that where the mortgage fixes no time for payment of the principal sum, the debt is due immediately, the mortgagor in such a case can compel the mortgagee to accept payment of the principal (with interest to the date of payment) at any time (Diehl v. 

Council of Zion, etc., 162 Pa. Super. Ct. 167-1948); and by the same token, the mortgagor can compel payment in full at any time. PAGE 194 

(2) Production of Tax and Water Rent Receipts: 

Sewer rent receipts are now usually required to be produced also. The obligation of the registered owner to reimburse the mortgagee for taxes paid by him does not, however, render a trustee personally liable (Reilly Township School District v. Pardee, 362 Pa. 283-1949). The right to recover taxes which the property owner should have paid, arising as it does out of an equitable doctrine, is not limited merely to a mortgagee, but has been allowed to a ground rent owner, a co-tenant, a remainderman who paid taxes payable by a life tenant, a tenant who paid taxes owed by his landlord, and a judgment creditor who issued execution (First Nat. Bank of Ashley v. 

Reily, 165 Pa. Super. Ct. 168—1949).

All such actions must be commenced within six years from the time the taxes are paid (id.). 

(3) Insurance Clause:- 

PAGE 196 New. Except as limited by his agreement, an owner of real estate has the right to take out insurance, beyond

§ 119 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

the amount necessary for the protection of the mortgagee, in the name of the owner alone and for his protection. In such case the holder of the mortgage has no equitable lien or other claim upon the proceeds of the additional insurance (First Nat. Bank of Jessup v. Cap pellini, 149 Pa. Super. Ct. 14—1942). § 523 of the Act of May 17, 1921, P.L. 682, 40 P.S. 658 (as amended) was repealed and supplied by the Act of April 25, 1945, P.L. 307, as amended by the Act of July 

19, 1951, P.L. 1100, § 9, 40 P.S. 657, but results in no substantial change in the standard form of the mortgagee clause of a fire insurance policy. PAGE 196 

(4) Default Clause: Attorneys' Fees. 

Attorney's fees have been held not to be "costs of sale" within the meaning of the Act of May 22, 1895, P.L. 111, providing that the lien of taxes shall be divested by a judicial sale, if the amount of the purchase money shall equal the amount of taxes after payment of "costs of sale" (City of Williamsport v. Ulman, 42 D. & C. 4241941).

For other cases involving allowance, reduction or disallowance of attorney's fees or commission, see Lindley v. Ross, 137 Pa. 629-1890; Imler v. Imler, 94 Pa. 3721880; and Abel v. Howell, 84 D. & C. 69—1952. 

§ 78. Where Defeasance Is Not Annexed to the Convey ance.

PAGE 198 

It was held in Smyser's Estate, 53 D. & C. 387-1944, that where a parol defeasance was recognized by the heirs of a decedent grantee and title was conveyed to the alleged mortgagor pursuant to the oral agreement of their father, the Commonwealth had no standing to tax the real estate at its full face value, for inheritance tax purposes, because the Act of June 8, 1881, as amended

§ 80 

by the Act of April 23, 1909, P.L. 137 (21 P.S. 951), which requires defeasances to be in writing, was not complied with.

The Act does not apply to a parol agreement creating an express trust of the deed (Kalyvas v. Kalyvas, 371 Pa. 371-1952). 

§ 80. Form of Bond and Warrant of Attorney. Analysis thereof. 

PAGES 201-202 BOND AND WARRANT. 

Because of the change in the form of procedure in the foreclosure of mortgages, brought about by the Rules of Civil Procedure, foreclosure by Scire Facias is no longer possible (see pages 116-7 of this supplement). Hence the words "a writ of Scire Facias is properly issued" (as shown in the second line on page 201 and in line 26 on page 202 of the original text) are now replaced by "a Complaint or any other legal proceeding is properly filed," or words of similar import.

PAGE 203 There is no legal requirement that the bond and the warrant of attorney be separate and distinct instruments. 

In the interest of simplicity and reduction in space and verbosity, as well as to eliminate an extra document requiring an additional signature, the two documents are now frequently combined in a single instrument. 

§ 81-A. Waiver Clauses of Bond and Warrant. 

(a) Waiver of Exemption. When the bond and the warrant are distinct and separate documents, the practice in Philadelphia in entering judgment on the warrant is to tear the warrant apart from the bond and file the warrant alone. The waiver of exemption, inquisition, etc. is usually contained in the

§ 81-C(a)

bond, but not in the warrant, which merely recites the provisions of the bond, including the waivers. It has been held that the waiver is actually given by the bond and is effective as such, even though there is no waiver in the document filed. It is only necessary or advisable to call the waiver (in the bond) to the attention of the sheriff at the time of execution to meet the possibility of a claim to exemption by the debtor (Opportunity B. & L. Assn. v. Silverman, 38 D. & C. 575-1940).

PAGE 215 For distinction between the lien of a mortgage made before a lease and one made after a lease where the mortgage contains no clause conveying the rents, issues and profits, see Fogarty v. Shamokin & Mt. Carmel Transit 

Co., 367 Pa. 447-1951, holding that where the mortgage is made first, the rents are not subject to its lien, and the mortgagee can abrogate the lease only by proceeding in ejectment or by foreclosure, and he cannot obtain the rents by mere notice to the tenant; but where the lease is made first, the mortgage binds the reversion of the lessor (mortgagor), which includes future rents due the mortgagor, and upon default under the mortgage and notice to the tenant, the latter must pay to the mortgagor. 

New. A lease made by a mortgagee in possession as lessor becomes a nullity upon satisfaction of the mortgage and cannot be sued upon thereafter. The tenant becomes either a tenant at will, or the right of possession reverts to him under his former lease from the mortgagor, if it has not expired (Shanefield v. Magness, 82 

D. & C. 272-1952). (b) Application of Rents Seized by Mortgagee.

PAGE 216 A mortgagee in possession is not entitled to compensation for his own services in connection with the management of the mortgaged property and collection of rentals, unless equitable considerations otherwise require (Winthrop v. Binns, 160 Pa. Super. Ct. 214-1947).

§ 123 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

New. A mortgagee out of possession of unseated lands is not a quasi trustee of the owner, and if he purchases the mortgaged property at Treasurer's sale for unpaid taxes, he acquires good title free of any trust for the mortgagor (David Oil Co. v. Fogle, 354 Pa. 150-1946). 

But a mortgagee in possession is a quasi or constructive trustee, and can gain no title no matter how long the possession continues, unless and until, by some word or action, he repudiates the trust, followed by a lapse of more than 20 years. Nor can he acquire a title free of the mortgagor's right of redemption at a tax sale for taxes he could have but did not pay out of the rents (Elliott v. Moffett, 365 Pa. 247-1950).

§ 82. Individuals. Married Women. 

(a) Individuals in General.

PAGE 217 On the point that a married man cannot mortgage when there is a fraudulent intent to bar his wife's dower, see 

Cancilla v. Bondy, 353 Pa. 249-1945, which holds that while a married man may mortgage his property if the transaction is a bona fide and for a consideration, he cannot deprive his wife of her dower rights without her consent by creating a voluntary mortgage or judgment, since that would be equivalent to a pro tanto conveyance of real estate in fraud of her dower rights. But the wife must assert her claim where the mortgage is foreclosed, in the foreclosure proceedings, as it is too late after there has been a sheriff's sale and title has passed.

Nor is such mortgagee protected, as a taker of a legal title would be, against prior recordable equities of which he had notice (Kepler v. Kepler, 330 Pa. 441, 446 -1938).

III. 

KINDS OF MORTGAGES. 

§ 88. Purchase Money Mortgages. 

The priority of lien of a purchase money mortgage under the Act of April 27, 1927, P.L. 440, § 1, 21 P.S. 622, cited in the original text, has been continued without change by $ 2 of the Lien Priority Law of June 28, 1951, P.L. 927, 68 P.S. 602, viz., its lien dates from the time of its delivery to the mortgagee if it is recorded within thirty days after its date.

§ 127 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

PAGE 228 A mortgage given to a third party would not be a purchase money mortgage even though executed and delivered at the same time the deed was delivered unless it was given pursuant to assignment by the grantor of the right to receive it (Citizens B. & L. Ass'n v. Arvin, 207 Pa. 293-1904).

PAGE 229 

§ 2 of the Lien Priority Law of June 28, 1951, P.L. 927, 

68 P.S. 602, provides: "Liens against real property shall have priority over each other on the following basis: (1) Purchase money mortgages, from the time they are delivered to the mortgagee, if they are recorded within thirty days after their date; otherwise, from the time they are left for record;"

Subsection (a) of § 4 of the Act (68 P.S. 601, note) 

repeals § 1 of the Act of April 27, 1927, P.L. 440, 21 P.S. 

622 (cited in the original text) only insofar as it is inconsistent with and supplied by the 1951 Act. The 1927 

Act gave the same priority to a purchase money mortgage as does the 1951 Act. But in effect the 1927 Act defined a purchase money mortgage (for the purposes of priority of lien) as "any mortgage given by purchaser to seller, for any part of the purchase money of the land as mortgaged." The 1951 Act contains no definition of a purchase money mortgage. In that respect, therefore, the 1927 Act is neither inconsistent with nor supplied by the 1951 Act, and would seem to remain in full force. 

Consequently the question of assignability of the right to receive a purchase money mortgage is still open, and has not been resolved by the 1951 Act. 

§ 89. Advance Money Mortgages. 

PAGE 230 So-called "open end" mortgages are fundamentally advance money mortgages without limit as to the amount

§ 90-A  

of the total indebtedness at any one time. As pointed out in the original text, advances made under such a mortgage become effective liens (as to third parties) only from the times the advances are actually made, except to the extent that the mortgagee is legally obligated to make them (Housing Mortgage Corporation v. Allied 

Construction, Inc., 374 Pa. 312-1953), to which extent the liens for the advances relate back to the date of recording of the mortgage. Except in construction mortgages (where the mortgagee is legally obligated to make future specified advances), the usual instalment mortgage, if it does expressly permit future advances, ordinarily limits the amount of them so that the total principal indebtedness (including all such future advances actually made) shall not exceed the original principal amount of the mortgage debt. This is done, not because of any legal requirement, but as a matter of policy on the part of the lender who usually does not wish to encourage such additional future loans. In such mortgages, the lender is under no legal obligation to make any future advance. Hence if he does so, its lien under the mortgage is subject to any other lien against the mortgaged property entered or recorded before the advance is actually made (see original text). Consequently, in practice, a bring-down title search is made covering the period between the date of recording of the mortgage (when a title policy or search was presumably made) and the date of the advance, to be sure that there are no intervening liens. 

§ 90-A. Mortgage by Equitable Estoppel. 

PAGE 232 

For a later case on equitable estoppel, see Phillips v. Tetzner, 357 Pa. 43-1947.

PAGE 232 Correction in text. The words "a mortgage" at end of fourth line of first paragraph of this page should read "property."

1956 SUPPLEMENT.

§ 91. First Mortgages. 

§ 41-a of the Fiduciaries Act of 1917 as amended (cited in the original text as authorizing investment of trust funds in first mortgages under certain conditions) has been repealed and is supplied by § 7 of the Fiduciaries 

Investment Act of May 26, 1949, P.L. 1828, 20 P.S. 821.7, which makes a few relatively minor changes in the conditions required for such investments by fiduciaries. 

§ 92. Second Mortgages. Second Mortgage Clause. 

PAGE 234 For any who might object to stating that a second mortgage taken by a grantor for part of the purchase price of the conveyance is not a purchase money mortgage (when in fact it is), the second mortgage clause suggested in the original text may be modified by omitting from the second sentence the words: "that it is not a purchase money mortgage and". The important point of the clause is that of the two mortgages given at the same time, the one which is to be second in priority shall clearly so provide. So long as this is done, it is binding, at least as between the two mortgagees (Association v. 

McDonald, 5 Phila. 442-1864; see also Hendrickson's 

Appeal, 24 Pa. 383-1855); and for that purpose, whether or not one of the mortgages is a purchase money mortgage is academic. However, there may be advantages of such a purchase money mortgage as against others, which would undoubtedly be prejudiced by a declaration of record that it is not a purchase money mortgage. Thus, although an agreement to subordinate a purchase money mortgage to a later mortgage is binding as between the mortgagees, it does not subordinate the purchase money mortgage to mechanics liens which would be prior to the other mortgage but subject to the purchase money mort gage (Watson Land & Improvement Co. v. Salyers, 247 

Pa. 454-1915). If, in fact, a mortgage is a purchase

§ 93 

money mortgage, it will be so treated (except, of course, as to any express agreement otherwise), whether or not the mortgage shows or states on its face that it is one 

(Continental-Equitable Title & Trust Co. v. Conservation 

B. & L. Assn., 266 Pa. 298, 300-1920; Commonwealth

Title Insurance & Trust Co. v. Ellis, 192 Pa. 321-1899). 

§ 93. Building Association Mortgages. Building and Loan Associations. 

PAGE 236 

Extensive amendments to the Building and Loan Code of May 5, 1933, P.L. 457, have been made which should be consulted. They are gathered in 15 P.S. 1074 pocket part. PAGE 238 

Act of May 23, 1947, P.L. 272, 15 P.S. 1074-915, adds a new § 915 to the Building & Loan Code of 1933 which authorizes Building & Loan Associations to grant chattel mortgages on specified household appliances to mortgagors of real estate on which the Association holds a mortgage. Such chattel mortgages must be first liens on the chattels purchased and not more than 75% of the purchase price and shall not total more than $1,000 in connection with any one piece of real estate. 

§ 94. Building Association Mortgages (Continued). 

Kinds of Restrictions On.

PAGE 239 As pointed out in the original text (page 238), a share mortgage loan is paid only when the shares for which the borrower is paying by the month have matured. If, as frequently occurred during the "depression," the association failed, the shares never matured. Indeed, in some instances, they became worthless. As a result, the bond and mortgage, which were to have been paid by cancellation of matured shares, remained entirely unpaid; and the borrower remained liable for the full amount, despite

§ 94-A 

131 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

his monthly payments for several years. His payments, of course, were for the shares, and were not on the mortgage debt. When the shares became worthless, the unfortunate individual, as a shareholder, had lost his money, just as if he had bought ordinary stock on the stock market and the stock had become valueless. But as a borrower, his debt was still outstanding, untouched and fully owing. He may have thought that his monthly payments were reducing the mortgage debt, but actually they were not; they were paying for the now worthless stock.

To remedy this hard situation, direct reduction mortgage loans were introduced. In this type of loan, the borrower's monthly payments are applied directly to the mortgage debt principal and interest. The monthly payments are constant in amount (i.e., the same amount is paid each month). At first, the larger part of the payment is interest and the smaller part is in reduction of principal. As the principal of the debt is thus reduced, the interest portion of the monthly payments likewise decreases and more of the payment is applied to principal, until the entire principal of the debt has been paid. Under this type of loan, since the payments are applied directly to the mortgage debt, there is no danger that the borrower will lose the benefit of the payments he has made if the association should fail.

PAGE 241 

New. § 94-A. Federal Savings and Loan Associations and Mortgages.

In recent years, federal savings and loan associations, organized under § 5 of the Home Owners' Loan Act of 

1933 (Act of June 13, 1933, c. 64; 48 Stat. 132; 12 U.S.C.A. 

1464) as amended, have sprung up throughout the country. Receiving their charters from the Federal Home 

Loan Bank Board, these associations are intended "to provide local mutual thrift institutions in which people

§ 95 

may invest their funds and in order to provide for the financing of homes, giving primary consideration to the best practices of local mutual thrift and home-financing institutions in the United States." In a general way, mortgage loans of these associations follow the same pattern as those of State-chartered building and loan associations, but are restricted to loans on first mortgages upon homes or a combination of homes and business property within a fifty-mile radius of their home office, with not more than $35,000 loaned on any one such property, except that they may lend on first mortgages on other improved real estate, without regard to those restrictions, up to 15% of the association's assets. A federal savings and loan association is limited to direct reduction mortgage loans and straight mortgage loans, having no authority for share or split mortgages. The form of direct reduction bond and mortgage (and, of course, of straight bond and mortgage) is substantially the same for a federal savings and loan association as for a building and loan association. 

§ 95. Leasehold Mortgages. 

PAGE 241 § 8 of the Act of April 27, 1855, P.L. 368, 21 P.S. 338, cited in the original text, relating to the mortgaging of leaseholds, has been repealed, but is supplied by Art. II, 201 of the Landlord and Tenant Act of April 6, 1951, 

P.L. 69, 68 P.S. 250.204. 

§ 95-A. Extent of Coverage of Lien of Mortgage. Chattel Mortgages. Industrial Mortgages. 

(a) Chattel Mortgages.

PAGE 243 The Act of June 1, 1945, P.L. 1358, as amended by Act of May 23, 1947, P.L. 270 provided that chattel mortgages may be on chattels of any kind including but not limited

§ 95-A(b) 

133 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

to live stock, poultry, farm machinery, etc. Under that

Act, it has been held that a chattel mortgage on machinery and equipment, which is accepted by a mortgagee who knows they are used in and about an industrial plant, is subordinate to a prior real estate mortgage, even though the latter describes only the land with appurtenances, with no mention of buildings (First National Bank of Mt. 

Carmel v. Reichneder, 371 Pa. 463-1952).

The Act of 1945 (as amended) was repealed by $10-102 of the Uniform Commercial Code of April 6, 1953, P.L. 3, 12A P.S. 10-102; but chattel mortgages were reauthor ized by § 9-102 of the Code (12A P.S. 9-102), without limitation on the nature of the chattel. Because of this, other Acts cited in the original text, authorizing chattel mortgages on specified types of chattels, were also repealed by the Code.

For a case construing the Act of July 15, 1936 (First 

Session) P.L. 47, 21 P.S. 841, cited on this page of original text, see United States v. Kemmerer, 43 D. & C. 197-1941. 

(b) Industrial Mortgages. 

PAGE 245 

For a case holding that the lien of an industrial mortgage covers a later installed sprinkler system despite agreement between the owner and the sprinkler company that it should remain personal property, see Schnebbe 

Fire Protection Eng. Corp. v. Sandt Est., 365 Pa. 2871950. The lien of an industrial plant mortgage covers only such machinery, equipment and chattels as constitute essential parts of the plant for its manufacturing purposes, and does not cover chattels not required for that particular purpose, such as desks, typewriters, chairs, adding machines, filing cabinets and trucks (First National Bank of Mt. Carmel v. Reichneder, 371 Pa. 4631952).

§ 95-A(b)  

New. Effect of Uniform Commercial Code. 

§ 9-313 of the Uniform Commercial Code of April 6, 

1953, P.L. 3, 12A P.S. 9-913, virtually eliminates the priority of lien of industrial mortgages on chattels and fixtures essential to the going plant when the chattels or fixtures are subsequently installed and are subjected to a chattel mortgage, bailment lease or conditional sale (any of which is called by the Act a "security interest") before the chattels or fixtures are installed. Thus if, in the 

Schnebbe Fire Protection case, supra, a form of "security interest" had been created with respect to the sprinkler system before it was installed, and the transaction had occurred and the real estate mortgage created after July 1, 1954, the effective date of the Code, undoubtedly the decision would have been in favor of the sprinkler company instead of in favor of the real estate mortgagee. 

On the other hand, the decision in the First National Bank of Mt. Carmel case, supra, would be the same under the 

Code as it actually was before the Code, because there the chattel mortgage was created on chattels and fixtures already in the plant, i.e., the "security interest" did not attach before the chattels became a part of the real estate. 

Whether the lien of a plant mortgage, created before the Code, has priority on fixtures which are installed under a "security interest" created after the Code and before the fixtures are installed, presents an interesting question. 

It can be argued that the Code changes only the remedy of the real estate mortgagee, and that since the contractual right to be paid remains unimpaired, the Code may and does apply to industrial mortgages in existence on its effective date. It seems more likely, however, especially in view of the decisions in the 1934 and 1937 Deficiency Judgment cases (see § 100D, pages 269-274 of original text), that the Code changes will not be applicable to such mortgages because the changes, if applicable, would take away a substantive, contractual right to have the mortgage lien enhanced by attaching to after-in-

§ 97(b) 135 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

stalled fixtures, etc., and would thus be an unconstitutional attempt to impair the obligation of contracts. Be that as it may, the final answer to this question must, of course, await judicial determination.

IV. 

DISCHARGE OF MORTGAGES. 

§ 97. By Payment and Satisfaction of Record. 

PAGE 247 (a) Payment.

A mortgage payable in instalments within a stated I time can be paid off at any time where the total of the stipulated instalments of principal will not equal the principal of the mortgage debt within the stated time 

(Blumenfeld v. Weisberg, 56 D. & C. 519—1946). Where the stipulated instalments of principal do equal the mortgage principal if they are duly paid over the stated time, the stipulated instalments are both the minimum required to be paid and the maximum required to be accepted; and the mortgagee cannot be compelled to accept payments of any more than the stated instalments as they 

I become due (Hensel v. Cahill, 179 Pa. Super. Ct. 114- € 1955). (b) Satisfaction of Mortgage.

PAGE 248 The normal mechanics to satisfy a mortgage, as outlined in the original text, is authorized by § 9 of the Act of 

May 28, 1715, 1 Sm. L. 94, 21 P.S. 681.

§ 97(d) 

In Schuylkill Trust Co. v. Haupt et ux, 359 Pa. 3381948, it was held that while satisfaction of record is an important fact in proving payment, it is not necessarily conclusive where the mortgagee retained the bond. PAGE 249 New. Attention is called to the old Act of April 11, 1856, P.L. 304, § 1, 21 P.S. 685, which authorizes satisfaction of mortgages or judgments upon production of the instrument with payment endorsed thereon by the legal holder whose signature is witnessed by two witnesses, and upon certificate made by the President Judge or the district judge of the proper county allowing the same. 

The instruments are then filed with the Recorder of Deeds in case of mortgages or the Prothonotary in case of judgments. This Act is not obsolete and is still in use in some of the counties according to letter received by author from Jonathan Hendrie, Esq., of Sayre, Pa. See also Ollendike's Petition, 9 Dist. 95 (footnote, p. 98)— 

1900; Raub v. Pearson #1, 3 Lack. L.N. 324-1897.

PAGE 250 (d) Obligation to Satisfy.

Though to recover damages for failure to satisfy a mortgage it is not necessary to prove malice as stated in original text, yet where a verdict of $10,000 was rendered and there was neither a wanton nor malicious refusal but an honest belief that plaintiff was not entitled to have the mortgage satisfied and mortgagee wanted the issue tried by a court, it was held that an order awarding a new trial was proper because the trial court has the right to review the evidence for the purpose of determining whether it supports the penalty assessed (Werner v. Auto Finance Co., 347 Pa. 217-1943).

But where the mortgagee showed no justification for failing to satisfy the mortgage within three months, a verdict and judgment for the property owner equal to one-half of the mortgage principal was upheld (Specktor v. Specktor, 158 Pa. Super. Ct. 323-1945).

! § (d) 137 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

In Krevolin v. Williams, 160 Pa. Super. Ct. 411-1947, it was held that a writ of inquiry to assess damages may not be used after a default judgment in an action to recover damages for failure to satisfy a mortgage under the Act of 1715, cited in original text. 

PAGE 251 

Where an action was brought under § 10 of the Act of May 28, 1715, 1 Sm. L. 94, 21 P.S. 682 (cited in the original text) for penalties against a mortgagee for failure to satisfy of record a mortgage averred to have been given for money to be lent but which, in fact, was never lent, and the defendant's answer averred that it had never received the mortgage, that the mortgage had not been recorded by it, that not having the mortgage, the defendant could not produce it for satisfaction, and that it was willing to enter into an amicable action to have the mortgage satisfied of record, the matter was held not to be within the terms of the Act of 1715, which was intended as a remedy against and punishment to a recalcitrant mortgagee; and that the proper remedy was under the Rules of Civil Procedure (Rules 1061 et seq.) provid ing for amicable actions to quiet title (Lepore v. Italian 

Victory B. & L. Assn., 171 Pa. Super. Ct. 35—1952). 

Stumpf v. Cervino, 44 D. & C. 511-1942, holds that extra-territorial service may be had on a non-resident mortgagee in an action under Act of 1851 (cited in original text) to have the court determine the proper amount due on a mortgage which mortgagor desires to pay off.

§ 98. Discharge by Order of Court in Proceedings to 

Satisfy Mortgages Presumed Paid. Lost Mortgages, etc.

PAGE 251 

Construing the Act of June 20, 1883, P.L. 138, 21 P.S.

The procedural provisions of the various Acts cited on pages 251-252 of the original text for compelling or obtaining satisfaction of fully paid or discharged mortgages have been suspended and superseded, as of January 1, 1947, by Rules of Civil Procedure 1061 et seq., which require any such action to follow, in general, a form similar to that in assumpsit, and give the Court broad authority to enter a decree appropriate to the circumstances.

New. The Commonwealth's Mortgages are, by the Act of

Justice is authorized to satisfy the mortgage on application of the property owner. 

New. The Act of May 23, 1949, P.L. 1692, requiring mortgages, ground rents and charges against land to be satisfied by decree of court 50 years after the principal has become due or payable, and where no due date, then 50 years after creation regardless whether any interest has been paid thereon, has most properly been declared unconstitutional by Judge Smith in the cases of Girard

appeal in 364 Pa. 576-1950. The Supreme Court de clared the whole of the Act invalid. 

§ 99. Discharge of Mortgage by Release. 

PAGE 255 On the point of the effect of release of part of mortgaged premises without consent of the mortgagor after he has conveyed the premises, see Greater Adelphia B. & L. Ass'n v. Trilling, 323 Pa. 361–1936. In Beckman, Sec'y of Banking v. Wendell, 48 D. & C. 196—1943, a holder of a judgment entered on a bond secured by a mortgage released a portion of the mortgaged premises, which had been previously sold by the mortgagor to a terre tenant without mortgagor's consent. It was held that the mortgagor's personal liability was released, citing the Greater Adelphia case and stating the reason for the rule to be that the original mortgagor has a right to pay off the mortgage obligation and thereupon require assignment to him of the security held by the holder of the mortgage. When the mortgagee puts it beyond his power to fulfill his part of the implied contract to do so, by releasing a portion of the security, he forfeits his right to collect the original debt against the original debtor.

New. A spouse's or so called family exemption under the 

Fiduciaries Act of April 12, 1949, P.L. 512, § 211, 20 

P.S. 320.211, formerly known as widow's and children's exemption under the Fiduciaries Act of June 7, 1917, 1 P.L. 447, § 12 (a), 20 P.S. Ch. 3, App. 471, now increased to $750, takes priority under the new Act, as it did under the former Fiduciaries Act of 1917 (supra) and the older exemption under the Act of April 14, 1851, P.L. 612, § 5, = over every claim of decedent's creditors whether liens of record or not, and whether debts incurred in decedent's lifetime or not (Graves Est., 134 Pa. 377-1889; In re 

Koss Est., 59 D. & C. 308-1947; Dent's Est., 21 D. & C. 

535-1934), including funeral expenses (Weir's Estate, 10 Pa. C.C. 187, 28 W.N.C. 268-1891), mechanics liens (Hildebrand's Appeal, 39 Pa. 133-1861), specifically devised property (Stachnick Estate, 376 Pa. 592-1954), tax liens (Appeal of City of Allentown, 109 Pa. 75—1885), and even the Commonwealth's Inheritance Tax (Hildebrand's Estate, 262 Pa. 112-1918; Henderson v. Briggle, 11 D. & C. 581-1928).

But the exemption is subordinate to mortgages which were liens in decedent's life time because a mortgage is more than a lien, it is a formal pledge of the property made effective by a conditional conveyance (see § 73 of original text; Nerpel's Appeal, 91 Pa. 334-1879). It is also subordinate to a ground rent and arrears thereon though accruing after decedent's death (Pepper's Est., 1 Phila. 562-1855); and by express provisions of both the Fiduciaries Act of 1917, § 12, 20 P.S. Ch. 3, App. 472, as well as the new Fiduciaries Act of 1949, § 216 (a), 20 

P.S. 320.216, to liens for purchase money existing at decedent's death, whether mortgages or judgments. So, too, the exemption is subordinate to a lien which, though not ordinarily preferred to the exemption, is prior to a mortgage that would exhaust the exemption (Miller's 

Appeal, 122 Pa. 95-1888; Koss' Est., 59 D. & C. 308-

§ 100(f)  

1947). The exemption was also held to be subordinate to a debt due the United States in Cupp's Estate, 14 York 16-1900, but not to a Government claim for overpayment to a veteran (Jones Est., 84 Pa. Super. Ct. 170—1924). 

Under the new Fiduciaries Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 512, § 216 (b), 20 P.S. 320.216 (b), a decree setting apart a family exemption is void against a subsequent bona fide grantee of, or holder of a lien on, real estate unless the decree is recorded in the deed book in the office of the Recorder of Deeds in the county where the real estate lies within one year after decedent's death or before the subsequent grantee or lien holder's rights accrue. 

Property in Possession of a Receiver. 

A mortgagee should not foreclose a mortgage on a property of a corporation in the hands of a receiver without first obtaining leave of the court that appointed the receiver, but if he does foreclose without first obtaining such leave, the sheriff sale is not void where it appears leave would have been granted if applied for (Blank & 

Gottschall Inc. v. First Nat. Bank of Sunbury, 355 Pa. 

502-1947). 

(f) Sales by Order of Bankruptcy Court. 

PAGE 264 While the power of the Bankruptcy Court to order a sale of real estate free of all liens including mortgages, ought not to be exercised when it is apparent that the liens are greater than the value of the property, yet it is a matter of discretion, and if the court abuses its discretion its action is not void but merely erroneous, so that any objecting lienholder must seek to set the sale aside in the bankruptcy proceedings and not in the State court (Montgomery B. & L. Ass'n v. Coscia et al., 53 D. & C. 

491-1944, citing Re Miller, 95 Fed. 2d 441-1938).

V. 

DEFICIENCY JUDGMENTS. 

§§ 100-B-100-C-100-D. 

PAGE 265 to 274 

New. Deficiency Judgment Act of 1941. Constitutional. 

In response to insistent public demand the legislature of 1941 again sought to correct the inequitable deficiency judgment rule and Representative Skale, in collaboration with the author, drafted a bill which was later enacted by a unanimous vote of both houses and became the Act of 

July 16, 1941, P.L. 400, 12 P.S. 2621.1 et seq., and held

§ 100-B-C-D 

constitutional in Fidelity-Philadelphia Trust Co. v. Allen, 343 Pa. 428-1941, and Penna. Co. v. Scott, 346 Pa. 131942. 

The Act was made broader in its scope than its predecessors and, according to its title, was intended "to protect the debtors, obligors or guarantors of debts for which judgments are entered or may be entered, and owners of real property affected thereby, and others indirectly liable for payment thereof, by prescribing the method of fixing the fair market value of such property sold on execution, and limiting the amount collectible thereafter on such judgments. 

The Act provides that whenever any real property is sold on execution to the plaintiff, directly or indirectly, and the amount for which it is sold is insufficient to satisfy the debt, interest and costs, before the balance of the judgment could be collected plaintiff is required to petition the court to fix the fair market value of the property sold. §§ 2, 3, 4 and 5 set forth the procedure to be followed to have the court fix the fair market value. § 6 requires the court to ascertain the fair market value (unless the parties agree thereon), and provides that the debtor and any other person liable, directly or indirectly, shall be released and discharged of all liability to the extent of the fair market value of said property, less the amount of all prior liens, costs, taxes and municipal claims not discharged by the sale, and also less the amount of any such items paid at the distribution on the sale, thereupon the plaintiff might proceed to collect the balance of the debt so ascertained.

The Act provides for its application to execution sales made before as well as after the Act, and § 7 provides that in the former case the petition must be filed within six months after the effective date of the Act, and as to the latter class within six months after the sale; otherwise the debtor, obligor, guarantor and any person directly or indirectly liable shall be released. By 7a, added by the Act of May 27, 1943, P.L. 681, 12 P.S.

§ 100-B-C-D 

145 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

2621.7a, procedure is provided for having the record judgment satisfied, after the six months' period has passed and no petition to fix the fair market value is filed. § 10 prohibits and makes unenforceable any waiver of the benefits of the Act.

It will be observed that unlike the preceding deficiency judgment Acts, it was not restricted merely to mortgage foreclosures, but applies equally to all sheriff sales of real estate on any kind of a judgment. This was not only logical, but saved the Act from being regarded as special legislation (see Pennsylvania Co. v. Scott, 329 Pa. 5341938). Also, the earlier deficiency judgment Acts applied whether the plaintiff in the mortgage foreclosure proceedings actually bid in the mortgaged property or not, whereas the new Act carefully provides that it applies only where the plaintiff directly or indirectly purchases the property at the sheriff sale. 

The first case to test the constitutionality of the new act was Fidelity-Phila. Trust Co. v. Allen, 343 Pa. 4281941. There in a brief per cur. opinion (Justice Drew dissenting), without expressing an opinion on the constitutionality of the Act's application to sales on judgments in personam made prior to its effective date, it was held that, "on the ground that it is desirable to preserve the uniformity of construction of the contract clause of both state and federal constitutions" (which clause reads the same in each constitution; see original text, page 272), the construction of the United States Supreme Court in 

Gelfert v. National City Bank, 313 U.S. 221-1941, and cases there cited, was followed. 

Thus the Act was held constitutional so far as it applied to mortgage foreclosure proceedings which were the basis of the case there before the court.

This case was followed by Pennsylvania Co. v. Scott, 346 Pa. 13-1942, in which Mr. Justice Horace Stern, who wrote the Winowich case opinion (323 Pa. 483-1936) in a clear, concise opinion, held (three Justices dissenting) that the Act was constitutional in its application as well

§ 100-B-C-D 

to sales on judgments in personam as to sales on judgments in rem, and whether entered and made before or after the Act. Thus, at a long last, the inequitable deficiency judgment rule in Pennsylvania has been effectually abrogated. Should mortgagees so desire, they are still free to proceed against a debtor's personal property in the first instance (Evans v. Provident Trust Co., 319 

Pa. 50-1935), but once they proceed against the real estate and buy in the same, the Act applies. Nor does the Act bar presentation of a claim on the mortgage bond against the estate of one of the deceased obligors without first proceeding against the mortgaged real estate (Shapira Estate, 93 Pitts. 231-1945). However, such a claim is barred after foreclosure (Moreland's Estate, 91 Pitts. 453-1943). The Act applies to a sheriff sale of real estate on a judgment on a mechanic's lien, where the property is bid in by the lienor for less than the amount of the lien (Hoffman Lumber Co. v. Mitchell, 170 Pa. Super. Ct. 326-1952).

Certain other decisions construing the Act are worthy of note. In Ochiuto v. Prudential Ins. Co. of America, 356 

Pa. 382-1947, it was held that a judgment debtor who, prior to enactment of the Deficiency of Judgment Act of 

July 16, 1941, P.L. 400, 12 P.S. 2621.1 et seq., paid a portion of a deficiency judgment entered on a mortgage bond, cannot thereafter recover back from the judgment creditor such payment. In Sharps Est., 48 D. & C. 641 -1943, it was held that the Act operates in favor of a terre tenant who has given bond at the time of acquiring real estate "under and subject" to a mortgage. 

For a case construing the words "fair market value" and holding that an offer to purchase the mortgaged premises is not conclusive, but is evidence of such value, see Union Nat. Bank of Pittsburgh v. Crump, 349 Pa. 339-1944. 

In Freeman, Secy., etc. v. Greenberg, 351 Pa. 206—1945, it was held the Act cannot be pleaded by a debtor depositing a mortgage of a third person as collateral for a judgment on which the debtor had borrowed from a bank.

§ 147 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

Union Trust Co., Guardian v. Tutino, 353 Pa. 1451945, holds that the Act applies where a mortgage includes more than one tract of land and execution and sale was had of one tract only. 

Dearnley v. Survetnick, 360 Pa. 572-1949, holds that the proper court to fix the fair market value is the one where the sale was held, and the value must be fixed as of the time of the sale, even though the property was sold before the Act was enacted; and therefore the plaintiff is entitled to interest on only the balance due him after crediting the defendant as of the time of sale. 

Reading Trust Co. v. Campbell, 159 Pa. Super. Ct. 

197-1946, holds that a mortgagee who, after foreclosure and purchase by it at sale, fails to have the fair market value fixed under the Act, may not recover from the former owner taxes assessed against the premises while he was the owner and liquidated into the mortgagee's judgment on the sci. fa. See also, McGrath Est., 159 Pa.

Super. Ct. 78-1946, holding that the Act must be liberally construed, and bars an action for taxes by subrogation where the mortgagee failed to proceed under the Act. 

A claim on a mortgage bond, after foreclosure and purchase at the sale, will not be allowed in the Orphans' Court where there has been no proceeding to fix the deficiency judgment within the period allowed (Moreland's 

Estate, 91 P.L.J. 453-1943).

§ 119. Discharge of Ground Rent by Judicial Sale. 

PAGE 308 Whether the statutory authority (under the Act of 

May 16, 1923, P.L. 207, 53 P.S. 2051) of the court to decree a tax sale of real estate free of liens, encumbrances, etc., included ground rents, and if so, whether it could constitutionally apply to ground rents created before the Act, was open to some question. Such doubts have been set at rest, however, by Art Novelty Mfg. Co. v. Ken worthey, 370 Pa. 471-1952, in which the Supreme Court, in a per cur. opinion, affirmed the decision and opinion

§ 151 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

of Judge Hagan (C.P. No. 1, Phila. Co.), holding that such a tax sale so ordered could (and did) discharge a ground rent created before the Act of 1923. 

The Act of May 22, 1945, P.L. 844, 53 P.S. 2051.1, adds a new section (§ 31.1) to § 31 of the 1923 Act, applying to counties of the first class (i.e., Philadelphia County), and expressly includes ground rents among the liens, encumbrances, etc., clear of which the court may direct a sale.
