§ 124. Effect of Not Recording Instruments. 

PAGE 313 

A deed need not be recorded in order to pass title.

Delivery of the deed alone passes title. Recording is essential only for protection against or constructive notice to subsequent purchasers, mortgagees and judg ment creditors (Malamed v. Sedelsky, 367 Pa. 353-1951).

PAGE 314 

The procedure provided in § 2 of the Act of April 1, 1863, P.L. 188, 21 P.S. 353 (§ 1 of which is cited in the original text) for compelling a former owner to record prior deeds, etc., has been suspended by the Rules of 

Civil Procedure, and is now superseded by Rules 1061 et seq. 

§ 126. Time Within Which Mortgages Must Be Re corded.

PAGE 316 

The Act of April 27, 1929, P.L. 440, 21 P.S. 622 (cited in the original text), providing that mortgages shall have priority, as among themselves, according to their respective times of recording (except that a purchase money mortgage recorded within thirty days from its date shall have priority as of the time of its delivery), has been repealed by the Lien Priority Law of June 28, 1951, P.L. 

927, 68 P.S. 601 et seq., insofar as there are inconsistencies between the two Acts. However, the Lien Priority Law of 1951 (§ 2, 68 P.S. 602) makes no change in the priority of mortgages, including purchase money

§ 127(a) 153 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

mortgages, as among themselves; and by § 3 (68 P.S. 

603), the Recorder of Deeds is still required to endorse on each mortgage and defeasible deed the time when it is left for record. By the later Act, however, the Recorder of Deeds is required to endorse such time also on the record of the mortgage or defeasible deed. This was not required under the 1927 Act, and the additional requirement will enable one who examines the records to know which of two or more such documents, recorded the same day, has priority.

PAGE 317 Since an unrecorded mortgage, though not entitled to lien status as against creditors, subsequent mortgagors or purchasers, is nevertheless a good lien as against the mortgagor, it is likewise good against the heirs of a deceased mortgagor so far as the mortgaged real estate is concerned (Marks v. Morrison, 41 D. & C. 669-1941). 

As to priority between two or more purchase money mortgages executed and delivered on the same date, see also page 129 of this supplement.

PART IV. 

ACQUISITION OF TITLE BY DESCENT AND WILL. 

CHAPTER IX. 

TITLE BY DESCENT. 

PAGE 327 

Footnote: See lucid explanation of the common law definition of title by purchase in Hough v. Farmers Bank & Trust Co., 359 Pa. 555, in note to page 557-1948. I.

MODERN LAWS OF DESCENT. 

PAGES 329-346 New. Intestate Act of 1947. 

The Intestate Laws of Pennsylvania have been rewritten and partly revised by the new Intestate Act of April 

24, 1947, P.L. 80, 20 P.S. 1.1, effective January 1, 1948, and made applicable to real and personal estates of all persons dying on and after that date. In the main, the new Act is substantially the same as the Intestate Act of 1917 which it replaces. It is the work of the Joint 

State Government Commission, acting in conjunction with an advisory commission composed of experts on the subject of Decedents' Estates Law. By notes to various

§ 130  

sections of the Act, the Commissioners indicate that in order to achieve brevity and to simplify the language of the older act, much of the law has been rewritten. This, the author fears, will prove unfortunate because of the likelihood of promoting litigation (see for example Martin 

Estate, 365 Pa. 280-1950). Lawyers are prone to argue that changes in language show an intent to change the law. It is to be regretted that the learned Commissioners did not follow the wise precedent of the Commission that drafted the codification and revision of 1917 in retaining the language of the earlier Acts when no change was intended. The 1917 Commission said, "We have avoided making any change in phraseology of existing statutes unless some definite or substantial change in purpose of the law was intended, even where some other words or expressions might seem better adapted to express that purpose. Our obvious reason has been that the phraseology of the older statutes * have acquired through long use and judicial decision a settled determinate meaning which should not be disturbed through any desire to attain mere elegance of diction." * * The principal changes by the new Act will be here briefly summarized and for convenience, again noted in a reference to the sections of the original text of this book covering the old law where changes have been made. 

Summary of Principal Changes. 

The new Act increases the share of the surviving spouse from $5,000 to $10,000, plus one-half share of the balance of the estate if no issue (§ 2 (3)), and such spouses inherit the whole estate if the decedent was survived by no issue, parent, brother, sister, child of a brother or sister, grandparent, uncle or aunt (§ 2 (4)). The right of grandparents to inherit in the absence of issue or parents of decedent, is clarified by § 3 (4). Under § 8, an adopted person may now continue to inherit from a natural parent who married the adopting parent. By § 6 (c), a person who participates, either as principal or accessory before the fact, in a wilful and unlawful killing may not inherit

§ 159 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

as a result of the killing. Certain procedural changes are made relating to the jurisdiction of the Orphans' Court. A person related through two lines of relationship shall take one share only which shall be the larger share 

By 3 (6), the Commonwealth acquires the estate in preference to relatives more distant than first cousins.

Except as otherwise hereinafter stated under section heading and page number of the original work, the intestate law under the new Act remains the same as that reviewed in the original work. The exceptions follow. = (c) Adopted Children.

PAGE 332 

The General Adoption Act of April 4, 1925, P.L. 127, as amended by the Act of April 26, 1929, P.L. 882, 1 P.S. 

1 et seq., cited in the original text, has been further amended several times as to various sections. The discussion and review of the subject in the original text is still applicable under the amendments, except as hereinafter noted. - PAGE 333 § 8 of the new Intestate Act substantially re-enacts the I prior law, except that it adds a proviso that "if a natural parent shall have married the adopting parent, the adopted person for the purpose of descent by, from or through him shall also be considered the issue of such natural parent." This changes the law as laid down in 

Fischer v. Robison, 329 Pa. 305-1938, cited on page 

333 of the original text.

On what law governs the right of an adopted child to inherit, see Zoell's Est., 345 Pa. 413-1942, which holds that if the law of the decedent's domicile does not allow I an adopted child to inherit, he cannot do so although the I law of the state of adoption or the state where a chattel # is physically situated would allow him to take. However, # in the case of real estate the rule is that the law of the I state where the real estate is located governs.

§ 134,2(a) § 134. Intestate Laws of Pennsylvania. Order of In heritance.

PAGE 334 Changes only are noted which the new Intestate Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 80, 20 P.S. 1.1 et seq., makes in the original text of the former Act of 1917. 

2 (a). Surviving Spouse and No Issue. 

The new Act, where there is no issue, increases the surviving spouse's allowance from $5,000 to $10,000 (Intestate Act of 1947, P.L. 80, § 2 (3), 20 P.S. 1.2 (3)). 

It has been held that the allowance is still confined to cases of actual intestacy and is not allowable where the spouse elects to take against the will of one who dies partially intestate (Martin Estate, 365 Pa. 280—1950).

PAGE 336 

Procedure in Setting Aside Allowance for Spouse.

The procedure for setting aside the spouse's allowance when it is claimed out of real estate is covered by § 10 of the new Act (20 P.S. 1.10). It differs from that provided in the old Act.

Under the new Act, the Orphans' Court of the county where letters testamentary or administration have been granted, or if no letters, then the Orphans' Court of the county of the family or principal residence of the decedent, regardless of the location of real estate, is given jurisdiction to appoint appraisers to set apart the real estate to the surviving spouse, although that court is given discretion, where the real estate is outside of county, to direct application to be made for appointment of appraisers to the Orphans' Court of the county in which the real estate lies. Exceptions to the appraisement, wherever made, must be filed in the court of original jurisdiction, although such exceptions may be referred by it to the court of the county in which the real

§ 134(3) 

161 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

estate is located. If the decedent was a non-resident of the State, then appraisers shall be appointed by the Orphans' Court of any county wherein any real estate of decedent shall lie, and that court is given jurisdiction concerning the allowance with respect to all of the real estate of decedent within the Commonwealth.

Other procedural matters are provided for in the new 

Act. 10 (c), (d), 20 P.S. 1.10 (c), (d) covers the case where the real estate claimed cannot be divided so as to set apart the amount claimed, or the real estate exceeds it in value. § 10 (e) provides for prorating of income when the spouse's allowance does not exhaust the entire real and personal estate. § 10 (f) provides for recording a certified copy of every decree confirming the appraisement in the Recorder of Deeds' office in the county where the land lies and indexing in the grantor's index, under name of the decedent and in the grantee's index, under the name of the surviving spouse, as under the old law. § 10 (g) provides a new additional remedy by enabling the surviving spouse to collect the allowance "in the manner provided for collection of legacies." And § 10 (h) changes the old law by placing costs, appraisers fees, expenses of recording, etc. on the estate as part of administration costs. The former Act placed the recording and registering charges on the surviving spouse.

PAGE 337 

(3). Widow's Share in Lieu of Dower.

The new Intestate Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 80 § 5 (a), 

20 P.S. 1.5a, is practically same as under Act of 1917, except the last sentence which, under the new Act, reads, "The widow shall receive the same share in a future estate owned by the husband as in an estate of which he dies seised, although the particular estate shall not terminate before the death of the husband." The purpose of the change is to include reversions and contingent interests.

§ 134(4) 

(4). Surviving Husband's Share in Lieu of Curtesy; etc. 

This is covered by § 5 (b), 20 P.S. 1.5(b), of the new

Intestate Act of 1947. Comment as to change to include contingent interests and reversions, made in the case of the widow, has equal application here. PAGES 337-338 Forfeiture of husband and wife's rights by non-support or desertion in case of husband and desertion in case of wife is the same under the new Intestate Act, § 

6 (a), (b), 20 P.S. 1.6(a), (b)), as in the old Act (see 

Hudak Estate, 383 Pa. 278-1955).

PAGES 338-343 

The order of inheritance under the old Intestate Act of 1917 has been condensed in the new Act of 1947, and is covered by §§ 3, 4, 20 P.S. 1.3, 1.4, thereof. The order of inheritance is the same in the new Act, except that the Commonwealth takes the estate in preference to next of kin more distant than first cousins if there be no issue of brothers and sisters. Also, the inheritance of grandparents is clarified by § 3 (4) in the new Act (20 P.S. 

1.3(4)) as follows: "If no issue of either of the decedent's parents but at least one grandparent survives the decedent, then half to the paternal grandparents or grandparent, or if both are dead, to the children of each of them and the children of deceased children of each of them, and half to the maternal grandparents or grandparent, or if both are dead to the children of each of them and the children of deceased children of each of them. If both of the paternal grandparents or both of the maternal grandparents are dead leaving no child or grandchild to survive the decedent, the half which would have passed to them or to their children and grandchildren shall be added to the half passing to the grandparents or grandparent on the other side."

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

PAGE 344 Restriction on Right of Slayers to Inherit from the Person Slain. $6 (c) of the Intestate Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 80, 20 P.S. 1.6(c), provides that a person participating, either as principal or as an accessory before the fact, in the wilful and unlawful killing of another person shall not in any way acquire property or receive any benefits as the result of such killing, but such property or benefits shall be distributed as provided by law. This provision refers the distribution of what would have been the slayer's share to the Uniform Slayers Act, i.e., the Act of August 5, 1941, P.L. 816, § 3, 20 P.S. 3443, which provides, "The slayer shall be deemed to have predeceased the decedent as to property which would have passed from the decedent or his estate to the slayer under the statutes of descent and distribution or have been acquired by dower, curtesy or by statutory right as surviving spouse." Cf. § 5 (a), ante, page 7 of this supplement. 

For a case construing the meaning of the word "wilful,' 

see Sobel v. The National Bank & Trust Co. of Erie, 71 

D. & C. 321-1950, holding that slaying by an insane person is not "wilful.' 99 The Slayer's Act has been held not to operate retroactively, so that where husband and wife acquired real property as tenants by entirety before the effective date of the Act, and after the effective date the husband killed the wife, he nevertheless took good title by survivorship (Wyckoff v. Clark, 77 D. & C. 249-1951), and upon his subsequent death the title passed to or through his estate (Smith Estate, 3 D. & C. 2d 757-1955). (24). Escheat.

165 

INTESTATE DESCENT IN PENNSYLVANIA EXPLANATION OF THE CHART (pp. 166, 167) 

1. No distinction is made between descent of real or personal property: $1; between whole and half blood; $4 (2); between citizen and alien: $4 (7); or concerning the source of ownership: $4(4). Persons begotten before decedent's death take as if born in his lifetime: $4 (3). Persons related to decedent through two lines of relationship take one share only, which is the larger share: $4 (8). Parents and grandparents receive shares by entireties, others as tenants in common: $4(6). Act passes only interest of decedent: $4(5). It does not apply to personal property of non-resident: $14. Shares of others than the surviving spouse are subject to advancements: $9. Shares may be lost by a wilful and unlawful killing ($6) or by failure to claim within 7 years: $13. 

2. The surviving spouse's statutory share is given in lieu of common law dower and curtesy and applies to present and future estates: $5. The respective intestate rights of widower and widow are the same. The widow's and children's exemption of $500 [now, under the Fiduciaries 

Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 512, $211, 20 P.S. 320.211, a "family exemption" of $750] is, of course, a separate and unrelated matter. A surviving spouse's share may be lost by desertion, and in addition a widower's share may be lost because of non-support: $6. 

3. A special procedure is available to confirm the surviving spouse's title to real estate when entire estate is claimed: $11. 

4. The $10,000 is allowed whether the intestacy is entire or partial. It is a vested right which does not have to be claimed. A procedure is available where the claim affects real estate: $$2 and 10. Intestate share given by will [Wills Act of 1947 $14 (4)] or by inter vivos transfer [Estates Act of 1947, §14(1)] does not always include the $10,000 allowance. 

5. Illegitimates, in absence of marriage of parents, are considered issue in the family of the mother only: $7. Adopted children are considered issue of the adopting parents and not issue of natural parents, except that the status of a natural parent married to an adopting parent does not change: $8. 

6. The shares going to persons in different degrees of relationship to decedent are calculated by dividing the entire share so descending "into as many equal shares as there shall be persons in the nearest degree of consanguinity to the decedent living and taking shares therein and persons in that degree who have died before the decedent and have left issue to survive him who take shares therein. One equal share shall descend to each such living person in the nearest degree and one equal share shall descend" per stirpes to the issue of each such deceased person but not so as to include persons more remote than first cousins: $4(1). 

7. Share of Commonwealth must be reduced to cash after notice to Attorney 

General: $12.

§ 139  

PAGE 354 

§ 139. Estate by Curtesy. 

The new Intestate Act of 1947, P.L. 80, 20 P.S. 1.5 

(b), continues the provision of the former Intestate Act of 1917, § 4, 20 P.S. 32, that the shares of the estate to which the surviving husband shall be entitled shall be in lieu and full satisfaction of his curtesy at common law. 

It changes, however, the phraseology of that part of the Act of 1917 which included remainder interests to read, "The surviving husband shall receive the same share in a future estate owned by the wife as in an estate of which she dies seised, although the particular estate shall not terminate before the death of the wife." The change was made so as to include reversions and contingent estates. 

Of course, it must be remembered that the allowance to respective spouses where there is no issue has been increased from $5,000 to $10,000 (Act of 1947, P.L. 80, § 2, 20 P.S. 1.2). 

PAGE 355 § 141. How Curtesy May Be Barred. 

(b) By Divorce. 

It should be noted that the Act of April 11, 1927, P.L. 

181, 48 P.S. 117a, operates to bar curtesy (or its modern replacement) after a divorce from bed and board (a mensa et thoro) is granted to any married woman, for it provides that as long as such decree remains in force, such married woman may encumber by mortgage or otherwise, or grant, convey or otherwise dispose of, real estate or any interest therein, whether acquired before or after the decree, without joinder of the husband and in all respects as though she were a feme sole. The Act was held constitutional and to be liberally construed in 

Scaife v. McKee, 298 Pa. 33-1929.

§141(c)169 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

PAGES 355-6 

(c) Non-support or Desertion by Husband. 

The same provision is in the new Intestate Act of 1947, P.L. 80, § 6 (a), 20 P.S. 1.6(a).

Where a wife, by ill treatment of her husband, was forced to leave him under circumstances which made him guilty of constructive desertion, her subsequent adultery does not restore the husband's curtesy (Jac Estate, 355 

Pa. 137-1946; see also Archer Estate, 363 Pa. 5341950).

PAGE 357 

3. Evidence. 

Although a surviving spouse is a competent witness, his or her testimony ought to be closely scrutinized, carefully weighed, and rejected where self-interest, improbability and lack of candor raises a doubt as to its credibility; and the rejection of a spouse's claim because of disbelief of such testimony by an auditing judge will not be set aside unless clear error is shown (Jones' Est., 58 

D. & C. 595-1947; and see Archer's Est., 67 D. & C. 605 -1949, aff. 363 Pa. 534-1950. See also comments on 

145 (c) re desertion by wife, post, page 171 of this supplement). 

The Act of April 24, 1931, P.L. 46, making a surviving spouse competent to testify in any proceeding involving dispute of such spouse's right to share in the decedent's estate because of alleged desertion or non-support, and I cited in the original text, was repealed by § 16 of the 

Intestate Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 80, 20 P.S. 1.16, insofar as it relates to inheritance, and by § 21 of the Wills 

Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 89, 20 P.S. 180.21, insofar as it relates to disposition by will, and has been supplied by § 6 (d) of the Intestate Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 1.6(d), 

| and § 9 (d) of the Wills Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 180.9 (d), respectively.

III. 

DOWER. 

§ 142. Dower. 

PAGE 359 

The new Intestate Act of 1947, P.L. 80, § 5 (a), 20 

P.S. 1.5(a), like its predecessor, the Intestate Act of 

1917, provides that the shares to a widow given thereunder "shall be in lieu and full satisfaction of her dower at common law," and includes lands aliened by her husband in his lifetime. As in the case of curtesy (see ante, comments under § 139, page 168, herein), the phraseology of the new Act extends the widow's rights so as to include reversions and contingent estates. Note also that the surviving widow's allowance like the surviving husband's, in cases of intestacy and no surviving issue, is increased from $5,000 to $10,000. (Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 80, § 2 (3), 20 P.S. 1.2). PAGE 361 

§ 145. How Dower May Be Barred. 

(a) By Voluntary Joinder in a Deed of Husband. 

New. Dower is not barred by failure of the wife to bring an action in the lifetime of her husband, nor by the

§ 145(e) 171 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

fact that the husband committed a fraud by falsely reciting in the deed that he was a single man (Ladner v. 

Conver, 60 D. & C. 512-1947). Nor does the statute of limitations begin to run in favor of the purchaser until death of the husband (id.).

PAGE 362 

(c) Desertion or Elopement and Adultery of the Wife.

The new Intestate Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 80, § 6 (b), 20 P.S. 1.6(b), re-enacts § 6 of the 1917 Act, cited in the original text.

As to the effect of a separation by mutual consent, it should be noted that where the separation is consentual but if one of the parties commits an act for which the other could have procured a divorce (e.g., adultery), the element of consent thereafter is turned into wilfulness and malice and the separation is turned into desertion under the Act and deprives the offender of all interest in the other's estate (Crater Estate, 372 Pa. 458—1953); 

Bowman's Estate, 301 Pa. 337-1930; Lodge's Estate, 287 

Pa. 184—1926), unless there has been condonation (Bowman's Estate, supra; Lodge's Estate, supra). But if both commit adultery after a separation by mutual consent, neither may share in the other's estate irrespective of who was the first to transgress (Archer Estate, 67 D. & C. 605-1949, aff. 363 Pa. 534-1950). 

(e) Ante-Nuptial or Post-Nuptial Agreement. 

Ante-nuptial or post-nuptial agreements to bar a widow's right must not be obtained by fraudulent conduct or concealment. The presumption, however, is against concealment or imposition (Holwig Estate, 348 Pa. 711943. See also Emery Estate, 362 Pa. 142–1949). But where the provision made for the wife is grossly disproportionate to the value of the husband's estate, fraudulent concealment will be presumed and the burden of proof will be thrown onto his executor to show that full disclosure had been made (McClellan Estate, 365 Pa. 401 -1950).

§ 145 (k)  

An ante-nuptial agreement requires one of two factors for validity, viz., reasonable provision for the wife, or a full and fair disclosure to her of the husband's worth (Ziegler Estate, 381 Pa. 436-1955; Snyder Estate, 375 

Pa. 185-1953). But a release by the husband does not require either of these two factors, and is binding in the absence of fraud, since the wife owes the husband no duty of support (Barnhart v. Barnhart, 376 Pa. 441954). Recital in the agreement that complete disclosure has been made is primary evidence of that fact, and a widow who elects to take against the will is incompetent to testify that in fact such disclosure was not made (Snyder Estate, supra).

Kirk should read 340 Pa. 203 instead of 240.

P.S. 180.21, but is supplied by § 4 (7) of the Intestate Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 1.4(7), and § 17 of the Wills Act of 

1947, 20 P.S. 180.17.

added to the older provision for escheat of property in the hands of trustees, where the cestui que trust or his whereabouts has been unknown for seven years, provisions for escheat of any property within or subject to the control of the Commonwealth (except property in the hands of corporations engaged in receiving deposits of money, securities or other property for safekeeping) whenever the owner or beneficial owner or his whereabouts has been unknown for seven years, or whenever any such property is without a rightful or lawful owner, or has remained unclaimed for seven years. 

§ 154. Proceedings to Escheat.

Although the new Intestate Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 1.1 et seq., advances the position of the Commonwealth to take the property of a decedent from the position of ultimate heir to that immediately following first cousins or other nearer relations, etc. (see comments, ante, relating to page

CHAPTER XI. 

TITLE BY WILLS. 

I. WILLS. 

§ 157. Modern Requirements of Wills. What Law Gov erns.

PAGE 374 A will of a non-resident, probated outside of the State, when an exemplified copy thereof is filed with the Register of Wills in the County of this State in which the

§ 158  

land lies, will pass title to such real estate in accordance with its terms provided the will conforms to the law of this State (Hoysradt et al. v. Tionesta Gas Co., 194 Pa. 251-1900). 

§ 158. Statute of Wills in Pennsylvania. 

New. The Wills Act of 1917 has been replaced as to all persons dying on or after January 1, 1948, by the new 

Wills Act of April 24, 1947, P.L. 89, 20 P.S. 180.1 et seq., cited as the Wills Act of 1947 (20 P.S. 180.20). The new 

Act, while differently worded, is substantially the same as its predecessor so far as fundamentals are concerned. 

The changes made, so far as they relate to real estate, will be noted herein under the appropriate section headings and numbers. 

§ 159. Who May Make a Will. Capacity. 

PAGE 375 The new Wills Act, as the old did, provides that any person of sound mind, twenty-one years of age or older, may make a will (§ 1 (a), 20 P.S. 180.1); but it adds by clause (b) (20 P.S. 180.1(b)) a new provision with regard to persons in Military Service and Mariners. As to them it provides, "During any war in which the United States is engaged, a person of sound mind eighteen years of age or older and being in the Armed Forces of the United States in active service at home or abroad, or being a mariner on land or at sea, may by will dispose of all of his real and personal estate subject to payment of his debts and charges and may thereafter revoke such will whether or not the United States is engaged in war and whether or not he is still in such service or is a mariner." 

This clause makes important changes in the law as it stood prior to the new Act: It permits soldiers and sailors in active service during a war, and mariners at sea who are eighteen years of age, to dispose of real as well as personal property, but requires such wills to be in writing just as other wills. It provides also for the

§ 175 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

revocation of such wills whether the war or service has ceased or not and whether testator has attained the age of twenty-one years or not. By amendment of May 22, 1953, P.L. 216, 20 P.S. 180.1, the requirement that it should apply only "During any war in which the United 

States is engaged" was removed. 

§ 160. Will Must Be Writing. 

There is no change in the requirement that the will must be in writing and that devises of real estate cannot be made by a nuncupative will (Wills Act of 1947, §§ 2, 3, 20 P.S. 180.2, 180.3). 

§ 161. Will Must Be Signed at End. Execution. 

PAGE 377 (a) Signing by Mark. 

§ 2 (2) of the new Wills Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 180.2(2), provides: "If the testator is unable to sign his name for any reason, a will to which he makes his mark and to which his name is subscribed in his presence before or after he makes his mark, shall be valid as though he had signed his name thereto: Provided, He makes his mark in the presence of two witnesses who sign their names to the will in his presence." (Italics supplied). § 2 (3) of the Act, 20 P.S. 180.2(3), provides, "(3) Signature by another. If the testator is unable to sign his name or to make his mark for any reason, a will to which his name is subscribed in his presence and by his express direction shall be as valid as though he had signed his name thereto Provided, He declares the instrument to be his will in the presence of two witnesses who sign their names to it in his presence." 

§ 162. Subscribing Witnesses. Gifts to Charities. 

PAGE 379 

The new Wills Act of 1947, § 7 (1), 20 P.S. 180.7, still declares a bequest or a devise for religious or charitable

§ 163-A  

purposes invalid if made within thirty days of the testator's death, computing that period by including the day on which the will or codicil was written and excluding the day of death. It provides, however, for the validity of such gift where all who would benefit by its invalidity agree that it shall nevertheless be valid. It provides also for saving such a gift where a prior will or codicil revoked by it contains an identical gift for substantially the same religious or charitable purposes. 

§ 163-A. Construction of Devises of Real Estate. 

PAGES 382-383 

The rules of interpretation as recited in this section of the original text remain the same under 14 of the new Wills Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 180.14, except those relating to adopted children. The former rule that a bequest or devise to children includes the children of the testator, but not of other persons, adopted after the date of the will (see last sentence of § 163-A, page 383 of original text), has been changed by the new Wills Act of 1947, § 14 (11) so as to include children adopted before the testator's death, regardless of whether the devise or bequest is to children of the testator or to children of some other person. The same also includes in such class bequests, illegitimates so far as the mother and her family are concerned, and where the parents of the illegitimate afterwards marry, then as to both parents and their families. These changes apply as well to lapsed and void devises.

Another change to be noted is that § 14 (4) of the new Wills Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 180.14(4), provides that "A devise or bequest of real or personal estate *** to the testator's or another designated person's 'heirs', or 'next of kin', 'relatives', or 'family' or to the persons entitled thereunto under the intestate laws, or to persons described by words of similar import, shall mean those persons, including the spouse, who would take under the intestate laws if the testator or other designated person

§ 163-B 

177 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

were to die intestate at the time when such class is to be ascertained, a resident of the Commonwealth, and owning the estate so devised or bequeathed: Provided, however, that the share of a spouse, other than the spouse of the testator, shall not include the ten thousand dollar allowance under the intestate laws. The time when such class is to be ascertained shall be the time when the devise or bequest is to take effect in enjoyment." (Italics supplied) 

Rule in Shelley's Case. The new Wills Act of 1947, 

§ 16, 20 P.S. 180.16, provides that the rule in Shelley's 

Case shall not be applied. (See also Act of July 15, 1935, P.L. 1013, 20 P.S. Ch. 2, App. 229; and Hough v. Farmers 

Bank & Trust Co., 359 Pa. 555-1948, footnote, page 557 thereof, for discussion of this rule now abolished.) 

New. Cemetery Lots. The Act of June 3, 1953, P.L. 278 

(No. 53), 20 P.S. 180.14, adds a new subsection (15) to the rules of interpretation, which provides: "If in a will no express disposition or other mention is made of a cemetery lot owned by the testator at his decease and wherein he or any member of his family is buried, the ownership of the lot shall not pass from his lawful heirs by a residuary or other general clause of the will but shall descend to his heirs as if he had died intestate." 

New. § 163-B. Slayer May Not Take Under Slain Per son's Will.

PAGE 383 

§ 7 (5) of the new Wills Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 180.7(5), provides, "Any person who participates either as a principal or as an accessory before the fact in the wilful and unlawful killing of any person shall not in any way acquire property or receive any benefits as the result of the wilful and unlawful killing but such property or benefits shall be distributed as provided by law." The last clause is intended to refer to the Uniform Slayer's Act of August 5, 1941, P.L. 816, § 4, as amended by Act of May 

23, 1947, 20 P.S. 3441-3456, which provides that property, including property appointed by will, which would

§ 165 

4 have passed to or for the benefit of the slayer by devise or legacy from the decedent, shall be distributed as if he had predeceased the decedent. The Slayer's Act, §§ 7, 8 and 9, 20 P.S. 3447, 3448, 3449, contains provisions governing also reversions, remainders, contingent remainders, executory and other future interests. As to the meaning of the term wilful and unlawful killing, see

Sobel v. The National Bank & Trust Co. of Erie, 71 D. & C. 

321-1950, holding that a killing by an insane widow is not wilful. 

§ 165. Election or Right to Take Against Will of De ceased Spouse.

PAGE 384 

The new Wills Act of 1947, § 8, 20 P.S. 180.8, makes no change in the prior law as to a spouse's right of election to take against the will, nor in the amount the spouse may take by virtue of the election (Martin Estate, 365 Pa. 280-1950). It clarifies some confusion in decisional law relating to that part of the spouse's estate passing under a power of appointment. As to this, § 8 (c) provides, "The surviving spouse, upon an election to take against the will, shall not be entitled to any share in property passing under a power of appointment given by someone other than the testator and exercised by the will of the testator whether or not such power has been exercised in favor of the surviving spouse and whether or not the appointed and the individual estates have been blended." PAGES 385-387 

§ 166. Proceedings Necessary to Take Against the Will. 

The new Wills Act of 1947, P.L. 89, 20 P.S. 180.10180.12, makes several changes in the procedure by which a spouse is required to exercise the right of election to take against the will. 

10 requires the spouse to manifest the election in

§ 179 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

writing signed and acknowledged. The election or a copy must be sent to the personal representative or his attorney and filed in the office of the Clerk of the Orphans' Court of the County where will is probated. If the estate includes real estate, the election or a duplicate or certified copy must also be recorded in the Recorder's office of each county in which the real estate lies, and shall be indexed by the Recorder in the Grantors' index under the name of testator and in Grantees' index under the name of surviving spouse. The costs of filing and recording shall be paid out of the estate as part of administration costs. This section is substantially the same as the prior law. 

Time of Making Election. 

§ 11 provides that as between the surviving spouse and other legatees and devisees, the election is in time if, within one year after probate of the will, the election is (1) sent to the personal representative or his attorney, or (2) filed in the Clerk's office of Orphans' Court and a copy is sent to the personal representative or his attorney, or (3) recorded in Recorder of Deeds' Office of the proper county and a copy is sent to the personal representative or his attorney. The court may extend the time if application for extension is made within one year after probate of the will. A certified copy of such decree of extension may be recorded and indexed in the Recorder's Office of the proper county. The principal changes from the prior law are to permit application for extension to be made within one year instead of seven months as formerly, and omission of procedure whereby a spouse may be compelled to elect after six months, etc. (see page 

386 of original text).

Failure to Make Election. 

§ 12 (a) of the new Wills Act, 20 P.S. 180.12, provides, substantially as under the prior law, that failure to elect within the time fixed shall be deemed an election to take under the will, and no payment from the estate except the

§ 166-A  

exemption allowed to the widow by law may be made within a year after probate unless the election is first filed.

New. Election as Against a Grantee or Lienholder. 

The Wills Act of 1947, by § 13, 20 P.S. 180.13, makes a new provision under which an election is void as against a bona fide grantee of, or a holder of a lien on, real estate in any county unless (1) the election is recorded in such county within one year after probate or, if thereafter, before the recording of the instrument or entry of the lien under which the grantee or lienholder claims, or (2) a certified copy of the decree extending the time of election has been recorded in such county within the year after probate, or, if thereafter, then before the recording of the instrument or entry of the lien and the election is recorded within the time fixed by the decree or before the recording of the instrument or entry of such lien. The purpose of this provision is to protect bona fide grantees, mortgagees and judgment creditors where the election is recorded after the year has expired. The spouse who fails to record the election, however, is allowed to take ahead of devisees if the minimum requirements of $10 have been met.

PAGE 387 On the question of procedure to file an election on behalf of a mentally incompetent spouse and the considerations which may move the court that appointed the guardian or committee to authorize such election, see 

Harris Estate, 351 Pa. 368, 382, 383-1945. 

§ 166-A. Effect of Election to Take Against a Will on Other Provisions Thereof.

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

will. Such spouse takes her share of real estate directed to be sold, as real estate (Sadowski Estate, 158 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 119, 124-1945, citing Petterson's Est., 195 Pa. 781900 and Cunningham's Est., 137 Pa. 621-1890). 

§ 166-B. How Right of Election May Be Barred. 

No change is made by § 9 (a) and (b) of the new Wills 

Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 180.9, which forfeits the husband's right of election who, for one year or upwards previous to the wife's death, either (1) neglected or refused to provide for her or (2) for that period wilfully and maliciously deserted her. The wife's right to elect is also forfeited if, for one year and upwards previous to her husband's death, she wilfully and maliciously deserted him. § 9 (c) also forfeits the right of election of a surviving spouse who participates either as a principal or as an accessory before the fact in the unlawful killing of the testator.

The principle laid down in § 166-B of the original text that death of the spouse before election also bars the right (citing McClintock's Estate, 240 Pa. 543—1913) has been written into § 12(b) of the new Wills Act of 

1947, 20 P.S. 180.12(b). 

§ 167. Revocation of Wills. Express Revocation. 

PAGE 389 

The new Wills Act of 1947, § 5, 20 P.S. 180.5, while differently worded from the prior Act of 1917, makes no changes in substance, so far as revocation of wills devising real estate is concerned, but it incorporates the requirement of existing law that if a will is revoked by destruction through another person in the presence of the testator, the direction of the testator to do so must be proved by the oaths or affirmations of two competent witnesses (Simrell's Est., 154 Pa. 604-1893). 

New. The Wills Act of 1947, § 6, 20 P.S. 180.6, very properly sets at rest the troublesome question of de-

§ 168  

pendent relative revocation or revival of prior wills when a revoking will is in turn revoked (see Burtt Will, 353 Pa. 217-1945 and Ford's Estate, 301 Pa. 183-1930).

The provision in the new Act on this point declares: "If, after the making of any will, the testator shall execute a later will which expressly or by necessary implication revokes the earlier will, the revocation of the later will shall not revive the earlier will, unless the revocation is in writing and declares the intention of the testator to revive the earlier will, or unless, after such revocation, the earlier will shall be re-executed. Oral republication of itself shall be ineffective to revive a will." The last sentence quoted is declaratory of the previously existing law (Shetter's Estate, 303 Pa. 193-1931). 

§ 168. Implied Revocation. 

(a) By Subsequent Marriage, Birth or Adoption of Child. 

PAGE 390 

Marriage. The new Wills Act of 1947 (see § 7 (3), 20 

P.S. 180.7(3), makes no change in the old law which provided that marriage after execution of a will impliedly revoked the will so far as the spouse is concerned, such spouse being entitled to the same share as if the testator had died intestate, "unless the will gives a greater share." The last clause quoted is new but merely declaratory of existing law (Lintner's Est., 297 Pa. 428-1929). 

In Bomberger's Estate, 47 D. & C. 627, 629—1943, citing Edwards's Appeal, 47 Pa. 144-1864, it was held the revocation as far as the spouse is concerned is not dependent upon a provision made to her. It resulted absolutely as a legal consequence of the marriage. The statute annexes no condition of provision as it does in the case of after born or after adopted children. Nor is any election necessary to be filed. PAGE 391 

Birth or Adoption. The new Wills Act of 1947, § 7 (4), 

20 P.S. 180.7(4), makes a radical change in its provision

§ 183 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

for after born or after adopted children as compared with the former Act. The new law reads, "If the testator fails to provide in his will for his child born or adopted after making his will, unless it appears from the will that the failure was intentional, such child shall receive out of the testator's property not passing to a surviving spouse, such share as he would have received if the testator had died unmarried and intestate owning only that portion of his estate not passing to a surviving spouse. 

Under the prior law such child not provided for would be entitled to the share it would have received had the parent died intestate. By the new Act, if a parent left his whole estate to the surviving spouse the after born or after adopted child would receive nothing (see Fownes 

Estate, 82 D. & C. 518-1952 and Frank's Estate, 67 D. & C. 198-1949).

New. Divorce. The Wills Act of 1947, § 7(2), 20 P.S. 

180.7(2), provides, "If the testator is divorced from the bonds of matrimony after making a will, all provisions in the will in favor of his spouse so divorced shall be thereby revoked." This provision is new and intended to overrule Jones' Est., 211 Pa. 364-1905 (cited in the original text at page 392) where a divorced wife was held entitled to a legacy under her former husband's will made before the divorce. 

§ 169. Probate of Wills. Limitations. 

PAGE 393 

§ 303 (a) and (c) of the new Register of Wills Act of 

June 28, 1951, P.L. 638, 20 P.S. 1840.303 (a) and (c) provides that a will, other than a nuncupative will, may be offered for probate at any time, but if offered more than two years after the decedent's death it shall be void as against a bona fide grantee or mortgagee whose deed or mortgage is recorded before the will is probated. (A nuncupative will must be probated within three months after the testator's death, under § 3 (a) of the Wills Act

§ 169 

of 1947, P.L. 89, 20 P.S. 180.3 (a)). This changes the prior law by reducing from three to two years after the decedent's death the time within which a will may be probated to be effective as against bona fide grantees and mortgagees whose deeds or mortgages are recorded before the will is probated. 

§§ 208 (a) and 303 (b) of the Register of Wills Act of 

1951, 20 P.S. 1840.208(a) and 1840.303(b), continue, in effect, the former law that the Register of Wills' decree probating or refusing probate of a will becomes conclusive unless an appeal to the Orphans' Court is taken within two years after the decree.

For later cases discussing conclusiveness of probate, see Brindle Will, 360 Pa. 53-1948; Rockett's Estate, 48 

D. & C. 6, aff. Rockett Will, 348 Pa. 445-1944.

II. 

LIENS OF DEBTS AND LEGACIES ON DECEDENT'S REAL ESTATE AND SALE THEREOF. 

Important Forenote. Read first. The Fiduciaries Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 512, 20 P.S. 320.101 et seq., hereinafter referred to by its authorized short title, Fiduciaries Act of 1949, has made radical changes in the law relating to liens on, administration of and disposition of a decedent's real estate. But, as the sections of the new 

Act working these changes are applicable only to estates of decedents dying on or after January 1, 1950 ($ 105, 20 P.S. 320.105), it is apparent that for a considerable period of time the old law will still govern in many instances. Consequently this supplement will first note decisional or statutory changes in the old law since the publication of the original text, under the § headings and page numbers of the original text, after which the new law will be set forth. The same arrangement will be followed as to Powers and Sales by Administrators, Executors, etc.

§ 170-A 

185 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

§ 170-A. Lien of Decedent's Debts. 

(b) Lien of Debts Not of Record. 

PAGE 396 (2) Duration of Lien of Debts Not of Record. 

A fiduciary who uses his personal funds to pay debts of a decedent is subrogated to the rights of the creditors, but his rights rise no higher than theirs, and if the fiduciary fails to commence action and index the same within the year from decedent's death, he cannot thereafter recover the sums so paid out of the real estate (Sivak 

Estate, 161 Pa. Super. Ct. 323—1947; Krick's Estate, 342 Pa. 212-1941).

New. U. S. debts. It has been held that real estate may be sold under a petition to pay a debt to the United States even after the year, because the United States is not bound to preserve its lien by action commenced within the year since it has a lien imposed by federal statute and its validity is not affected by the limitation imposed by the Pennsylvania statute (Gibson's Estate, 97 

Pitts. L.J. 182-1949 and see also Bellanca Estate, 12 

Fay. L.J. 36—1949).

PAGE 398 Of course, if, within the year, the real estate is sold by direction of the court for payment of debts, the lien, though discharged from the real estate, is transferred to and continues against the proceeds of the sale even though no action is commenced and indexed within the year (Weidner's Estate, 40 D. & C. 407—1940). And the same is true where there is an equitable conversion or an actual sale by the executor under a discretionary power of sale (see § 183 of original text, page 460). But a sale made by a devisee, and not by an executor under a power in the will or by decree of court, even though the devisee's sale is made within the year, does not cast the lien on the fund (Finley Estate, 309 Pa. 200—1932).

Such lien is lost if no action is commenced and indexed within the year (Straub Estate, 74 D. & C. 77—1949).

§ 170-A  

New. Debts not of record no longer liens on real estate of a decedent dying on or after January 1, 1950. 

The Fiduciaries Act of 1949, 20 P.S. 320.101 et seq., changes the entire policy of the decedent's estate law in regard to decedents' real estate. While under the former law, a decedent's real estate was an asset liable for the payment of debts, it was not an asset in the hands of an administrator unless brought into the administration by an order of the Orphans' Court. Nor was it an asset in the hands of an executor unless power over it was given either by will or by order of the Orphans' Court 

(see original text, § 170-D and § 170-E). Creditors' rights to pursue real estate were restricted by the former law to one year from decedent's death unless they preserved the right or lien by commencing and indexing a suit (see original text, § 170-A). But unless preserved in the manner indicated, the real estate became discharged of such liens, and the right to collect out of the real estate was wholly lost. This policy has now been changed. Real estate now not only remains an asset for the payment of a decedent's debts, but all of his real estate except such as is occupied by an heir or devisee is committed to the possession and administration of the administrator or the executor who are designated in the new Act as "personal representative" (Fiduciaries Act of 1949, § 102 (4), 20 

P.S. 320.102(4)), and this without the necessity of having any authority under the will or first securing an order of the court (§ 501 id., 20 P.S. 320.501). Moreover, he is given full power to dispose of the same except in certain circumstances which will be reviewed hereinafter in this supplement under the headings of the sections dealing with powers of administrators and executors over real estate. Thus, a great advance has been made toward abolishing the distinction between a decedent's real and personal estate by bringing both under the control of the personal representative in the first instance. As a corollary to this new concept, it is no longer necessary to re-

§ 170-A 

187 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

quire the elaborate procedure of the old Act for continuing liens against decedents' estates after one year from the date of death, and such procedure is therefore omitted from the new Act.

So, § 615 of the new Fiduciaries Act of 1949, 20 P.S. 

320.615, provides: "Limitation upon claims All claims against the decedent, subject only to the provisions of sections 611 and 612," (these relate to liens, charges and judgments which were liens at death) "shall become unenforceable after one year from the decedent's death against a bona fide grantee of, or holder of a lien on, real property of the decedent who has acquired his interest for value from or through those entitled to the property by will or by intestacy, either- (1) More than one year after the death of the decedent and when no letters issued in the Commonwealth upon the decedent's estate were in effect; or, (2) Within such year if no letters upon the decedent's estate have been issued in the Commonwealth during that year.

Nothing in this section shall be construed to limit the right of a personal representative subsequently appointed to recover from the heir or devisee the value of property so sold or encumbered."

By this change, the creditor is deprived of his former security of a lien against real estate which he could continue by commencing and indexing his action within one year, and is given, in lieu thereof, the right to look to the personal representative to see that sufficient funds are made available for payment of the debts out of both real and personal property. If no personal representative has qualified, then the creditor must see that one is appointed and that he pursues the heirs or devisees who may have sold the real estate to a bona fide purchaser, to recover the value of the real estate so sold. The Commissioners who drafted the Act explain that the purpose is to make title to real estate freely alienable after one year where (1) no letters are granted or (2) there is a

§ 170-A  

vacancy in letters. Where no personal representative is appointed for a period of one year, the creditor can look only to the heir or devisee or next of kin for the payment of his claim. It is further explained that by all claims is meant all claims, including those of the Commonwealth but excluding claims of the United States. 

The latter presumably remain liens because of the several Acts of Congress which impose them (see § 170-C (b), page 403, and § 186-C, page 464, of original text). It would seem also that the Commonwealth's inheritance taxes should be excluded and that they still remain liens, since they are not, strictly, "claims against the decedent" (see § 170-C (a), page 402 of original text). 

Whether the creditors will fare better or worse under the new law than under the old, only time will tell. On the one hand, if letters are promptly issued, the creditor can rely, with reasonable confidence, on the personal representative to look after the rights of the creditors who have notified him of their claims (see § 614, 20 P.S. 

320.614). But if no letters are taken out and the heirs or devisees dispose of or encumber the real estate, bona fide and for value, before the creditor realizes that his debtor is dead, then there is left only the right to pursue the heir or devisee-a doubtful right if the heir or devisee has spent the proceeds and is judgment proof. 

Continuing the liability of the heir or devisee is a throwback to early law when the Province of Pennsylvania was founded and when real estate was first made liable for payment of decedent's debts, with the difference that the unpaid debt then remained a lien on the real estate until paid (see genesis of the rule as recounted by Mr. 

Justice Simpson in Kirk v. Van Horn, 265 Pa. 5491920), whereas now, under the new law, the lien is gone and a mere right of action against the heir or devisee is substituted therefor. However, the heirs or devisees, after one year when no letters have been granted, and after six years where granted and no account filed, may proceed under $ 756, 20 P.S. 320.756, to have title

§ 170-A (c) 

189 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

confirmed in them by the court, discharged of liens then not of record (see page 216 of this supplement). The endeavor to clear title to decedent's real estate so as to make it freely alienable by protecting the purchaser, may yet prove to have been at the cost of creditors, especially of those who do not live in the same community and have no knowledge or means of knowing that their debtor died until too late to protect themselves. Under the old law they at least had a year in which to protect themselves.* PAGE 399 

(c) Lien of Debts of Record Under the Old Law. 

A judgment held by the Commonwealth, upon sale of a decedent's real estate upon which it is a lien, is entitled to share in the proceeds in accordance with the order of priority of liens and is not postponed as a debt due the Commonwealth which, under § 13 (a) of the Fiduciaries 

Act of 1917, 20 P.S. Ch. 3, App. 501, would be paid last (Morrison's Estate, 343 Pa. 157-1941). Upon distribution of the proceeds of the sale of real estate for payment of debts, its judgment takes priority over funeral expenses and claims of general creditors (Engle's Estate, 344 Pa. 535-1942, Dry's Estate, 61 D. & C. 504-1947).

Liens of record continue for five years after the expiration of one year from decease of the defendant, and unless revived by writ of scire facias against the decedent, his heirs, executors or administrators, issued within six years of decedent's death, the lien is lost (Hershberger v. Hershberger, 345 Pa. 439-1942); nor would a conveyance by the devisees or heirs within the five years serve to revive it (DiBerardino B. & L. Ass'n v. DeGregoria, 158 Pa. Super. Ct. 516-1946). It has been held that a debt of record which had lost its lien during decedent's lifetime could not be preserved against the real estate by merely issuing a summons and indexing After this supplement went to press, the Act of February 23, 1956 (No. 

352) amended § 732 of the Fiduciaries Act of 1949 to restore the former one year lien of decedent's debts.

§ 170-A (d) 

the same within the year after decedent's death (Bagaj 

Estate, 66 D. & C. 512-1948).

PAGE 400 

Mortgages.

It is to be noted that as against heirs of a mortgagor, a mortgage not recorded until after the mortgagor's death is nevertheless good against the mortgaged real estate, even though no action was commenced thereon within one year after mortgagor's death, since it is a conditional conveyance, although there might be no recovery on the bond (Marks v. Morrison, Administrator, 41 D. & 

C. 669-1941).

New Subdivision in 170-A. (d) Judgments against Devisees or Heirs.

A judgment or other lien, entered against a devisee or heir after decedent's death, binds such devisee's or heir's interest in the real estate inherited. All such liens, however, will be discharged if the land is sold by order of the court for payment of decedent's debts, or if sold under a power in the will. Such sale divests the lien from the real estate sold, but since the proceeds of the sale are distributable as real estate, any overplus remaining after payment of decedent's debts, payable to the devisee or heir, is subject to the judgment or other lien if it was a lien at the time of the sale (Pearsol's Estate, 80 Pitts. 

L. J. 412-1932; Sayers's Appeal, 79 Pa. 428, 431-1875).

New Law Relating to Lien of Debts of Record. 

§ 611 of the Fiduciaries Act of 1949, P.L. 512, 20 P.S. 

320.611, provides that liens and charges on real and personal estate of the decedent which existed at his death "shall not be impaired." As to a judgment which, at the decedent's death, was a lien on real estate owned by him, or which was conveyed by him by deed not recorded in his lifetime, § 612 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.612, changes the law by reducing the period during which it continues

§ 170-B 

191 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

as a lien without revival. Under the old law it was automatically revived for five years from the decedent's death, but under the new Act it continues as a lien for five years from its inception, or last revival, or for one year from decedent's death, whichever shall be the longer.

Thus, if a judgment was entered or last revived two years before a decedent's death, it would continue as a lien for three years more after his death; but if it had been entered or last revived four years and six months before decedent's death, it would continue for one year after his death. The section provides that "during this period it shall rank according to its priority at the time of death, and after this period, it shall not continue to be a lien on the real estate, unless revived." The section also provides that "Any judgment against the decedent which is a lien on real estate aliened by him may be revived by an action of scire facias brought against the decedent, but before any judgment shall be entered thereon, the personal representative shall be made a party defendant and served with process in the action.” 

§ 170-B. Liens of Legacies. 

PAGE 400 The new Wills Act of 1947, P.L. 89, § 14, clause 13, 

20 P.S. 180.14(13), now provides that pecuniary legacies of $100 or less shall no longer be a charge or lien upon decedent's real estate. There is no change in the law as to legacies of a greater sum than $100 excluding interest. Elimination of legacies of $100 and less as a charge against real estate is a most desirable improvement in the law. There were no liens against real estate more troublesome to remove than these small legacies, especially when they were trivial amounts such as one dollar or five dollars, etc. Legatees all too frequently refused to accept or release them, and the cost of proceedings to release such liens was an unfair burden on the devisees or heirs of the real estate. For an interesting

§ 170-B  

discussion and summary of cases dealing with the lien of legacies, see article by James G. Schmidt, Esq., then 

Senior Title Officer, now Vice President, of Commonwealth Land Title Insurance Co. (of Philadelphia) in 

Fiduciary Review, July 1948, page 1. 

Note that the Act of May 23, 1949, P.L. 1692, declaring, inter alia, a charge or other lien against real estate to be conclusively presumed paid after fifty years, was held unconstitutional and the whole act invalid (Girard Trust 

Co., Trustee v. Pennsylvania R. R. Co., 364 Pa. 5761950).

Of course, when the real estate is sold by a personal representative under § 541 etc. of the new Fiduciaries Act of 1949, 20 P.S. 320.541 etc., the liens of legacies, as of debts not of record, are discharged, and a bona fide purchaser for value takes a good title free and clear of them (see especially § 547 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.547).

Under § 804 of the Fiduciaries Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 512, 20 P.S. 320.804 (amended editorially by Act of June 30, 1951, P.L. 988), the lien of a legacy (as well as dower, recognizance, annuity instalment or other charge) on real property is presumed to have been released or extinguished, and the legacy, etc. becomes irrecoverable, twenty years after it became due, if in the meantime no payment has been made on account, or if no proceeding has been brought or no written acknowledgment of the existence thereof or no written promise to pay the same has been made within the twenty years by the owner of the property. This section of the Act provides for recording, in the Recorder of Deeds' office in the county where the land lies, of evidence of such payment or acknowledgment or promise, and provides that in the absence of such recorded and indexed evidence within the twenty year period, the legacy, etc. shall be irrecoverable from any purchaser, mortgagee or other lien creditor.

These provisions are open to a number of questions which must await judicial construction. For example,

§ 170-B 193 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

§ 804(a) provides that the lien shall be presumed released or extinguished if, within the twenty year period, "no proceeding has been brought or no written acknowledgment of the existence thereof or no written promise to pay the same has been made within such period by the owner or owners of the property subject to the charge, ." (Italics supplied). A proceeding brought within the twenty year period for the purpose of enforcing payment of the legacy, etc., is undoubtedly the type of proceeding contemplated by the Act to overcome the presumption of release or extinguishment. Such a proceeding would be brought, of course, by the party entitled to payment of the legacy, etc., and not by the owner of the property subject to the charge. Literally, however, according to the Act, the proceeding is to be brought (as the written acknowledgment or promise are to be made) "by the owner or owners of the property "' etc.—a rather curious provision. Again, § 804(b) provides for perpetuating evidence of any such payment, acknowledgment or promise within the twenty year period "by recording it" in the office of the Recorder of Deeds, etc. Just how a payment on account is recorded is not explained. Whether a notation of the payment on the margin of the record of the legacy or charge itself (if the legacy or charge is recorded) and on the indices of the charge would be sufficient compliance with the Act, is not clear. § 804(b) provides for recording of evidence of any payment on account, acknowledgment or promise within the twenty year period. § 804(c) then provides for renewal by recording and indexing in successive twenty year periods, of "such evidence of the charge." Nothing in the prior provisions speaks of any "evidence of the charge." They deal with evidence of payment on account of, acknowledgment of the existence of, and promise to pay, the charge. Does this provision in 804 (c) mean that the charge itself may (or must) be recorded and indexed?

Probably not; for "such evidence of the charge" would

§ 170-B  

be inapt language for such a meaning. The legislature probably means that the validity and lien of the charge may be renewed in each successive twenty year period by recording and indexing within that period fresh evidence of any payment, acknowledgment or promise made within the period. But the language of the Act certainly does not say that; and it will require judicial construction to reach that or any other clear meaning.

Whether this Act, in effect, repeals § 7 of the Act of 

April 27, 1855, P.L. 368, 12 P.S. 80, insofar as the lien of legacies and other charges on real estate are concerned (see § 170-B, page 401 of original text), on the ground that it is inconsistent (under § 1401 (b) of the Fiduciaries 

Act of 1949, P.L. 512, 20 P.S. 320.1401 (b)), is also debatable. While the substantive grounds on which release or extinguishment is presumed are the same in both Acts, the 1855 Act requires a twenty-one year period, whereas the 1949 Act requires only a twenty-year period.

Until this section of the 1949 Act has been clarified, either by legislative amendment or by judicial construction, it will not be safe to rely on either the 1855 Act alone or the 1949 Act alone. It is not inconceivable that these provisions of the 1949 Act, or some of them, might be held invalid because of vagueness. In such event, the 

1855 Act would remain in full force. On the other hand, if the 1949 Act provisions should be held effective (with judicial clarification), then the 1855 Act would probably be held repealed for inconsistency. Until the courts have cleared away these and other uncertainties, discretion indicates compliance with both Acts. Thus, the holder of a legacy or other charge should see that evidence of payment on account, etc., is recorded and indexed, or suit is brought, within twenty years after the payment is due; and the owner of the real estate who wishes to remove the cloud of the lien of such a legacy, charge, etc., should wait for twenty-one years after the payment is due before bringing his suit.

195 

1956 SUPPLEMENT.

POWER AND AUTHORITY OF ADMINISTRATORS AND EXECUTORS TO ADMINISTER OR SELL REAL ESTATE. 

§ 170-D. Administrators Power Over Real Estate (Old

§ 170-D(a) 

PAGE 405 (a) In general.

Under the law of 1917, real estate descends to an intestate's heirs, and an administrator has no authority over it except upon application to the Orphans' Court under 16 of the Fiduciaries Act of 1917, P.L. 447, 20

P.S. Ch. 3, App. 551, regulating sale for payment of debts (Colison Estate, 356 Pa. 531-1947).

Under the 1917 law, an administrator's power over the decedent's real estate was limited to sale under court order for payment of debts (Huff's Estate, 300 Pa. 641930).

Correction in text. Citation of Wolfe v. Lewisburg 

Trust etc. Co., on page 405, should read 305 Pa. 583-1931.

PAGE 406 (b) When and How Power to Collect Rents may be Obtained.

After the liens of debts not of record have expired, the remedy to compel administrators or executors to collect rents for payment of debts is available only to creditors who had preserved their liens (Graham Estate, 41 D. & C. 276-1941, aff. 147 Pa. Super. Ct. 57-1941 and 346 

Pa. 479-1943).

An order directing an executor to collect rents under $ 14 of the Fiduciaries Act of 1917, P.L. 447, 20 P.S. 

Ch. 3, App. 503, is necessary only to assure the tenant of the authority of the executor, and is not a necessary element of the right to collect the rents; and where an executor has in fact collected rents and the estate is insolvent, the court may properly enter an order nunc pro tunc, after the expiration of one year, ratifying such collection for the benefit of decedent's creditors (Shibe's 

Estate, 41 D. & C. 315-1941, citing, inter alia, Joerger's 

Est., page 405 of original text, and 50 D. & C. 246-1944; 

Grigg's Estate, 54 D. & C. 25-1945).

PAGE 407 (c) Power over Real Estate which Decedent Contracted in his lifetime, to sell or buy. 

Supporting original text, see Bidelman Estate, 360 Pa. 195-1948.

PAGE 408 

New. (d) Duty and Power with Regard to Real Estate of Which Decedent was Lessee.

A leasehold, being personalty, survives the death of either lessor or lessee and is an asset of the estate (Wartanian's Estate, 305 Pa. 333-1931); and the administrator is chargeable with the value thereof (Wiley's Appeal, 8 W. & S. 244-1844); and if he surrenders it without attempting to realize the value thereof he will be surcharged (Coppel's Estate, 4 Phila. 378–1861). In Berman's Estate, 58 D. & C. 678—1947, Judge Van Roden, in a well considered opinion, held that a clause in a lease reading that "lessee will not assign nor underlet the premises (etc.) without the written consent of the Lessor first had and obtained," did not prohibit assignment by lessee's administrator or executor in the absence of special language prohibiting such assignment. In Kiernan v. Keeney, et al., 62 D. & C. 469–1947, Judge Oliver held that where there was added to the covenant against assignment a clause which read, "Any transfer by process of law shall be deemed an assignment by the lessee," it was such special language as prohibited transfer of the lease to a legatee under the lessee's will. 

§ 170-E. Power of Executors over Real Estate (Old Law. 

For power under Fiduciaries Act of 1949, see pages 205 et seq. of this supplement).

Supporting original text of the first paragraph of this section, see Kelley's Estate, 297 Pa. 17-1929, and Gatins 

Estate, 159 Pa. Super. Ct. 563-1946.

§ 170-E(1) 

Powers may also be given, and sometimes are, to others than the personal representatives, as e.g., a life tenant or devisee (Long v. Stout, 305 Pa. 310-1931); or to a trustee (Cresson v. Ferree, 70 Pa. 446-1872). Such powers are generally construed in the same manner as those granted to executors. 

Powers by Will

PAGE 409 

(1) Discretionary Power of Sale. 

Where a testatrix authorized and empowered her executor to sell all real estate of which she died seised if in his judgment he might deem it necessary or advisable in the settlement of her estate, it was held that heirs or legatees or others interested in the estate ordinarily have the right to furnish the necessary money for payment of decedent's debts and thus remove the cloud on their title to the land arising out of its liability to be sold for payment of debts. So, where the devisee advised the executor that he was willing to pay all debts and asked for the amount thereof, it was wrong for the executor to exercise his power of sale without giving devisee the information asked and an opportunity to pay. Under such circumstances, the sale was set aside (Noonan Estate, 163 Pa. Super. Ct. 70-1948, aff. 361 Pa. 26-1949). 

(2) Power Working a Conversion. 

PAGE 410 In Shaffer v. Shaffer, 354 Pa. 517-1946, where the will provided, "I direct that my executors hereinafter named, shall, at their discretion, sell and convey all or any part of my real estate," it was held that this did not work a conversion but was a discretionary power. But see Mahoney's Estate, 356 Pa. 358-1947.

PAGE 411 

Illustrative of the second class of conversions, viz., absolute necessity to sell in order to carry out the will,

§ 170-E(b) 

199 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

see Lagges Estate, 361 Pa. 205, 207-1949, which also illustrates the third class at page 208, viz., blending of real and personal property into one fund. Kikel v. Kikel, 372 Pa. 200-1953, also involves the third class. 

A power working a conversion does not operate, however, to convert the spouse's share of real estate if the spouse elects to take against the will (Petterson's Estate, 195 Pa. 78—1900; Cunningham's Estate, 137 Pa. 6211890; Hoover v. Landis, 76 Pa. 354-1874). But it does so operate where a spouse claims merely by virtue of an antenuptial agreement, because her status is that of a creditor and the agreement does not constitute an intervivos conveyance of her share (Mahoney Estate, 356 

Pa. 358-1947).

New. Direction to Convert does not Obviate Restrictions on charitable Gifts Imposed by the Wills Act. 

PAGE 412 It should be noted that this fiction of equitable conversion does not extend so far as to eliminate the necessity of compliance with the restriction imposed by the 

Wills Act of 1917 on charitable bequests. See § 162 of 

I original text and ante this supplement. This restriction 

I was held to apply to all real estate located in this State, I and a direction to convert it did not serve to obviate Pennsylvania's requirement (Koehler Partition Case, Almira Home Appeal, 360 Pa. 460-1948). 

PAGE 413 (b) Duration of Powers to Sell.

The mooted question, which often plagued the title I searcher called upon to decide how long a power given I to an executor could be validly exercised, was at last definitely settled in a clear authoritative opinion by Mr. 

Justice Jones in Shaffer Estate, 360 Pa. 390, 3941948, where he said (p. 394), "there is a conclusive presumption that a general [i.e., a discretionary] power of sale is for the payment of debts: see opinion of Mr.

§ 170-E(b)  

Justice Sharswood at Nisi Prius in Evans v. Chew, 71

Pa. 47, 51, affirmed per curiam by this Court. So that, in any case, the exercise of a discretionary power of sale can be supported on the basis of the purpose constructively furnished by the presumption. And, in no event, is there any compelling need for the factual presence of a distributional or debt-paying purpose as a prerequisite to an executor's exercise of a discretionary power of sale." (Italics supplied)

While the plain import of this decision is that an executor's discretionary power may be exercised even though more than a year has passed and without proof of existence of any unpaid debts or necessity of distribution, it seems, nevertheless, that just as all the devisees or heirs interested have a right to reconvert real estate equitably converted by a peremptory power (Henderson v. Henderson, 133 Pa. 399-1890) by electing to do so (see Hunter Common Place Book, vol. 1, page 275 and 

1939-1952 Supplement, page 87), so, too, they can, after the expiration of a year, terminate the executor's discretionary power by so electing if there are no unpaid debts or by providing for unpaid debts (Noonan Estate, 163 Pa. Super. Ct. 70-1948, aff. 361 Pa. 26—1949).

PAGE 414 (par. 7) 

Where a power is complete but its exercise only is postponed for the benefit of a particular person (e.g., a life tenant), the consent of that person will be equivalent to the happening of the stipulated contingency (Dingee 

Estate, 109 Pa. Super. Ct. 455-1933). But if it appears from the will that the power was not to be operative until the death of the life tenant, the conversion is held to take place only at the life tenant's death (Hackett's 

Estate, 44 D. & C. 593-1942; Knoppel's Estate, 46 D. & C. 391-1942).

New. Power to Sell Foreclosed Property. 

PAGE 415 Where a fiduciary forecloses a mortgage held by decedent in his lifetime, the real estate so acquired is considered as personalty in the hands of the fiduciary, and he may convey without express power in the will or by leave of court, such real estate being in the nature of a temporary investment (Colladay's Estate, 49 D. & C. 

614-1944; Fullerton v. Shuster, 53 D. & C. 323-1944; and see cases gathered in Judge Hunter's Common Place Book, vol. 1, page 705 and 1939-1952 Supplement, pages 308-309). 

§ 170-F. Sales for Payment of Debts. 

PAGE 415 A petition by an executor to sell decedent's real estate for payment of his debts, when granted, operates to supersede an agreement of sale by the devisees made before the completion of the administration of the estate, and extinguishes liability under such agreement (Powers 

Estate, 153 Pa. Super. Ct. 161-1943).

But the Orphans' Court has no power to grant a petition for or decree approval of a sale of real estate for payment of debts where the widow has previously claimed it for her widow's exemption (Koss Estate, 59 D. & C. 

308—1947); though the executor, as representing creditors, may except to undervaluation of the property 

I claimed (Witt Estate, 14 Som. 273-1949). Nor is the - rule different where decedent has waived his exemption and the creditor has levied on, but not sold, the real estate before death (Beetem & Co. v. Getz, 5 Pa. Super. Ct. 711897; see also Fiduciary Review, September, 1950, page 

4). A sale for payment of debts, directed by the Orphans 

Court, is a judicial sale (Jack's Estate, 59 D. & C. 465, 469-1947).
