§ 170-J. Executor's, Trustee's and Guardian's Power to Lease. (a) Executors and Administrators C.T.A. 

As to estates of decedents dying before January 1, 1950, the applicable law relating to power to lease may be stated as follows:-Executors, generally speaking, because their duty and purpose is to wind up an estate, are

§ 170-J (b) 208 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

not regarded as having the power to lease real estate unless such power is given in the will by express terms or necessary implication. A bare power of sale does not include the power to lease (King v. Myers, 60 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 345-1915); though authority to lease may be implied from other language of the will. But where a discretionary power to sell is given to an executor who is also a trustee, he is regarded as having authority to lease at least until the property is sold (Moore v. Trainer, 252 

Pa. 367-1916). 

The new Fiduciaries Act of April 18, 1949, P.L. 512, § 542, 20 P.S. 320.542, applicable to estates of decedents dying on or after January 1, 1950, now gives to personal representatives, except as otherwise provided in the will, power to lease any real estate which he is entitled to possess (i.e., real estate not occupied by an heir or devisee, or when directed to take possession of such by the court, § 501, 20 P.S. 320.501). Such a lease may be for a term expiring not more than one year after decedent's death unless terminable by the personal representative at any later time on thirty days' notice, or unless a longer term is approved by the court. (b) Trustees and Guardians. Powers to Lease.

No change has been made in the law with regard to the power of trustees and guardians to lease real estate committed to their charge. Under the Fiduciaries Act of 

June 7, 1917, P.L. 447, § 31, 20 P.S. Ch. 3, App. 717, as well as under §§ 962 and 1062 of the new Fiduciaries 

Act of 1949, 20 P.S. 320.962 and 320.1062, trustees may lease real estate for a term not exceeding five years, as may guardians also for a term of five years, but not beyond the date when the minor, if living, would attain his majority. But the court, under the new as well as under the old law, may authorize a lease by either trustee or guardian for a longer term (see Frankford 

Trust Co. v. D. A. Schulte Inc., 302 Pa. 421-1931). 

The provisions of these Acts do not apply to real estate

204. § 170-K 

which the fiduciary had acquired by foreclosure, because such real estate is still regarded, so far as the fiduciary's powers are concerned, as personal property and may be so administered (Beutel's Estate, 45 D. & C. 418-1942, aff. 347 Pa. 237-1943; and see McGlinn's Estate, 334 

Pa. 411-1939). 

§ 170-K. Powers of Foreign Fiduciaries. 

This term includes personal representatives, guardians of minors and testamentary trustees appointed under the laws of the jurisdiction in which the decedent was domiciled at the time of his death or in which the minor is domiciled, and not also appointed under the laws of this Commonwealth. With respect to powers over real estate, the law is substantially the same under the Fiduciaries 

Act of 1917, P.L. 447, § 58 (d), 20 P.S. Ch. 3, App. 996, as it is under § 1101 of the new Fiduciaries Act of 1949 as amended, 20 P.S. 320.1101, except as pointed out in the comment to the new Act, "The 1917 act lists the powers granted." whereas the new Act (§ 1101) "gives general powers and then lists the limitations." This is undoubtedly a more convenient arrangement. In general, therefore, it may be said that a foreign fiduciary may exercise all powers of a local fiduciary subject to conditions and limitations with regard to instituting proceedings imposed on non-resident suitors generally (as e.g., bond for costs, etc.), but may not act where there is an ancillary administration in this Commonwealth. However, before a foreign fiduciary may act he must file with the register of wills in the county where the land lies: (1) an exemplified copy of the will or other instrument, if any, in pursuance of which he has been appointed or qualified, and when he is an executor, administrator c.t.a., testamentary trustee or testamentary guardian and wishes to exercise a power granted by will with respect to Pennsylvania real estate, the will must be probated in Pennsylvania; (2) an affidavit to the effect that, after diligent search and inquiry, the estate of which he is fiduciary is not indebted to any person in this Common-

Fid. Act 205 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

wealth, and that he will not exercise any power which he would not be permitted to exercise in the jurisdiction of his appointment. For an excellent discussion of the powers under the old law, see Judge Braham's opinion in Bradley v. Burke, 67 D. & C. 239-1948.

The new Fiduciaries Act of 1949 adds a new condition by § 1101 (3), 20 P.S. 320.1101(3), that "when the foreign fiduciary is a personal representative or a trustee under the will of a nonresident decedent, he shall not exercise any of his powers within the Commonwealth for one month after the decedent's death." The purpose of this requirement, according to the comment, is to afford Pennsylvania creditors a limited time to take out ancillary letters, because debts not of record are no longer liens against a bona fide purchaser for value. Under the old law, it will be remembered, they were liens for one year from date of death.

Also the new Act, by subdivision (4) of the same §, requires that when a foreign fiduciary exercises a power to sell or mortgage any Pennsylvania real estate, all local and State taxes must be paid or provided for. This, the comment states, is analogous to the requirement of a waiver from the Pennsylvania Department of Revenue before the transfer of stock.

New. Power of Administrators and Executors to Ad minister, Lease and Dispose of a Decedent's Real 

Estate Under the New Fiduciaries Act of 1949. 

Foreword. 

Prior to the Fiduciaries Act of 1949, 20 P.S. 320.101 

I et seq., effective January 1, 1950 as to decedents dying on and after that date so far as it relates to powers over real estate, the general rule was that an administrator I was appointed to administer only the personal property of a decedent, which he was authorized to take into possession, convert into cash, pay the costs of administration, inheritance taxes and debts, and was then required to account for and distribute the balance to the parties

1949 Fid. Act entitled under the intestate laws. Real estate of a decedent descended to his heirs, and his administrators had no authority over it except upon application to the Or phans' Court under § 16 of the Fiduciaries Act of 1917, P.L. 447, 20 P.S. Ch. 3, App. 551, regulating sale for payment of debts, etc. (Colison Estate, 356 Pa. 531-1947).

While lands were assets for payment of debts, they were not assets in the hands of the administrator until made so by an order of court (Bishop Estate, 63 D. & C. 2981948). Executors and administrators c.t.a. had, of course, such additional powers as were given in the will. 

The new Fiduciaries Act of 1949, 20 P.S. 320.101, uses the term "personal representative," which it defines to mean an executor or administrator of any description. 

Personal Representative's Powers under New Act. § 

501, 20 P.S. 320.501, provides: "A personal representative shall have the right to and take possession of, maintain and administer all the real and personal estate of the decedent, except real estate occupied by an heir or devisee. He shall collect the rents and income from each asset in his possession until it is sold or distributed, and during the administration of the estate, shall have the right to maintain any action with respect to it and shall make all reasonable expenditures to preserve it. The court may direct the personal representative to take possession of, administer and maintain real estate occupied by an heir or devisee if this is necessary to protect the rights of claimants or other parties. Nothing in this section shall affect the personal representative's power to sell real estate occupied by an heir or devisee." 

The Joint State Government Commission which drafted the new Act comments that this is a distinct departure from existing Pennsylvania law as far as real estate is concerned, "and is based on the premise that the personal representative, except as stated, should have the duty as well as the right to control real estate until it is sold or distributed by decree or until control is relinquished to the heir or devisee because it is not needed

Fid. Act 207 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

for administration. The theory of the drafters has been that the legal and equitable title to real estate passes to heirs or devisees as heretofore, but that real estate should be administered [in the same way] as personal property, with a few minor exceptions when the nature of real estate requires a different treatment." It is not contemplated that rent should be collected by the personal representative from real estate occupied by heirs or devisees unless needed for payment of claims, but it can be sold under the general power given by the Act unless specifically devised, in which case a court order must first be obtained (§ 543, 20 P.S. 320.543. See infra).

The Fiduciaries Act of 1949 has been held not to change the substantive law that a decedent's real estate devolves upon his heirs or devisees, although it is now subject to additional powers granted to the personal representatives who, however, have no right or duty to take possession of or collect rents on real estate occupied by an heir or devisee, under § 501 of the Act (20 P.S. 

320.501), except upon order of the court (Brown, Executrix v. Bailey, 84 D. & C. 269—1952). § 502 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.502, provides that the court may authorize the personal representative to renounce his right to administer property of no value to the estate, in which event full legal and equitable title will be in those beneficially interested in the asset, subject, of course, to their right to disclaim also (Roop v. 

Greenfield, 352 Pa. 232-1945; Reynolds v. Cridge, 131 

Pa. 189-1890. See also Minner v. Pittsburgh, 363 Pa. 199-1949). § 503, 20 P.S. 320.503, authorizes the personal representative to protect himself, his employes and the bene- Ificiaries from liability to third persons by liability in- #surance at the expense of the estate. (See Anderson 

Estate, 77 D. & C. 74-1951.) 1 

Power to Sell. § 541, 20 P.S. 320.541, provides: "Ex- 

@cept as otherwise provided by the will, if any, the per- $sonal representative may sell, at public or private sale

1949 Fid. Act any personal property whether specifically bequeathed or not, and any real property not specifically devised. 

When the personal representative has been required to give bond, no proceeds of real estate shall be paid to him until the court has made an order excusing him from entering additional security or requiring additional security and in the latter event, only after he has entered the additional security." (Italics supplied) This is a radical change from the prior law, under which no real estate could be sold except by order of the court unless the power to do so was given by will. 

But the right of the executor or administrator to sell real estate under the new Act is no more unqualified than his right to sell personal property (as to which see Minichello Estate, 368 Pa. 639, 644-1951). So, where sale is not necessary for payment of debts, taxes or administration expenses, nor for distribution, the personal representative may not sell real estate against the objection of a distributee who offers to take it at a value satisfactory to the other distributees; and the power of the Orphans' 

Court, under § 545 of the Act (20 P.S. 320.545), to restrain such a sale should be exercised (Polanco Estate (No. 1), 80 D. & C. 436-1953; and see Lehman Estate, 2 D. & C. 2nd 276-1955, and Anderson Estate, 77 D. & 

C. 74, 79-1951).

Family Exemption. 

Under the new Fiduciaries Act as well as under the old, the widow's or family exemption ($750.00 under the new Act, $500.00 under the old) takes precedence over all debts except cost of administration, and when selected out of real estate, even over all creditors whose claims were liens at the time of decedent's death, mortgages and purchase money liens alone excepted. See discussion on pages 141-2, 201 of this supplement. It follows also that the exemption supersedes the personal representative's right to sell the real estate claimed if the

Fid. Act 209 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

claim is made before such real estate is actually sold (Fiduciary Review, Sept. 1950, page 4); and this, even though the property claimed is needed to pay debts of the decedent (Koss Estate, 59 D. & C. 308-1947). But the personal representative may except to an undervaluation of the property claimed (Witt Estate, 14 Som. 273 —1949; cf. Matheny Estate, 164 Pa. Super. Ct. 18—1949).

The real estate claimed must be valued as of the date of the claim and not as of the date of decedent's death. 

This is the rule under the old law (Stokes Estate, 167 Pa. Super. Ct. 128-1950) as well as under the new (§ 

213 (a), Fiduciaries Act of 1949, 20 P.S. 320.213(a)), and real estate specifically devised may be claimed (Stachnick 

Estate, 376 Pa. 592—1954).

Power as to Real Estate Specifically Devised. 

Where there is a will which contains no power of sale and the personal representative desires to sell property specifically devised, he must obtain an order of the court under § 543 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.543, which provides: "When the personal representative is not authorized to do so by this act or is not authorized or is denied the power to do so by the will, if any, or when it is advisable that a sale have the effect of a judicial sale, he may sell any real or personal property of the decedent, including property specifically devised, at public or private sale, or may pledge, mortgage, lease or exchange any such property, or grant an option for the sale, lease, or exchange of any such property, under order of the orphans' court of the county where letters testamentary or of administration were granted, upon such terms and upon such security and after such notice as the court shall direct, whenever the court shall find such sale, pledge, mortgage, lease, exchange, or option to be desirable for the proper administration and distribution of the estate." (See Klingerman Estate, 81 D. & C. 397—1952; Biddle 

Estate, 74 D. & C. 470—1950.)

1949 Fid. Act Additional Control of the Court. 

In addition to the above provision, § 545, 20 P.S. 320.- 

545, gives the Orphans' Court discretionary power, on its own motion or upon application of any party in interest, to restrain a personal representative from making a sale where no authority to do so is given by will, or from carrying out any contract of sale. The order may be conditioned upon the applicant giving bond for protection of the parties in interest who might be prejudiced thereby. The restraining order will not be effective against a bona fide grantee or holder of a lien unless the decree or a certified copy thereof is recorded in the Recorder of Deeds' office in the county where the real estate lies, before the recording or entering of the instrument or lien under which the grantee or lienholder claims. (See Polanco Estate (No. 1), 80 D. & C. 4761953.) §§ 541 and 545 seem to recognize the danger of committing to an administrator the unrestricted power to sell at any price at private sale by requiring him to secure an order excusing him from entering additional security or requiring him to enter additional security in the amount fixed, and § 545 empowers the court of its own motion to restrain the sale. In order to effectuate this protection the Philadelphia Orphans' Court has adopted a local rule (No. 130.6, effective January 1, 1955) which requires a personal representative who is under bond, to furnish the court, in a petition under $ 541 of the Act for a decree to fix the amount of, or waive, additional security, complete information including, inter alia, a full description of the real estate sold, name and address of purchaser, price to be paid, terms, etc., a copy of the agreement of sale, a list of liens known, including mortgages, delinquent taxes, judgments, etc., the names of the parties in interest and whether sui juris (the same to be given notice or join in the petition), and the inventory as well as the assessed value of the property.

Fid. Act 211 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

Upon filing the petition, the court, by preliminary decree, appoints an appraiser of its own choosing, and upon his report makes final decree granting or refusing the petition.

This Philadelphia rule operates to protect parties in interest by insuring notice to them of the sale so that they can, before it is too late, ask the court to exercise its power under § 545 to restrain an improvident sale. 

So, too, creditors are protected both by the information requirements, especially as to the assessed value, and by the impartial appraisal. 

While this rule will furnish adequate protection in the case where a personal representative under bond must apply to the Orphans' Court before he can collect the purchase price, in counties where no such rule has been adopted or in cases where application is not required, as, for example, an executor, where the will contains no limitation of the power to sell given by the Act, there is no such protection. Nor does it afford protection in the case of a corporate fiduciary of this State which, under § 323, is not required to enter a bond, or even a foreign corporate fiduciary where the Register of Wills does not I require a bond (see § 323 (a), 20 P.S. 320.323(a)); or under certain circumstances, a non-resident executor (§ 323 (c), 20 P.S. 320.323(c)).

It is true that § 545 gives the court power to restrain in all cases; but such restraint is expressly made ineffective as to the purchaser unless the restraining order is made and recorded before the deed, etc. While the court has power to restrain of its own motion, it is not likely that the situation will be brought to the attention of the court in time to act unless the parties in interest, including creditors, be given notice. No such notice is required by the Act—a grave omission. Of course, honorable or responsible executors and corporate fiduciaries would not exercise this power of sale without giving full notice to everyone concerned; but what of the others?

The right to surcharge may prove an inadequate remedy

1949 Fid. Act in cases where an impecunious executor deliberately or negligently sells for an inadequate price. Nor is it any answer to say that the situation was the same under the old law where an executor was given full power to sell by will, because under the old law the creditors had a lien for one year from death, and the parties in interest, usually because of such creditors' liens, had the same time in which to learn what the executor intended doing. Now there is no such protection should an executor sell to a favored purchaser as soon as he qualifies and before some heirs (e.g., non-residents) even know they are heirs. 

Perhaps the several Orphans' Courts, if they have the power, may by rule require notice in this class of cases also, so as to bridge this omission. 

Power to lease. See new § 170-J, page 203 of this supplement, supra, where this subject and § 542 of the Fiduciaries Act of 1949, 20 P.S. 320.542, are discussed. 

Power Given in Will. $ 544 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.544, construes the effect of powers given in a will. This section provides: "A testamentary power to sell, unless expressly restricted, shall include the power to sell at public or private sale or to mortgage for any purpose of administration or distribution, but shall not include the right to grant an option without court order. A private sale may be made, with court approval, under the provisions of this act although the will has directed a public sale." This is substantially the same as under the prior law. The section further provides that such power to sell, pledge, mortgage, lease or exchange, not given to any person by name or description, shall be deemed to have been given to the personal representative and may be exercised without court approval. This provision obviates the court approval necessary under prior law. 

But the new section also provides that when the personal representative has been required to give bond, he cannot receive the proceeds of sale unless he applies for a court order under § 541, supra.

Fid. Act 213 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

It may be wondered, since plenary power to sell is given under § 541, why the need of § 544, and why draftsmen of wills in the future should bother to include powers of sale. James G. Schmidt, Esq., Vice President of Com monwealth Land Title Insurance Co. (of Philadelphia), adequately answered this question in his address to the 

Pennsylvania Bar Association in January, 1950 (Penna. 

Bar Assn. Annual Report for 1950, pages 470-477), by pointing out that a testamentary power can be drafted to include an unrestricted power to lease or to grant an option, and would, of course, include power to mortgage; and moreover, the exercise of such power would not be subject to the power of restraint given to the court by § 545 (see McClure Estate, 81 D. & C. 295—1953).

Kind of Title Personal Representative Can Give. 

The kind of title which a purchaser acquires by a conveyance under § 541 is fixed by § 547 of the Act, 20 P.S. 

320.547. It is declared to be the full title of decedent, clear and discharged from the lien of legacies, liabilities for all debts and obligations of decedent, all liabilities incident to the administration of decedent's estate, and all claims of distributees and of persons claiming in their right. This also applies to sales under testamentary powers and sales pursuant to court decree, except that sales pursuant to a decree under § 543 also divest liens of record at the time of decedent's death, but not mortgages, whose liens are preserved from discharge in judicial sales under existing law, unless the holder of the mortgage consents in writing filed in the proceeding in which such sale is ordered. Nor is there any obligation on the part of a purchaser to see to the proper application of the purchase price. Nor is a bona fide purchaser to be prejudiced by the terms of any will or codicil probated thereafter nor by the subsequent revocation of the letters of the personal representative. 

While this results in divesting a judgment lien entered

1949 Fid. Act against a devisee, the lien is transferred to the proceeds of the sale. So, where the executor sold at private sale and without order of court, and before paying debts or filing his account and having it audited, and distributed from the proceeds of the sale a sum of money to the devisee as his entire share, in the face of notice to the executor of the judgment creditor's lien, the executor was surcharged (Adamo Estate, 82 D. & C. 222-1952; and see December 1952 Fiduciary Review, page 1, for analysis of the case).

Title of and Conveyance by Heirs and Devisees. 

§ 104 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.104, declares: "Legal title to all real estate of a decedent shall pass at his death to his heirs or devisees, subject, however, to all the powers granted to the personal representative by this act and lawfully by the will and to all orders of the court.' 

It has been held that under these provisions, while exercise of the powers by a personal representative may affect the title of the devisee, until such powers are exercised, title to the real estate remains in the devisee, who, when he was in possession at the time of death, may convey good title where no application has been made by the personal representative for an order to take possession and there is no necessity for any such order (Brown, Executrix v. Bailey, 84 D. & C. 269-1952). 

Distribution of Real Estate on an Accounting.

Where the personal representative has not seen fit to exercise his power of sale, the court may, under § 734, 20 P.S. 320.734, at the audit of the personal representative's account, either partition, direct sale of, or award the real estate in kind to the heirs or devisees. This, in the normal course of procedure, is followed by a schedule or decree of distribution, which should contain a description and recital of decedent's title of the real estate awarded in kind, which, when recorded under § 736, 20

Fid. Act 215 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

P.S. 320.736, in the county where the land lies, would seem to be all that is necessary to vest title in the heirs or devisees (see address of Judge Hunter, of Philadelphia 

Orphans' Court, to the Pennsylvania Bar Association, July, 1950; and see also Polanco Estate (No. 1), 80 D. & C. 436-1953).

Where the personalty is insufficient for payment of debts, and the personal representative has not filed his account, the Court of Common Pleas has no jurisdiction on a complaint in equity to partition real estate, such jurisdiction being exclusively in the Orphans' Court under the Intestate Act of 1947 and the Fiduciaries Act of 

1949 (Riggio, Jr. v. Riggio, 82 D. & C. 150—1952). 

Distribution without an Account. 

A personal representative, after one year from date of advertisement of the grant of letters, under § 732 as amended, 20 P.S. 320.732, if he chooses, but at his own risk, may make distribution of real estate to the heirs or devisees without filing an account, free of the rights of claimants who have not given notice of their claims. 

Where more than one year has elapsed from the date of decedent's death and no letters issued in Pennsylvania are in effect, the heirs or devisees can convey the real estate clear of claims of creditors (§ 615 (1), 20 P.S. 

320.615(1)). 

Also, under § 615 (2), 20 P.S. 320.615(2), if the heirs or devisees convey within the year, and no letters are issued within said year, then after the expiration of the year from decedent's death, the title of the purchaser will be clear of the creditors' claims. But as explained on pages 186-9 of this supplement, the heirs and devisees in either case would remain liable for the value of the property sold, if it should be needed for payment of creditors, unless title is confirmed as set forth in the next paragraph.

1949 Fid. Act Confirmation of Title in Heirs and Devisees Where No Personal Representative is Appointed or No Account is 

Filed.

Under § 756 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.756, a procedure is provided by which heirs and devisees, after one year, where no letters have been granted, and after six years where granted but the personal representative has filed no account, may have their title confirmed in them. Such decree, when recorded and indexed in the county where the land lies, operates to confirm title in them, discharged of all decedent's debts not liens of record at the time of the decree.

Real Estate Specifically Devised. 

While the personal representative cannot sell real estate specifically devised without an order of court, he may administer it under § 501, 20 P.S. 320.501, if not occupied by such heir or devisee, though under § 735, 20 P.S. 320.735, if no claimants or distributees would be prejudiced, the court may order possession delivered to the devisee (see Brown, Extrx. v. Bailey, 84 D. & C. 269 -1952). If sale is desired in such a case before the expiration of one year from decedent's death, in the absence of an order of sale by the court, the specific devisee and personal representative must both join in a deed in order to make good title.

Recording Proceedings in County Where Real Estate Lies. 

§ 549 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.549, provides: "Certified copies of proceedings of any court of the Commonwealth relating to or affecting the real estate of any decedent may be recorded in the office for the recording of deeds in any county in which the real estate lies." 

The new Act is not too clear in the sections of Article V, other than $ 543, as to the court of what county the various applications must be made. The court having jurisdiction of the administration? Or the court of the county where the land lies? Presumably the former, but

Fid. Act 217 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

even that rests on the implication of convenience. § 102 (2), 20 P.S. 320.102(2), merely defines the word "court" to mean "the orphans' court having jurisdiction," which, of course, gives no inkling at all to the legislative intent. 

Trustees-Power to Sell, Etc. Under New Fiduciaries Act. 

In general most of the powers given to trustees with respect to real estate are substantially the same as those conferred on personal representatives. § 961 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.961, gives a trustee power to sell real estate at public or private sale, unless otherwise provided in the trust instrument, under the same limitation, if trustee is under bond, viz., that the court excuse or direct additional security as in the case of personal representatives under bond. § 962, 20 P.S. 320.962, confers power to lease except as otherwise provided in the trust instrument (see supra, § 170-J (b), page 203 of this supplement for discussion of details). Note that this section applies only to testamentary and irrevocable trusts becoming effective on or after January 1, 1950.

Sale by Order of Court. 

§ 963, 20 P.S. 320.963, provides that where the trustee is not authorized to do so by the Act, or is denied the power to do so by the trust instrument, or when a judicial sale is deemed advisable, he may sell for "any purpose of administration or distribution" or may mortgage, lease or exchange real estate or grant an option, under order of court, after such notice as the court may direct, etc. 

The option provisions of this section apply only to testamentary and irrevocable trusts effective on or after January 1, 1950.

Construction of Power Granted in Trust Instrument. 

$ 964, 20 P.S. 320.964, provides that a power given in a trust instrument, unless expressly restricted, includes power to sell at public or private sale or to mortgage for any purpose of administration or distribution, but not

1949 Fid. Act to grant an option without court order. The court is empowered to approve a private sale although the trust instrument directs a public sale. When a power is not given to any person by name or description, it may be exercised by the trustee without court approval; but if the trustee is under bond, a court order must be secured excusing or requiring additional security before the proceeds of such sale can be collected. § 965, 20 P.S. 320.965, grants to the court substantially the same power to restrain a trustee's sale as it has over personal representatives under § 545 (reviewed at pages 

210-2 of this supplement).

Title of Purchaser. 

§ 967, 20 P.S. 320.967, assures the purchaser of the full title of the trust, without obligation to see to the application of the purchase money, etc., substantially the same as provided in case of a personal representative's conveyance under § 547 (see page 213 of this supplement). 

Distribution in Kind. § 983 (9), 20 P.S. 320.983 (9), authorizes distribution in kind of trust real estate under court order, as in § 734 (see page 214 of this supplement).

Application of Act to Existing Trusts. 

§ 105 (2), 20 P.S. 320.105(2), provides that §§ 961 

(power to sell) and 963 (orders of court as to options), apply to testamentary trusts and irrevocable trusts only if becoming effective on or after January 1, 1950, but apply to revocable trusts whenever created. For further discussion of the applicability of the new Act and changes worked by it in powers of trustees, see Fiduciary Review of January 1950, page 1.

Guardians' Powers to Sell, Lease, Etc. 

Small Estates. Natural Guardian. 

§ 1001, 20 P.S. 320.1001, provides that when the entire estate of a minor, whether resident or non-resident, has

Fid. Act 219 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

a net value of $1,000 or less, the court may award the estate to the minor or to the parent or other person maintaining the minor, without appointment of a guardian or entry of security, and may direct the sale or mortgage of the minor's real estate by the parent or other person maintaining the minor as "natural guardian.' 

Appointed Guardians. 

Appointed guardians are those appointed: (1) by the court of the county where the minor resides; or, if the minor resides outside of the state, by the court of the county having jurisdiction of the estate from which the minor's estate is derived, or by the court of the county where an asset of such minor's estate is located (§ 1011, 20 P.S. 320.1011); (2) appointed by a testator in his will pursuant to § 18 (b) of the Wills Act of 1947, 20 P.S. 

180.18(b), known as testamentary guardians; or (3) appointed by an intervivos instrument pursuant to the Act of 1945, P.L. 253, 20 P.S. 1178. The former (i.e., 1) are required to enter security, except corporate fiduciaries unless the court so requires. The latter, (2) and (3), need not enter security unless the court requires it (see § 1022, 20 P.S. 320.1022). § 1063 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.1063, provides that a court appointed guardian may grant an option, sell, mortgage, lease or exchange real estate only under order of the court. He has no such broad power to sell, mortgage, exchange, etc. which a personal representative or a trustee may exercise. It may be noted that guardians may also present petitions under the Revised Price Act as heretofore. There is a question whether the authority given by this section extends to cases where the minor has only an undivided interest in real estate and does not own the whole property. The doubt arises from the failure of the 

Act to expressly mention undivided interests, as the Revised Price Act does. In view of the fact that undivided interests are seldom leased and rarely mortgaged, it may be argued also that the coupling of the words "sell" with mortgage and "lease" indicates that the legislature

1949 Fid. Act had in mind property owned solely by the minor and not property owned in common. The Revised Price Act of 

July 1, 1937, P.L. 2659, however (see § 2 (a) 7, 20 P.S. 

1563 (a) (7)), in express terms extends the authority of the court to undivided interests, and for this reason it would be advisable, until the question is decided, to restrict applications under § 1063 of the new Fiduciaries Act to cases where the minor owns the whole property, and to make applications to sell undivided interests under the Revised Price Act as heretofore. 

Lease Without Approval by Court. 

The extent to which a guardian may lease the minor's real estate without approval of the court has been discussed hereinbefore (see § 170-J (b), pages 203-5 of this supplement). § 1066, 20 P.S. 320.1066, provides for the quality of title from a guardian acting pursuant to the Act in substantially the same language as in the case of trustees (see $967 of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.967, and page 218 of this supplement).

Foreign Fiduciaries. 

Powers of sale of foreign fiduciaries have been hereinbefore considered (see § 170-K at pages 204-5 of this supplement). 

Absentees and Presumed Decedents.

Problems often arise where owners of property disappear and it is not known whether they are living or dead. 

Other owners are sometimes unavoidably absent, and are in situations where they cannot be reached, as, e.g., during the last war, when many were enmeshed in the wave of the war in Europe, some in concentration camps and others where communications were interrupted or impossible. Meanwhile, their real estate might be neglected where they were sole owners, and desirable sales where they were co-owners prevented. §§ 1201 to 1205, 20 P.S

§ 

221 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

320.1201 et seq., provide a procedure whereby the property of an absentee who was a former resident of the Commonwealth, or a nonresident having property within the Commonwealth, after one year's absence without being heard of, can be protected by the appointment of a trustee to take charge of and care for his property. 

But court approval is required to sell or lease the absentee's property. After seven years' absence, or a shorter period if the absentee has been exposed to specific peril of death, a decree may be obtained from the court establishing the death; and then the real and personal estate is administered and distributed by personal representatives as in the case of other decedents, except that the court, in making distribution, must require refunding bonds. (See, for examples, Ruben Estate, 87 

D. & C. 78-1953; Kleinhofer Estate, 68 Montg. 1241952; In re Ottmiller, 3 Lycom. 60—1951.)

PART V. 

SEARCHES. REAL ESTATE BROKERS. 

SETTLEMENTS, ETC. 

CHAPTER XII. 

SEARCHES. 

§ 172. How Records Are Indexed. 

PAGE 424 Tioga Trust Co. v. Home Owners Loan Corp., 42 D. 

& C. 165–1941, holds that a judgment creditor, if he desires to bind a particular asset of the debtor against a future purchaser or mortgagee, has the burden to see that his judgment is recorded and indexed in the same name as that in which the asset is registered. Thus, a judgment entered and indexed against Margaret E.

§ 172 

Kelley is not superior in lien to a subsequent mortgage given by Margaret Kelly, the judgment debtor, where she held title to the mortgaged property in the latter name.

However, the Supreme Court, in Department of Public 

Assistance v. Reustle, 358 Pa. 111-1948, held differently from the foregoing case and ruled, following Coral Gables

Inc. v. Kerl, 334 Pa. 441-1939 (cited in the original text), that the omission of the middle initial, where the first and surname were identical, would not ordinarily divert the title searcher's inquiry until he had satisfied himself that such entry did not relate to the person as to whom he was making the search, especially where the name was an uncommon one. So a judgment indexed against Rosie Reustle was held sufficient constructive notice to bind property conveyed thereafter in the name of 

Rosie C. Reustle. See Mr. Justice (now Chief Justice) 

Stern's opinion discussing the earlier cases. 

But a judgment correctly entered against "Nikolai 

Borys" but incorrectly indexed "Mikola Borys" was held not binding against an innocent purchaser of the property for value who acquired title after entry of the judgment, because judgments against persons having a first or given name beginning with the letter "M" are recorded in a different part of the index from judgments entered against persons having a first or given name beginning with the letter "N." Nor could the judgment creditor plead the error of the clerk, because it was the creditor's duty to see that his judgment was correctly indexed (Jaczyszyn v. Paslawski, 147 Pa. Super. Ct. 971942). See also Russeck v. Shapiro, 170 Pa. Super. Ct. 

89-1951, holding that a mortgage by Abraham Wagner on real estate owned by him in that name was not subject to a prior judgment against the same individual but entered and indexed against him as Albert Wagner.

§ 175-A 223

§ 175. Search at Registry Bureau. 

PAGE 426 

The Act of April 22, 1915, P.L. 172, formerly 53 P.S. 

8721, et seq., (cited in the original text) providing for registration of deeds in cities of the second class, was re pealed by the Act of July 14, 1935, P.L. 1014 (No. 331). 

The Act of May 16, 1921, P.L. 649, 21 P.S. 325, repealed and supplied by the Act of July 18, 1941, P.L. 

421, 21 P.S. 325.1, requires the Recorder of Deeds in the several counties to accept and record all deeds duly acknowledged; and where the deed is required to be registered in any office in the county other than the recorder's office, the duty is imposed on the recorder to have it registered before returning the recorded deed to the party entitled. For this service the recorder is entitled to a fee of 25 cents for first description and 10 cents for each additional description.

New. § 175-A. Lien Priority Law. 

The Lien Priority Law of June 28, 1951, P.L. 927, 68 

P.S. 601 et seq., applicable to liens created on real estate on and after September 1, 1951, gathered together the rules governing priority of liens which had previously been scattered throughout various separate statutes. 

While the new Act merely restates the previous statutory law in some respects (such as the priority of purchase money mortgages), it also codifies other rules of priority which had developed through court decisions (such as for verdicts). In general, § 2 of the Act, 68 P.S. 602, provides that liens shall have priority according to their respective times of entry or deposit for record. More specifically, it provides that "liens against real property shall have priority over each other on the following basis: (1) Purchase money mortgages, from the time they are delivered to the mortgagee, if they are recorded within thirty days after their date; otherwise from the time they are left for record.

§ 176 

(2) Other mortgages and defeasible deeds in the nature of mortgages, from the time they are left for record. (3) Verdicts for a specific sum of money, from the time they are recorded by the court. (4) Adverse judgments, orders and decrees, from the time they are rendered. (5) Amicable judgments, from the time the instruments on which they are entered are left for entry. (6) Writs which when issued and indexed by the prothonotary create liens against real property, from the time they are issued. (7) Other instruments which when entered or filed and indexed in the prothonotary's office create liens against real property, from the time they are left for entry or filing. 

$ 4 (b) of the Act, 68 P.S. 601, note, provides that the 

Act shall not affect the priority of any lien "which now, by law, has priority to all other liens;" nor affect any provision of the Judgment Lien Law of July 3, 1947, 

P.L. 1234, 12 P.S. 877 et seq.

Such effects as the Lien Priority Law may have upon searches for various liens and encumbrances will be considered and discussed hereinafter under the appropriate headings.

Beginning at the bottom of page 431, the original text read, "Whether a mortgage on seated lands is protected rom discharge on a treasurer's tax sale, held under the Act of 1913 and its supplements, is rendered uncertain by he City of Erie case, as the Act of 1929 is given only a passing reference in the Court's opinion in that case.

Fort Pitt Chemical Co., 345 Pa. 471-1942, which expressly ruled that the Act of April 30, 1929, P.L. 874, 21 P.S. 651, did not by implication repeal § 31 of the

Harrisburg, 314 Pa. 70-1934). Without statutory authority, however, taxes accruing after a mortgage, though superior in payment, are not superior _n point of time, and hence would not discharge such mortgage on seated lands, although on unseated lands, such mortgage would be discharged (idem).

§ 176-A. Encumbrance Search (Inheritance Taxes). 

(a) Federal Inheritance Tax.

PAGE 433 The Act of Congress (as amended to October 21, 1942), c. 619, 53 Stat. 128, 26 U.S.C.A., 1940 Ed. § 827, cited in the original text, providing for a lien of Federal Inheritance (i.e., Estate) Taxes on the gross estate of a decedent for ten years after his decease, has been carried over into the Internal Revenue Code of 1954 (Act of 

August 16, 1954, c. 736, 68A Stat. 780, 26 U.S.C.A., 1955 

Ed., § 6324 (a) (1)), without substantial change. 

In Decker Estate (No. 2), 355 Pa. 476-1947, it was held on reargument (see 355 Pa. 331-1946) that the lien of federal estate taxes provided for in 53 Stat. 128, 448449, 26 U.S.C.A., §§ 3670-3672, attaches only at the decedent's death, and that although it is a general lien on decedent's gross estate, it does not take priority over pre-existing mortgages or pledges the liens of which attached in decedent's lifetime. 

But in Detroit Bank v. U.S., 317 U.S. 329-1943, it was held that the lien of the estate tax was superior to that of a mortgage given for full value by the survivor of a tenancy by the entireties, not only as to the interest of the decedent which accrued by death, but as against the interest which the survivor owned. This case also sup-

§ 176-A(a)(3) 227 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

ports the ruling of the General Counsel for the Treasury Department (cited at the bottom of page 433 of the original text) by holding that the lien of federal estate tax under § 315 (a) of the Revenue Act of 1926 (which corresponds to the ten year lien provisions under the Acts of 1942 and 1954) is entirely separate from and independent of the lien under § 3186, Revised Statutes (which requires filing or recording of notice of the lien in order to give it validity as against a prior mortgage, etc.), and is superior to the lien of such a mortgage, even though no notice of the tax lien is filed or recorded. PAGES 434-436 

(3) Release or Discharge of the Lien. 

Under the 1954 Internal Revenue Code, the Secretary of the Treasury or his delegate (i.e., the Director of Internal Revenue) may issue a certificate of release of any lien imposed with respect to any internal revenue tax (including estate tax), if the liability for the tax and interest "has been fully satisfied or provided for," or where a proper surety bond is furnished, or where the fair market value of the remaining property in the estate subject to the lien is at least twice the amount of the unsatisfied tax liability and all other prior liens (Act of 

August 16, 1954, c. 736, 68A Stat. 781, 26 U.S.C.A., 1955 

Ed., § 6325). 

The Act of March 4, 1931, c. 515, 46 Stat. 1528 and sup plements, formerly 28 U.S.C.A., 1940 Ed. §§ 901, 902, 

904 and 905 (cited on pages 435–6 of the original text), has been repealed and is supplied by the Act of June 25, 1948, c. 646, 62 Stat. 972 as amended, 28 U.S.C.A., 1950 

Ed., § 2410, without substantial change, except that the former provision exempting the United States from liability for costs, etc., has been modified by the newer Act (c. 646, 62 Stat. 973, 28 U.S.C.A., 1950 Ed., § 2412) to leave the United States liable for costs when such liability is expressly provided by Act of Congress. 

In Michigan v. U.S., 317 U.S. 338-1943, it was held

§ 176-A(b)(1) 

that such lien was also superior to State and City property taxes accruing after death. 

New. U. S. Liens-Redemption. 

The Act of April 20, 1950, 12 U.S.C.A., § 1701-k, pro vides that the right of redemption shall not arise where the subordinate interest of the United States is derived from the issuance of insurance under the National Housing Act, or from issuance of guaranties or insurance un der the Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944. 

(b) State Inheritance Tax. 

(1) Lien of Pennsylvania Inheritance Tax. 

PAGE 437 

Where the Commonwealth fails to file a certificate of nonpayment within five years after the death of a life tenant in accordance with § 39 of the Transfer Inheritance Tax Act of June 20, 1919, P.L. 521, as amended, 72 

P.S. 2443, and the property is sold by the remainderman, the lien against the real estate is lost (Com. v. Cherry's 

Estate, 47 D. & C. 398-1943).

But while the inheritance tax on future estates in real property is not payable until the person liable to pay comes into actual possession, yet it springs into life at the death of the testator and becomes a lien from that instant. Hence the Commonwealth's claim for such tax is entitled to priority in distribution of the proceeds of sale of real property which was devised to the judgment debtor after the death of the life tenant, where the judg ment, though prior in date to the life tenant's death, was subsequent to the death of the testator (McDermott v. 

Jones, 54 D. & C. 9—1945). 

§ 177. Encumbrance Search (Judgment Liens). 

Important Forenote. Read First. Virtually all of the 

Acts cited on pages 439 to 444 of the original text were

§ 177(b) 229 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

repealed, first by the Act of May 28, 1943, P.L. 774, effective on that date, relating to revival of judgment liens, and later by the Judgment Lien Law of July 3, 1947, P.L. 1234, 12 P.S. 877 et seq., which also repealed the

Act of 1943. These two later Acts supplied those repealed, but made a number of changes in the law as it existed under the older Acts. In searching titles, however, since liens, their duration, priority and treatment will be governed by the law as it existed at the time of their creation, the law under the older Acts must still be considered and applied with respect to liens arising prior to the later Acts. Consequently this supplement will first note under the appropriate headings, sections and page numbers of the original text any decisional and statutory changes and interpretations of the former law, after which the new law will be considered. 

1 (b) Duration and Revival of Judgment Liens. 

PAGE 441 

Lien of verdicts. Note that if a plaintiff appeals from a money verdict in his favor, and after reversal secures a larger verdict, the lien thereof does not relate back to the date of entry of the original verdict which was set aside by the appeal; so that a mortgage recorded after the first verdict, but prior to the second, has priority over both (Colonial Trust Co. v. Flanagan, 344 Pa. 556 -1942). 

Correction in Text: The first line of the second para graph on this page should read: "The principal Acts relating to liens and revival of judgments are the following:" 

Under the Act of 1827, a judgment lien is lost as to other judgment creditors whose rights have intervened if the lien is not revived by proceedings begun within the five year period (Dime Bank of Lansford v. Summit 

Hill Trust Co., 341 Pa. 424-1941).

§ 177(b)(1) (1) Lien of Judgment Against the Original Defendant, and His Heirs or Devisees.

PAGE 442 Reviving a judgment in personam to continue its lien does not reduce it to a judgment in rem; so execution may properly issue against a defendant's personal property on the last revived judgment (First Nat. Bank of Scranton v. Jermyn, 63 D. & C. 569-1948; Bache v. Dorris, 81 

D. & C. 461-1952).

Following § 15 (g) of the Fiduciaries Act of 1917 (cited in the last paragraph on page 442 of the original text), see Frank DiBerardino B. & L. Ass'n v. DeGregoria, 158

Pa. Super. Ct. 516-1946. 

The method thus provided for continuing a lien of record which was a lien at the time of a decedent's death by sci. fa. to revive, etc., is exclusive, and an action in assumpsit begun and indexed, etc., as in the case of a "debt not of record," cannot be substituted (Bagaj Estate, 66 D. & C. 512—1948). 

(2) Lien of a Judgment as Against a Purchaser or Mort gagee.

PAGE 443 A terre tenant is defined as one who becomes the owner of an interest in real estate after the lien of a judgment (or a mortgage) has attached. And a revival of the original judgment is not a personal judgment against a terre tenant; it does nothing more than continue the lien of the original judgment existing against the land of the defendant at the time of its conveyance to the terre tenant (Adelson v. Kocher (Hayes et ux, Appellants), 154 

Pa. Super. Ct. 548-1944). 

Under the Act of March 26, 1827, P.L. 129, as amended, 12 P.S. 868, real property owned by the judgment debtor and conveyed by him cannot be sold in execution on the judgment except while the judgment lien is effective as to the terre tenant. Hence, where execution was issued

§ 177(b)(2) 231 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

on such a judgment before the lien expired but the execution sale took place after the expiration of the lien, the sale was invalid as to the terre tenant, since neither the execution nor the levy, of itself, continues or extends the lien of the judgment (Steinhardt v. Russian Orthodox 

Catholic Mutual Aid Society, 366 Pa. 222-1951). But where the sale is of mortgaged property in execution of a judgment on the mortgage bond, the sale is effective, because the judgment relates back to the mortgage, the lien of which continues against the mortgaged property without need of revival (Idem).

PAGE 447 The rules governing the continuance of judgment liens without revival where the land has been conveyed while it was still a lien (see pages 447-8 of original text) were quoted with approval and held to be a correct statement of the law in Simmons v. Simmons, 150 Pa. Super. Ct. 

393, 405, 406-1942, affirmed per curiam, 346 Pa. 521943, on the opinion of Keller, P. J., of the Superior 

Court. (See also Behler, Adrx. v. Loch, 154 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 399-1943.) Nor does it affect the automatic revival of the judgment lien against the land of a terre tenant whose deed was recorded within the five years from date the judgment was entered, that no sci. fa. to revive was issued against the original judgment defendant within five years thereafter (Ellinger v. Krach, 150 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 384-1942, affirmed per curiam, 346 Pa. 52—1943).

New. Change in Law. 

The law summarized in Rules II, III, IV and V, as set forth on pages 447 and 448 of the original text and as confirmed by the cases therein and above cited, was changed by the Act of May 28, 1943, P.L. 774 (effective as of that date), which abrogated the rule that a conveyance within five years from the time a judgment lien was first entered or last revived would, of itself, continue the lien without more against the land held by the terre

§ 177(2) 

tenant for a further period of five years from the date of recording of the deed. The Act of 1943 expressly provides in effect that the lien of a judgment, as to purchasers, mortgagees and other lien creditors, is limited to five years from the date of its entry or revival unless revived within that time, and cannot be otherwise continued except in case of the death of the judgment debtor as to real estate owned by him or conveyed by his deed not recorded in his lifetime. The Acts on which the former rules of revival rested were expressly repealed. 

There was a further provision to the effect that any judgment which was a lien on real estate of a terre tenant only, by virtue of the Act of April 16, 1849, P.L. 663 (that is, the Act which was construed to effect automatic revival for five years from the recording of a deed to a terre tenant, etc.), should continue as a lien for six months only from effective date of the Act or five years from the date of recording of the deed, whichever period is the shorter, unless revived in accordance with the 1943 Act. The purpose of this Act was, of course, to reduce the period of a conveyancer's or title searcher's search from ten years, as formerly customary, to five years. 

This Act of 1943 was expressly repealed but supplied by the Judgment Lien Law of July 3, 1947, P.L. 1234, 12 P.S. 877 et seq., which now regulates judgment liens and prescribes the procedure for their revival and continuance (see below for details of this new Act). The consequence is that judgment liens are limited to five years unless revived by agreement or action commenced and indexed within that time as provided by the Act. 

While the Act excepts from its operation the revival of judgments which were a lien on a decedent's real estate at the time of his death (see § 8, 12 P.S. 884), the new 

Fiduciaries Act of 1949, § 612, 20 P.S. 320.612 (see page 

191 of this supplement), as hereinbefore stated, reduces the continuance of such liens to one year from the date of decedent's death or five years from the entry of the judgment or last revival, whichever period is the longer.

Lien Law 

233 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

New Judgment Lien Law of 1947. 

The Act of July 3, 1947, P. L. 1234, 12 P.S. 877 et seq., known as the "Judgment Lien Law," changes the preexisting law as set forth in the original text (pages 441 et seq.), on this subject, and repeals all of the Acts there cited. However, by § 8, the lien of any judgment entered prior to the effective date of the Act (September 1, 1947) remains unaffected and continues as a valid lien for the same periods as under the old laws but no longer unless revived prior to the expiration of such periods in the manner provided by the new Act. 

Briefly, the Act provides that-Every judgment entered and indexed shall be a lien upon all real property within the county where the judgment is entered, which, at the time of entry and indexing, is owned by the person against whom it is entered, and shall continue a lien for five years and no longer unless revived (§ 2, 12 P.S. 878).

Under § 3, 12 P.S. 879, revival may be accomplished by indexing either-(1) judgment of revival by agreement between plaintiff and defendant, or (2) a writ of scire facias issued out of the court in which the judgment was originally entered and within five years from the date of indexing of the original judgment or the last revival. If any real property bound by the judgment has been ac- Iquired by a terre tenant within the five year period, the judgment shall be a lien against the real estate owned by the terre tenant only if the terre tenant is a party to the revival agreement or scire facias; but if a terre tenant's deed has been recorded during the existence of the judgment lien, the lien must be revived as to the terre tenant within five years from the date of recording of his deed. 

In such event, however, the judgment will bind the terre tenant's real estate only for the same period as it continues against the defendant. To continue the lien thereafter, it must be again revived, as to both the defendant and the terre tenant. But the lien of a judgment in favor of the Commonwealth may be revived by the filing, with

1947 Lien Law the prothonotary in the county in which the judgment was entered and within five years of its entry, a mere suggestion of nonpayment (Act of September 26, 1951, P.L. 1505, 12 P.S. 885. See also pages 344-5 of this supplement). And a lien for personal property taxes in favor of the County, imposed under the Act of June 17, 1913, P.L. 507, § 1, as amended, 72 P.S. 4821, by amendment of 

August 19, 1953, P.L. 1157, 72 P.S. 4881, may now be revived by filing and indexing with the prothonotary of the Court of Common Pleas of the county a suggestion of nonpayment and averment of default, within five years from the entry of the original lien or the last revival thereof, in much the same manner as liens of judgments in favor of the Commonwealth may be revived. 

In case of a judgment against two or more joint defendants, any revival must be against all the defendants unless less than all the joint tenants agree to be bound by the judgment, in which event it will bind only those agreeing.

A writ of scire facias to revive, when indexed, is a lien on all property then owned by the defendant within the county in which the judgment or revival is obtained (§ 

4, 12 P.S. 880).

A person serving in the armed services in time of war is protected. A writ of scire facias may be issued and indexed at any time (in order that the lien may be kept alive), but judgment upon it cannot be entered against him during his period of service or within six months after termination of war (§ 5, 12 P.S. 881). 

Until December 31, 1954, the making of judgment searches will be affected by the Fiduciaries Act of 1917, the Fiduciaries Act of 1949 and the Judgment Lien Law of 1947. The application of these Acts can best be shown by the following example:- 

Judgment entered November 20, 1944 judgment debtor died November 19, 1945-his death revived the judgment until November 18, 1950; real estate was sold by decedent's heirs November 10, 1949-the property being

§ 177(c) 235 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

subject to the judgment, the recording of the deed permits revival against terre tenant for a period of five years following the recording of the deed, or until November 9, 1954, provided it was properly revived against the judgment debtor or his representatives. Therefore, in making searches in June, 1952, to cover a conveyance made by the purchaser from the heirs as above set forth, judgment searches would be made as follows:-(a) against the purchaser (grantor), November 10, 1949 to date; and (b) against the decedent, personal representatives and heirs of decedent, November 19, 1945 to date.

Because of § 3 (a) of the Judgment Lien Law, 12 P.S. 

879, a conveyance no longer works a revival of a judgment for a period of five years from the recording of the deed to a terre tenant; it is necessary that the judgment be revived within five years from its entry or last prior revival, as the case may be. As the Judgment Lien Law became effective September 1, 1947, it may be well to point out that for a period of five years, or until August 31, 1952, appropriate searches must be made for any judgments which might have been revived by deed under the earlier law. 

(c) Discharge of Judgment Liens. 

PAGE 448 Judgments against a decedent's real estate, unless a I purchase money lien or prior to a purchase money lien, are discharged or divested where the real estate is claimed as the family exemption (Koss Estate, 59 D. & C. 

PAGE 449 It should be noted that liens in favor of the Commonwealth are only divested to the extent permitted by statute because of the inherent right of the sovereign to have priority of payment of its obligations (Com. v. 

Udziewicz, 353 Pa. 543, 545-1946). But that case also held that a Treasurer's tax sale divested the lien of a

$ 178 

judgment in favor of the Commonwealth entered pursuant to a contract for reimbursement of old age assistance, since such a judgment was not the result of the exercise of a sovereign prerogative. 

But in Pittsburgh Petition, 376 Pa. 447-1954, it was held that a sale of land by the City Treasurer for unpaid taxes does not divest the Commonwealth's lien for unpaid unemployment compensation, because such contributions are taxes, and in seeking to collect them under its lien, the Commonwealth is exercising a sovereign prerogative; and hence its lien is not divested by the Treasurer's sale in the absence of any statutory divestiture. 

However, a lien by the County for personal property tax under the Act of July 3, 1947, P.L. 1249, amending the Act of June 17, 1913, P.L. 507 (as amended), 72 P.S. 

4881, is divested by a sheriff's sale on foreclosure of a mortgage recorded prior to the passage of the 1947 amendment which gave the lien of such taxes priority over all other liens, etc., the amending Act being held to be prospective and not retroactive (Elmore v. Philadelphia, 179 Pa. Superior Ct. 535-1955).

§ 183. Lien of Decedent's Debts. 

PAGE 459 Judgments that were liens against decedent's real estate at the time of decedent's death, upon distribution of the proceeds of real estate sold for payment of debts, take priority over claims for funeral expenses and claims of general creditors (Dry's Estate, 61 D. & C. 504—1947), and are preferred over costs of administration except such as are connected with the sale (Gulick's Estate, 63 Pitts. 68-1915). Such judgments take priority also over unpaid income taxes accruing after the judgment was entered (Meyer Estate, 159 Pa. Super. Ct. 296-1946; 

Decker's Estate (No. 1), 355 Pa. 331, 341-1946; cert. den. 331 U.S. 807—1947).

Under the law applicable in cases of decedents dying before January 1, 1950, real estate sold within the year of decedent's death remained subject to the lien of decedent's debts, and unless the sale was made by the executor pursuant to a power to sell or unless sold by decree of the Orphans' Court, the liens of decedent's debts were not divested. But unless the creditors preserved their liens by commencing and indexing suit within the year after decedent's death, their liens would be gone after the year expired (Finley's Estate, 309 

Pa. 200-1932; see also Skolnek's Estate, 342 Pa. 49, 52 -1941).

$ 184 

New Law Relating to Lien of Decedent's Debts. 

PAGE 460 The Fiduciaries Act of 1949 appears to carry out the intent of the framers of the Act, that real estate shall be more freely alienable, and lien of debts as charges on real estate, no longer exists as against a bona fide purchaser or lienholder for value. A claim against an individual may still be collected from his estate, and §§ 614 et seq. of the Act, 20 P.S. 320.614, point out the means of preserving claims; but the transfer of real estate is greatly facilitated by the transfer of the claim to the proceeds rather than retaining a lien on the real estate itself (For fuller discussion, see pages 186 et seq. of this supplement). 

§ 184. Mechanics' Liens. 

PAGE 461 or 

New. 2 of the Lien Priority Law of June 28, 1951, P.L. 

927, 68 P.S. 602, provides that liens against real property shall have priority over each other on the basis therein stated. Mechanics' liens, as such, are not mentioned, but might be construed as falling within the sixth or seventh (and last) category of the various types of liens set forth, viz., "(6) Writs which when issued and indexed by the prothonotary create liens against real property," "(7) Other instruments which when entered or filed and indexed in the prothonotary's office create liens against real property." In either of these types, the Act provides that the priority of lien shall be based upon the time of issue (in the case of writs) or the time they are left for entry or filing. If either of these (probably the seventh category, if any) apply to mechanics' liens, it will eliminate the retroactive effect of filing a mechanics' lien as provided for under the Mechanics' Lien Act of 1901 (see original text); and a mechanics' lien claimant who wishes to obtain such a lien will still be able to do so, but his lien will be subject to other liens

§ 186(a) 239 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

created between the time the mechanic's work was commenced and the time when his mechanics' lien is filed. 

It is doubtful, however, that the legislature intended such a result, especially since the Lien Priority Law neither expressly repeals any part of the Mechanics' Lien Act of 1901 as amended nor expressly mentions mechanics' liens; and unless and until the Lien Priority Law is declared by the Pennsylvania Supreme Court to supersede the Mechanics' Lien Act in respect to the priority of mechanics' liens, the title examiner should continue to treat a mechanics' lien as valid and entitled to the priority given to it under the Mechanics' Lien Act. 

As to waiver of the right to file mechanics' liens, see Wood v. U. S. Steel Corp., 383 Pa. 157–1955, holding that any language in a contract with a general contractor that provides with reasonable clarity that no mechanics' lien shall be filed by anyone, is binding on a subcontractor if the contract is properly executed and filed.

New. On January 1, 1948, there became effective a new law relating to collection of delinquent taxes known as the Real Estate Tax Sale Law, being the Act of July 7, 1947, P.L. 1368, 72 P.S. 5860.101 et seq. This law is made applicable to all counties, other than counties of the first class (i.e., Philadelphia) unless the county commissioners of any such county, by the end of January 1948, adopted a resolution electing not to have the Act apply to their county. The Act also applies to municipalities and taxing districts, as limited by the Act, which accept the Act by resolution of City Council or School District. Where this 

Act is applicable, a Tax Claim Bureau is set up which is constituted the agency for collection of delinquent taxes and conduct of sales of real estate therefor. By the Act of May 20, 1949, P.L. 1602, 72 P.S. 5860.102, any county which had elected not to have the Act apply to it, might elect, by resolution of the county commissioners in January of any year, to operate under the Act thereafter. 

The amending Act of July 17, 1953, P.L. 457, 72 P.S. 

5860.102 (pocket part) extends a similar privilege to any city or school district to which the Act might apply but which had not accepted the Act, if it notifies the Tax 

Claim Bureau on or before May 1st of any year that it elects to avail itself of the Act.

By this Act, the taxes levied by any taxing district are declared to be first liens and to have priority on the proceeds of a public sale held under the Act, except costs of the sale and its proceedings, tax liens in favor of the 

Commonwealth, and (by amendment of June 30, 1951, P.L. 991) municipal claims having equal priority with the tax liens. The claim remains a lien on the property until paid or until the property is sold as provided in the Act. 

If the tax is not returned or transferred to the Bureau within the time required by the Act, its lien on the prop-

§ 186(b) 243 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

erty is lost, although the taxes may be recovered by an action in assumpsit at any time within six years after they first became due. Excluded from this Act are counties of the first class (Philadelphia) and cities of the first or second class or second class A. Also excluded are school districts of the first class or within cities of second class A. The Act was further amended by the Act of 

May 6, 1955 (No. 15), in minor details.

A sale held under the Act discharges all liens or estates except ground rents or mortgages which have been recorded before the taxes became liens (Act of July 7, 1947, P.L. 1368, § 609, 72 P.S. 5860.609). But under § 

612, as amended by the Acts of May 20, 1949, P.L. 1579 (No. 477), § 2, and January 18, 1952, P.L. 2098, § 5, 72 

P.S. 5860.612 and 5860.612–1, where the sale is under court order, the purchaser's title is free and clear of all liens, taxes, etc. (including mortgages), except only ground rents. The provisions of the Act as amended, relating to notice of the sale, must be complied with (Ross Appeal, 366 Pa. 100—1950). 

The old law of May 29, 1931, P.L. 280, and its amendments, 72 P.S. 5971a et seq., while repealed, superseded or supplied by the Real Estate Tax Sale Law of 1947 as amended, as to those counties and taxing districts in which a Tax Claim Bureau has been set up, remains in full force as to the counties, municipalities and taxing districts to which the 1947 Act is not applicable. Under the Act of May 29, 1931, the collector is required to make returns not later than the first Monday in May of the year following the levy. The lien of the tax becomes a first lien, and is first payable out of the fund arising out of any judicial sale except costs of the sale and of the writ upon which sale is made, and excepting also liens in favor of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania. A tax sale under this Act is invalid if the notice is not given to the owner as required by the Act (Hess v. Westerwick, 366 Pa. 90-1950; Luther v. Penna. Game Commission,

§ 186-A  

381 Pa. 442-1955). See also Act of May 6, 1955 (No. 

19), amending § 7 of the Act of 1931, P.S. 5971g, as to notice of sale. § 186-A. Tax Searches by the Receiver of Taxes in Philadelphia.

PAGE 463 The Receiver of Taxes (now called the Commissioner of Taxes) in Philadelphia is no longer responsible under his certificate for the accuracy of tax information furnished, but he is required to furnish it without charge (Act of May 11, 1945, P.L. 460, 53 P.S. 4942). 

§ 186-B. State Corporation Taxes. 

PAGE 464 

The Commonwealth's lien for taxes settled against a corporation is superior to a mortgage, even a purchase money mortgage, created by that corporation although the settlement of the taxes was subsequent to the date the mortgage was recorded (Harper v. Consolidated Rubber Co., 284 Pa. 444-1925), and even though they arose subsequently to the mortgage (Com. v. My Pop's Root 

Beer Co., 84 D. & C. 386-1952).

It had been held that a strawman who takes title merely for the purpose of executing a mortgage for a corporation is not a predecessor in title within the meaning of $1401 of the Fiscal Code (72 P.S. 1401), and hence sale under the mortgage would not divest the Commonwealth's lien for corporate taxes (Com. v. City Realty Co., 56 

D. & C. 166-1946; Com. v. Corner Realty Co., 41 D. & C. 

236-1941). But these decisions were overruled by the 

Supreme Court in Commonwealth v. Hoffman-Henon Co., 

382 Pa. 213-1955, which settles the matter to the contrary.

New. § 186-B 1. Judgment Liens of the Commonwealth. 

Liens in favor of the Commonwealth, because of the inherent right of the sovereignty to have priority in pay-

§ 186-C 245 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

ment of its obligation, ordinarily are not divested except to the extent permitted by statute. But where the Department of Public Assistance entered into an agreement with one receiving old age assistance and the Public Assistance Law of 1937, P.L. 2051 as amended, 62 P.S. 2501 et seq., provided for a confession of judgment, the Commonwealth was held not to be dealing in its sovereign capacity, so that its judgment was divested by a tax sale, especially as the Tax Sale Act of May 29, 1931, P.L. 280, § 4, 72 P.S. 5971d, gives the Commonwealth priority in payment out of the proceeds, but does not preserve the lien. Generally speaking, a judicial sale divests all prior and subsequent liens on property sold unless preserved by statute or by nature incapable of discharge (Com. v. Udziewicz, 353 Pa. 543-1946). But unemployment contributions due the Commonwealth under the Unemployment Compensation Law are taxes, and liens therefor are not divested by a treasurer's sale of the debtor's real estate for city taxes (Pittsburgh Petition, 376 Pa. 447-1954). 

Under the Act of September 26, 1951, P.L. 1505, 12 

P.S. 885, a judgment in favor of the Commonwealth may be revived by mere suggestion of nonpayment filed with the prothonotary of the county of original entry within five years. When indexed in the judgment index, after notice to all proper parties by registered mail or advertisement as provided in the Act, and if no objection is filed within fifteen days after such notice is so served, the judgment is revived for five years with the same force and effect as if a sci. fa. to revive had been issued. 

§ 186-C. Federal Income Taxes. 

PAGE 464

§ 186-C  

of 1954, 68A Stat. 779, 26 U.S.C.A., 1955 Ed., §§ 6321, 

6322, with a minor change. The wording in § 6322 has been changed to conform with a change in § 6203 of the Internal Revenue Code of 1954 which provides that assessments shall be made by recording the liability of the taxpayer in accordance with rules and regulations of the 

Secretary of the Treasury.

As to judgment liens by § 3672 of Internal Revenue 

Code, 26 U.S.C.A. 3672, 56 Stat. 957, see Meyer Estate, 159 Pa. Super. Ct. 296-1946. See also Decker Est., No. 

1, 355 Pa. 331-1946, which rules that the federal Act of June 29, 1939, c. 247, 26 U.S.C.A. 3672 (b) (1), preserves the rights of a pledgee which accrued before the attaching of the federal tax lien.

PAGE 465 

53 Stat. 449, amended by 53 Stat. 882, 26 U.S.C.A. 

3672, cited in the original text, providing that federal taxes are not a lien, as against any mortgagee, pledgee, purchaser or judgment creditor, until notice has been filed at the place provided by the law of the state, has been carried over into the Internal Revenue Code of 1954, 68A Stat. 779, 26 U.S.C.A., 1955 Ed., § 6323 (a)(1). 

However, with respect to state, county and city taxes, and municipal claim liens, it has been held that the liens of the federal government for income taxes, though general, nevertheless are on an equal basis with such state and local taxes, etc.; and the principle first in time, first in right, applies. So, where the federal tax lien, though not confined to any specific property, antedated the state, local tax, and municipal claim liens on a property, it took priority (U. S. v. Greenville (U.S.C.C.A. 4), 118 Fed. 2d 

963-1941).

It should be noted that the priority accorded to the federal government by 53 Stat. 448, 449, 26 U.S.C.A.

U.S.C.A., 1955 Ed., §§ 6321, 6322, covers not only unpaid income taxes, but all other taxes due the federal government not otherwise specially provided for. Estate and gift taxes, for example, do carry their own special lien provisions. See ante, § 176-A (a) of the original text and of this supplement.
