§ 188. Examination of Property. 

PAGE 466 For additional recent cases holding that a grantee or mortgagee of real estate is bound by such notice of the rights of a party in possession which an inquiry from him would have elicited, see Malamed v. Sedelsky, 367 Pa. 

353-1951; Geyer v. Thomas, 364 Pa. 242—1950 (where party in possesion claimed title under a resulting trust); 

Atlantic Ref. Co. v. Wyoming Nat. Bank, 356 Pa. 2261947; Sidle v. Kaufman, 345 Pa. 549, 557—1942 (options in lease to party in possession); Kinch v. Fluke, 311

Pa. 405—1933 (mortgagee of vendor held bound by notice of a prior outstanding unrecorded agreement of sale to the party in possession of whom he made no inquiry); 

Most Excellent Assembly, etc. v. Superb Realty Co., 353

Pa. 256-1945 (lease by mortgagee in possession binds purchaser at foreclosure sale). But when the land is occupied by two persons and there is a recorded deed in the name of one of them, such joint possession is not notice of an unrecorded title in the other (Schell v. 

Kneedler, 359 Pa. 424-1948; Overly v. Hixson, 169 Pa. 

Super. Ct. 187-1951).

§ 191 

CHAPTER XIII. 

REAL ESTATE BROKERS, AGENTS AND SALESMEN. 

§ 191. Modern Real Estate Business Now Regulated by Law. 

PAGES 497-8 

While the preparation of leases, deeds, mortgages and other instruments of conveyancing constitutes practicing law (Childs v. Smeltzer, 315 Pa. 9—1934), yet it is, and has long been part of the art of conveyancing; and the statute prohibiting the practice of law by those not members of the bar was construed not to include the preparation of leases, deeds, mortgages and other instruments of conveyancing drawn by a title company or real estate broker, so long as they are drawn in connection with titles insured by it or, in the case of a real estate broker or agent, in connection with properties bought or sold by him, since this is incidental to the business for which they are engaged (LaBrum v. Commonwealth Title Co. 

etc., 358 Pa. 239-1948). 

§ 192. Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929. 

PAGE 499 The 1929 Act was extensively amended by the Act of 

May 25, 1945, P.L. 1023, 63 P.S. 431 et seq., and also by amending Acts of 1947 and 1949 and 1952. These amendments will be noted under the headings of the original text.

New. State Real Estate Commission. 

The duties of the Department of Public Instruction, committed to it by the Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929, have now been largely transferred to a commission in that department, composed of the Superintendent

§ 192(a)249 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

of Public Instruction and five persons appointed by the Governor, which persons must be qualified real estate brokers who have been engaged in the real estate business no less than ten years (Act of May 2, 1949, P.L. 781, 71 

P.S. 163). 

(a) Parties Required to be Licensed Under the Act. 

1. Real Estate Brokers.

PAGE 500 Judge Flood, in a well considered opinion in Rabinowitz v. Miller, 51 D. & C. 131-1944, ruled that the Real

Estate Brokers License Act governs any transaction involving the sale of real estate without regard to location of the property, but does not apply to a transaction occurring outside Pennsylvania, although the real estate is located here. 

In Schultz v. Palmer Welloct Tool Corp., 207 Fed. 2d 

652—1953 (CCA3rd), Judge Goodrich held that an Ohio real estate broker who was not licensed in Pennsylvania could not recover commissions on the sale of a Pennsylvania manufacturing plant which included real estate valued at $9000 in a transaction involving $335,000. It was held that in making the sale the broker acted as a real estate broker and, having no Pennsylvania license, was barred from recovery under § 16 of the Real Estate 

Brokers License Act, 63 P.S. 446. So also, one who performs broker's services at a time when he holds no broker's license, cannot recover a commission for such services (Ludington v. Shelley, 2 D. & C. 2d 404—1954). 

In Alford v. Raschiatore, 163 Pa. Super. Ct. 6351949, it was held that, although the Act abridges the power to make contracts by one not a licensed real estate broker, it must be liberally construed to effectuate its purpose, and therefore one not a licensed broker could not recover on a promise to pay him the equivalent of a broker's commission if he introduced a buyer. It was also held that the mere introduction of one who subsequently

§ 192(a)2 

bought was negotiation of a sale within the meaning of the Act. § 2 (a) of the Act was amended by the Act of June 

21, 1947, P.L. 767, 63 P.S. 432 (a), by excluding from the definition of Real Estate Broker the phrases "all persons, co-partnerships, associations, and corporations, foreign and domestic, the principal business of whom or which is the purchasing, selling, renting, trading or exchanging of real estate whether for another or as purchaser, seller, or owner of such real estate," and "all persons who negotiate or offer for sale any mortgage or other security for which real estate is the collateral," and by adding to the phrase, "as well as all managers of office buildings, apartment buildings and other buildings and persons employed by banking institutions and trust companies" the words "the owners of such buildings."

PAGE 501 

Custis & Co. v. Pennsylvania Salt Mfg. Co., 351 Pa. 

148-1945, holds that a person or corporation cannot, by the simple expedient of a contract, remove himself or itself from the all embracing terms of the Real Estate 

Brokers License Act. 

2. Real Estate Salesmen. 

The definition of real estate salesman under § 2(b) of the earlier Acts was amended by the Act of January 18, 1952, P.L. 2117, 63 P.S. 432 (pocket part), so as to more specifically "mean and include any person employed by a licensed real estate broker to list for sale, sell or offer for sale, to buy or offer to buy, or to negotiate the purchase or sale or exchange of real estate, or to negotiate a loan on real estate, or to lease or rent, or offer to lease, rent or place for rent, any real estate, or collect or offer, or attempt to collect, rent for the use of real estate, for or in behalf of such real estate broker."

Smukler v. estate broker (§ 7(b) as amended, 63 P.S. 437 (b) (pocket part)).

2. Salesmen's Applications and Qualifications. 

By the 1952 amendment of § 7(c), an applicant for a real estate salesman's license must be a citizen of the United States and must be at least eighteen years of age. 

New. Limited Licenses. 

The amendment of January 18, 1952, P.L. 2109, 63

P.S. 436a, added to the licensing provisions of the Act authority to issue limited brokers' and salesmen's licenses to any person, partnership, association or corporation to engage in the real estate brokerage or sales business exclusively within the limited field or branch of cemetery lots, plots and mausoleum spaces or openings. Applicants for such limited licenses must take an examination in that branch of the business, must have the same general qualifications (as to age, citizenship, etc.) as general real estate brokers and salesmen, and under such a limited license, may not carry on the business of real estate broker or salesman except in the limited field covered by the license. 

The Act of June 21, 1947, P.L. 767, 63 P.S. 437 (b) and (c), empowers the commission to investigate the allegations in any application and, if it finds them untrue, may refuse to examine or license the applicant, setting forth in writing its findings and reasons for refusal and furnishing a copy to the applicant. 

(d) Examinations. 

PAGE 505 These are now, by the Amendment of 1945, P.L. 1023, § 7, 63 P.S. 437, conducted and prepared by the State 

Real Estate Commission. While applications must still be directed to the Department of Public Instruction, the

Act of 1945, § 7, 63 P.S. 437 (c), provides that the commission shall prepare and hold all such examinations. 

The Act of June 21, 1947, P.L. 767, 63 P.S. 437(a), now provides that salesmen's examinations shall be limited to mathematics applicable to real estate business, the Real Estate Brokers License Act and ethical practices thereunder. 

The 1945 amending Act, supra, § 7 (c), 63 P.S. 437 (c), permits the commission to make special dispensations or exceptions because of religious scruples of applicants as to the day of the week upon which examinations are held; and under the amendment of 1952, 63 P.S. 437 (c), real estate salesmen's examinations may be held at Harrisburg on the second Saturday of any month. 

By the Act of 1945, the prohibition in the same section, which made any individual who failed to pass at two successive examinations ineligible to take another for two years, has been changed by reducing the ineligible period to six months. But the former right of such an applicant to receive a temporary permit to operate as a real estate salesman until the next regular examination was deleted in the 1952 amendment.

§ 10(d) of the Act, as amended, 63 P.S. 440 (d), requires that an appeal from the commission's revocation of a license be taken within thirty days. Where a broker, acting as his own attorney, and in no way misled, filed his appeal five days beyond the thirty day period, the court has no power to allow the appeal nunc pro tunc, and must dismiss it as too late (Comm. v. Williams, 80 

D. & C. 119-1951).

§ 194. When a Broker is Entitled to Commissions.

(a) The Contract of Employment. 

PAGE 515 

Pratt Appeal, 158 Pa. Super. Ct. 189, 191–1945, using the language of this section of original text. 

1. Employment by Fiduciaries. 

PAGE 516 The rule laid down in Clark v. Provident Trust Co., 329 Pa. 421-1938, cited on page 516 of the original text, to the effect that a broker's commissions were not considered earned in case the fiduciary received and accepted a higher offer before settlement, was changed by the Act of July 2, 1941, P.L. 227, as amended, 20 P.S. 2254. This

Act provides that a fiduciary who has entered into an agreement of sale of real estate and who thereafter receives a higher offer prior to settlement, is not relieved from the payment of broker's commissions to the broker who negotiated such agreement of sale. However, the 

Act restricts payment to commissions aggregating not more than 5% and provides for the sharing of those commissions by all the brokers who produced purchasers with whom the fiduciary actually entered into agreements of sale (Coyle Estate, 156 Pa. Super. Ct. 185–1944; 

Neely v. Pennsylvania Co., 152 Pa. Super. Ct. 70—1943).

But this Act was held to have no application where a broker was not specially employed by the fiduciary to procure a purchaser, and no agreement was actually executed by the fiduciary with a purchaser produced by the broker (Pratt Appeal, 158 Pa. Super. Ct. 189–1945, affirming on the opinion of the court below, In re Fern wood Cemetery Lotholders Fund, 53 D. & C. 436-1945).

§ 194(b)1 

Even if a purchaser is produced by a claiming broker ready, able and willing to contract at a price acceptable to the fiduciary, if no agreement of sale is actually executed with that purchaser because of a higher offer made by another party with whom a contract is made, the first broker is not entitled to share in the commissions because he has not brought himself within the terms of the 

Act (Betge Estate, 160 Pa. Super. Ct. 233-1947; Pratt 

Appeal, supra).

Nor is a broker entitled to a share of the commissions where he did not represent the original purchaser with whom an agreement had been made, but merely another who had made an increased offer which the fiduciary did not accept because the original purchaser made a new agreement to buy at an increased price. Merely representing an unsuccessful bidder whose competition forced up the price is not enough (Re Straight Mortgage Pool, 

(b) Nature and Extent of Services Necessary. 

1. Ordinary Contract of Employment. 

PAGE 518 This subsection of original text quoted in full and followed by Judge Soffel in Arnheim, Inc. v. Sunray Laundry Inc., 71 D. & C. 86-1950. See also Stevenson v. 

Nichols, 362 Pa. 25-1949.

A broker who is employed to and does produce a purchaser ready, willing and able, is entitled to his commission where the owner is unable to accept the purchaser because of a lease of the property, made by the owner after employment of the broker and without notifying the broker until he produced the purchaser (Rubin v. 

Kosove, 171 Pa. Super. Ct. 439-1952). And where the owner refuses to accept the purchaser produced, without assigning any reason for the refusal and raising no question as to the purchaser's financial ability, the broker is entitled to his commission without being required to

§ 194(b)259 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

prove his customer's financial responsibility (Kline v. 

Lapida, 171 Pa. Super. Ct. 516-1952. See also Wilkinsburg Real Estate & Trust Co. v. Lewis, 173 Pa. Super.

Ct. 372-1953, to the same effect as to commissions on rental).

While merely producing, without specific employment by the owner, an unsuccessful bidder, whose offer results in a higher price from the ultimate purchaser, is not sufficient to entitle the broker to a commission (Re 

Straight Mortgage Pool, 45 D. & C. 695—1942; and see 

Coyle Estate, 156 Pa. Super. Ct. 185, 188-1944), where the broker has been employed by the owner and produces a ready, willing and able purchaser whom the owner is unable to accept because of purchase by a lessee on the same terms, under an option in the lease, the broker is entitled to his commission even though he was informed of the option when first employed (Real Estate Co. of 

Pittsburgh v. Cavazza, 174 Pa. Super. Ct. 19—1953).

PAGE 519 

New. Vendor's Right to Interpleader as Protection Against Rival Brokers' Claims. 

Whether a vendor can invoke interpleader proceedings to protect himself against claims of two brokers for commission, each of whom claims to have been the efficient cause of the sale, is a question on which the nisi prius judges have disagreed. In Brooke v. Smith, 2 D.R. 767 —1893 (C.P. 4, Phila. County), where two brokers sued separately for commissions, Judge Arnold concluded there was but one sale and only one commission earned, but two claimants for it, and that this made a proper case for interpleader. 

More recently, in Clyde v. First National Bank of 

Chester, 54 D. & C. 514–1945 (C.P. Del. Co.) Judge

Sweney granted a petition requiring two brokers to interplead when both claimed to have been the efficient cause of the sale to the purchaser. And this, notwith-

§ 194(b)1 

standing one of them also claimed to have a further claim, based on production to the vendee of a different prospect ready, able and willing to contract, but without averring he had been employed to procure a purchaser. 

On the other hand, in Leach v. Brothers, 44 D. & C. 438 

-1941 (C.P. Lawrence County) President Judge Braham ruled that a vendor was not entitled to an interpleader where two brokers claimed under separate contracts of employment and one actually secured a purchaser willing to pay an amount acceptable, but before concluding the sale the vendor interfered by employing a second broker to complete the sale, and who actually consummated the sale. This circumstance, it was held, may have made the vendor liable to both brokers.

This case was followed in Fronefield v. Pennsylvania 

Co., 32 Del. Co. 283-1943 (C.P. Del. Co.) where much the same situation prevailed and Judge McDade refused an interpleader.

An interpleader was also refused in Lannon v. Church of the Holy Apostles, 55 D. & C. 94-1945 (C.P. 7, Phila. 

Co.) where Judge Crumlish held that in disputes between two or more real estate brokers who claim from a vendor commissions for effecting the same sale, if the vendor is subjected to liability to two or more brokers, such liability is imposed as a result of a separate, independent contract between the vendor and each broker, and does not constitute double or multiple liability on the same contract as required by Pa. R. C. P. 2302 and 2303. See also 

Rand v. George Realty Co., 83 D. & C. 276-1952 (C.P. 

Lawrence Co., Braham, P. J.) to the same effect on a petition by one broker for leave to intervene in a suit for commission by another broker.

These cases are not easily reconcilable. Much must depend on the facts as alleged in the petitions and answers. If it appears that a vendor might possibly become liable for two commissions on the same sale because of the nature of his separate contracts with each broker and his acts pursuant thereto, there cannot be an

§ 194(b)261 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

interpleader. Obviously, an owner of real estate may so handle his arrangements with two or more brokers as to render himself independently liable to each (Prager v. 

Winn, 170 Pa. Super. Ct. 224-1952); and this same reasoning was applied by Judge Wissler in refusing in terpleader in Bard v. Karlip, 57 D. & C. 685—1946 (C. P. 

Lanc. Co.). But if the claim arises out of one transaction, and each broker contends he was the efficient cause of the sale, since each claim would be mutually exclusive of the other (Follansbee Estate, 161 Pa. Super. Ct. 311947), then an interpleader would be proper. In any event, as the vendor is apparently the only party entitled to appeal, a grant of an interpleader would not be reviewable (Schlessinger v. H. B. Smith Co., 300 Pa. 154 -1930). PAGE 519 

Rental Commissions. 

Where a lease for a five year term provides that the broker is to be paid a commission of five per cent "for his services in negotiating this lease and collection of rent," and in practice the broker deducted 5% of each rental instalment as paid, the agreement was interpreted to mean that the broker was entitled to the commission only on rental payments collected, and was not entitled to 5% of the rent due for the balance of the term if the tenant moves out; and this is so even though the broker secured another tenant for the balance of the term whom the owners refused to accept (Langman v. Vitullo, 70 D. & C. 604-1950). 

2. Special Contracts for Commissions. 

PAGE 520 A provision in a contract providing for payment of the broker's commission "as, if and when settlement hereunder shall be completed only," does not deprive a broker of his commissions where failure to make settle-

§ 194(b)3 

ment was in no way the fault of the purchaser but resulted solely from the default of the seller (Rottner v. 

Mencoff, 71 D. & C. 484-1950).

Where the agreement of sale provided that the brokers' commissions "shall be paid at the date of settlement," the language was held to be ambiguous, so that it could not be determined, as a matter of law, that settlement was a condition precedent to the brokers' right to recover, and the meaning of the provision could be resolved only by consideration of the relevant facts by the triers of fact (Hall v. Sheraton Hotel Co., 372 Pa. 563-1953). 

3. Exclusive Agency Contracts. 

PAGE 522 

Where a broker claimed commissions under a contract reading, "I hereby list exclusively with you the following property for the sum of $20,000 and in the event of a sale to pay a commission of 5% on the purchase price This agency shall extend for a non-withdrawal period of 12 months" etc., the word "list" was held equivalent, in real estate parlance, to "I engage or employ you as agent for sale of," and in connection with the words "in the event of a sale I agree to pay you a commission, meant sale by the agent and not a sale by anyone. So, where the owner himself sold the property during the term of the contract, it was held that the broker was not entitled to commission, but might recover compensatory damages for the withdrawal of the property prior to the expiration of the term of employment (Quille v. Wible, 64 D. & C. 427-1948). For what may be included in such damages, see S. V. Thompson Co. v. Goldman, 41 Pa. 

Super. Ct. 209, 217-218-1909. 

Where a broker, holding an exclusive agency contract, took other employment and requested the vendor to communicate with another broker in whose hands the first broker placed the property, and the second broker

§ 195(b) 263 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

sold the property and was paid the commission, the first broker was denied recovery under his exclusive agency contract (Close v. Keefe, 51 D. & C. 576-1944). 

There is a distinction between an exclusive agency to sell and an exclusive right to sell. The former merely prohibits appointment of another agent to sell, but does not prevent a sale by the principal himself, without liability to the original agent for his commission. An exclusive right to sell, however, preserves to the agent his commission, no matter who makes the sale (Dain v. 

Loeffler, 256 Pa. 319—1917; Byrne v. Bushkoff, 172 Pa. 

Super. Ct. 101-1955; Quille v. Wible, 64 D. & C. 4271948). So, under an exclusive right to sell, a broker is entitled to his commission where the owner conveys to his mortgagee (to prevent foreclosure) in consideration of $1000 and release from liability on an $18,000 bond and mortgage (Whiteman & Co. v. Fidei, 176 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 142-1954). 

§ 195. When a Broker Is Not Entitled to His Commission. 

PAGES 524-525 (b) When the Employment is to Sell Property for a 

Fiduciary.

The rule stated in the original text that a broker who produces a ready, willing and able buyer for property of a fiduciary loses his right to a commission if the fiduciary sells to another for a higher price, has been changed by the Act of July 2, 1941, P.L. 227, as amended, 20 P.S. 

2254. Under this Act as amended, the fiduciary, although still bound to sell at the higher price in some instances (see pages 58-60 of this supplement), is not relieved from the payment of commission to the first broker, who, however, must share the single commission, for which the fiduciary is liable, with other brokers entitled (see pages 

257-8 of this supplement).

§ 195(d)  

(d) Secretly Representing Both Buyer and Seller.

PAGE 526 For additional case of waiver of agent's disloyalty, see Fenner v. No. 90 Building & Loan Ass'n, 146 Pa.

Super. Ct. 596-1941. 

§ 199. When a Broker Is Personally Liable. 

PAGES 531-532 

A broker signing an agreement of sale for an undisclosed principal becomes personally liable, even though the other party to the contract had knowledge that the broker was acting only as agent (Schelly v. Gribbin, 161 

Pa. Super. Ct. 20—1947).

But where an agreement of sale under seal describes the purchaser as a named person "or his nominee," the seller's right of action is limited to the named person or such other person as the named person may nominate, and no action may be maintained on the sealed instrument against an allegedly undisclosed principal, the use of the term "or his nominee" being no indication that the purchaser was acting as an agent (Toll v. Pioneer Sample 

Book Co., 70 D. & C. 48-1949, affirmed 373 Pa. 1271953).

While generally a principal, either disclosed or undisclosed, is liable on a contract made by his duly authorized agent, yet if the contract is under seal and is not executed by the principal, the seal prevents recovery against a principal who is not disclosed by the contract itself 

(Toll v. Pioneer Sample Book Co., 373 Pa. 127-1953). 

See this case for a statement of exceptions to the rule thus laid down.

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

CHAPTER XIV. 

SETTLEMENTS. TITLE INSURANCE COMPANIES. 

§ 200. Title Insurance Companies. 

PAGE 534 

On the origin and development of title insurance in Pennsylvania, see interesting and informative article en titled "Pennsylvania Land Titles and the Development of Title Insurance" by William C. Neil, Title Officer of Land Title (now Commonwealth Land Title) Insurance 

Company, published in The Shingle (Philadelphia Bar 

Association publication), November, 1955, page 173.

PAGE 535 New. The preparation of deeds, mortgages and other documents for persons to or for whom applications for title insurance had been issued or were in contemplation to be issued, as incidental to the business of insuring titles, has been held not to be the practice of law (La- 

Brum v. Commonwealth Title Co. of Phila., 358 Pa. 239 -1948). 

§ 201. Application for Title Insurance. Rates. 

PAGE 538 The rates stated in the original text have been changed by title insurance companies in the Philadelphia area. 

They are now inclusive of examination and settlement fees, and begin at a minimum of $60 for a single property in Philadelphia or Bucks County ($50 in Chester, Delaware or Montgomery County), for either a conveyance or a mortgage, where the face amount of the title insurance is $2,000 or less. The charge increases at the rate of five dollars per $1,000 (or fractional part thereof) of insurance through $15,000 of insurance; then at $4.50 per $1,000 (or fractional part thereof) above $15,000

§ 205 

to and including $30,000 of insurance, with decreasing rates as the insurance amount increases, until the rate reaches $1.25 per $1,000 (or fractional part thereof) on insurance in excess of $2,000,000. 

§ 205. Settlements (Conveyance). 

PAGE 550 The acknowledgment fee is charged against the grantee and is payable by him because a deed signed and delivered is effectual to pass title whether recorded or not (Reichert Estate, 56 D. & C. 1-1946, aff. 356 Pa. 269 -1947; Keller v. Donkin, 31 D. & C. 636-1938). Since, therefore, recording a deed is distinctly for the grantee's benefit, to give notice to the world of his title and to protect him against possible subsequent grantees, mortgagees etc., it is proper that he should pay the acknowledgment fee for putting the deed in a recordable condition, as well as the recorder of deed charges. Unless the agreement of sale provides otherwise, the tender of an unacknowledged deed is a good tender.

PAGE 552 

Normally, a title company will not make distribution where payments are deposited by ordinary check until the checks are collected. If, therefore, immediate distribution is desired the vendee should be notified to pay in cash, for even a certified check is not the equivalent of cash under all circumstances (Gehringer v. Real Estate- 

Land Title and Trust Co., 321 Pa. 401-1936), although in ordinary practice, where the deposit is made by certified check, the title company will make distribution, by its own checks, immediately. 

New. Interest on Purchase Money.

The general rule is that if the purchaser under an agreement of sale has entered into possession of the property, he must pay interest on the purchase money not yet paid to the seller, even though the latter failed to deliver

§ 267 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

the deed at the time required (McCormick v. Crall, 6 

Watts 207-1837). But where the vendee in possession is without fault, and the vendor has been guilty of wilful and vexatious delay or gross laches in carrying out the terms of the agreement, and meanwhile the purchase money has been appropriated (as, for example, deposited with and held by a title insurance company), the vendor cannot have interest on the purchase money, especially where the vendee has incurred liability for interest to a prospective mortgagee (Kreider v. Brubaker, 371 Pa. 279-1952).

New. Zoning Use Registration Permit. 

If the property being conveyed is in Philadelphia and consists of either a building used, designed or intended to be used by three or more families, or a commercial establishment containing one or more dwelling units, the seller must deliver to the purchaser at settlement a use registration permit (or certified copy thereof), issued by the "appropriate city officer," indicating the zoning classification of the property and the use to which it may be legally put (Act of July 27, 1955 (No. 104), 21 P.S. 

611-615, effective September 1, 1955). Severe penalties may be imposed upon the seller for failure to obtain and deliver the permit (see page 50 of this supplement).

The procedure for obtaining a use registration permit is to apply at the Zoning License Bureau. The Bureau then investigates the property, and if no violation of the zoning ordinances is found, the permit is issued. If the 

Bureau finds a zoning violation, application must be made for a nonconforming use permit or for an exception or variance. In the normal case where no zoning violation exists, the process requires from one to two weeks, and the cost of the use registration permit varies from $2.00 to $10.00 or more.

PAGE 558 

New. Suggestion (18). If the property to be conveyed is in Philadelphia and is a multiple dwelling for three or more families or a combination store or other commercial property and dwelling, be sure that the zoning use registration permit is delivered to the seller (see pages 50, 267 of this supplement). 

§ 207-A. The Title Insurance Policy. Nature of Reme dies and Liabilities Thereunder.

(a) Nature of a Title Insurance Policy. 

PAGE 559 New. It has been ruled by the Department of Justice of 

Pennsylvania that title insurance companies have no power under the Title Insurance Law of May 17, 1921, 

P.L. 682, as amended by Act of July 1, 1937, P.L. 2540, 40 P.S. 895, 896, nor under the Banking Code of May 15, 1933, P.L. 624, 7 P.S. 819-1102, to issue completion bonds 

(In re Completion Bonds, 42 D. & C. 537-1941). 

(b) Nature of Liability Under the Policy. 

2. Negligence in Conduct of Settlement. 

When a title company distributes the purchase price under the mistaken belief that a lien upon the property had been discharged, and has been forced to make good, out of its own funds, the damage to the purchaser, it is not entitled to impress a trust upon the money distributed to the seller and follow it into the hands of the seller's administrator, but may claim as a general credi-

§ 207-A(b)2 269 

1956 SUPPLEMENT. 

tor only. This because a title company is the agent of the purchaser and has no separate or independent contract relation with the seller, since it pays out none of its own funds, but merely distributes the funds placed in its hands by the purchaser, and its right to recover from the seller sums it was forced to pay under its policy or insurance contract arises from subrogation only (Kamel's Estate, 42 D. & C. 130-1941).
